<meta>This is the title page of a 1524 edition of two foundational works by Augustine of Hippo, printed in Nuremberg. It marks the beginning of the volume.</meta>

## DIVINE <term>AURELIUS AUGUSTINE</term> <note>Aurelius Augustine (354–430 AD), Bishop of Hippo, was one of the most influential theologians in Western Christianity. The title "Divine" (Latin: *Divi*) was a common honorific for saints and church fathers in the Renaissance.</note>

# ON NAT-
# URE AND
# GRACE

### ONE BOOK.

->*Also one book On the Spirit and the Letter.* <note>The Latin title is *De Spiritu et Littera*. This work explores the relationship between the "letter" of the law and the "spirit" of God's grace.</note><-

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration of a right hand emerging from a cloud, grasping the hilt of a vertical sword. Large flames rise from the entire length of the blade toward the top of the frame.</image-desc>

->Nuremberg, In the Year 1524.<-

<summary>The title page for a Renaissance printing of Saint Augustine's theological treatises "On Nature and Grace" and "On the Spirit and the Letter." These works address the necessity of divine grace over human nature and the legalism of religious law.</summary>
<keywords>Augustine of Hippo, Nature and Grace, Spirit and the Letter, Christian theology, 16th-century printing, Nuremberg, Reformation-era publishing</keywords>

--- Page 6 ---
<meta>This page follows the title page of the 1524 Nuremberg edition of Augustine’s treatises. It features a personal address from the printer, Johann Petreius, to the reader, setting a spiritual and exhortatory tone for the volume.</meta>

### Johann Petreius to the Christian reader.

Grace and peace in Christ. While the <term>Divine Aurelius Augustine</term> <note>original: "D. Aurelius Augustinus". Augustine (354–430 AD) was the Bishop of Hippo and a foundational theologian for Western Christianity.</note> is great in his other works, he shows how great he truly is—or rather, Christ dwelling within him shows—especially in these two: the one *On Nature and Grace* and the other *On the Spirit and the Letter*. We have printed these separately for your benefit, Christian reader, so that you may perceive from this how Augustine handled the Gospel writings—or rather, how they handled him. Certainly, while we spend our days debating about Scripture but meanwhile live so comfortably and luxuriously, Scripture has been for many, until now, an abominable idol.

->Come now, brothers, let us shake off<-
->that yoke of <term>Pharaoh</term> <note>Petreius uses Pharaoh as a metaphor for the spiritual bondage of sin or legalism that enslaves the human heart.</note> who is in<-
->our hearts; let us flee by the<-
->strong hand of the Father who<-
->draws us. Let us walk through<-
->the middle of the sea, through<-
->the <term>mortification</term> <note>original: "mortificationẽ". This refers to the theological concept of "putting to death" the sinful nature or worldly desires.</note><-
->of ourselves—specifically in Christ—<-
->so that we may receive<-
->the Lord’s bright command,<-
->enlightening the eyes. Let<-
->us, through the Spirit who makes<-
->the law, prepare a tabernacle for<-
->the Lord, so that the name of our<-
->God alone may be sanctified within us,<-
->until at last we obtain that land which<-
->God promised to our fathers, a land<-
->flowing with milk and honey, the<-
->region of the living. Farewell.<-

<summary>A preface by the printer Johann Petreius introducing two of Augustine's works. He exhorts the reader to move beyond intellectual debate and comfort, using the Exodus as a metaphor for a spiritual journey toward God's grace.</summary>
<keywords>Johann Petreius, Augustine, Preface, Grace, Mortification, Exodus metaphor, Nuremberg printing, Reformation theology</keywords>

<meta>This page provides the table of contents for the first of the two treatises mentioned in the printer’s preface. It moves from Johann Petreius’s personal exhortation to the formal structure of Augustine’s work "On Nature and Grace," which was written around 415 AD to counter the teachings of Pelagius.</meta>

<sig>a 2</sig>

# TABLE OF CONTENTS
## OF THE CHAPTERS OF THE DIVINE AURELIUS
### Augustine’s book on nature and grace
### against the Pelagians. <note>The Pelagians followed Pelagius, a monk who taught that human nature was not fundamentally corrupted by original sin and that humans could achieve salvation through their own free will and effort, rather than solely by God's grace.</note>

On the error of those who wish to excuse their sins by blaming nature, as if they were not committed voluntarily, and what the justice of God truly is. Chapter 1.

That no one is sufficient in himself to achieve perfect <term>righteousness</term> <note>original: "iustitiā". In this context, it refers to a state of being right or "justified" before God.</note>, nor can anyone be justified except through faith and the sacrament of the blood of Christ. Chapter 2.

That God indeed established nature well, but that nature has been darkened by vice contracted from <term>original sin</term> <note>original: "originali peccato". This is the foundational Christian doctrine—strongly championed by Augustine—that all humans inherit a stained nature due to the fall of Adam.</note>. Chapter 3.

That the grace of Christ is not granted based on merits, but is given freely, though not to everyone. Chapter 4.

That those who are liberated by grace from the punishment of eternal damnation are called <term>vessels of mercy</term> <note>original: "uasa misericordiæ". A biblical metaphor from the Book of Romans, referring to those chosen by God to receive His kindness.</note>. Chapter 5.

That it is the claim of worldly wisdom that infants do not need a physician and that adults are sufficient for their own righteousness. Chapter 6.

That the author of the submitted book <note>The "submitted book" refers to a work by Pelagius or one of his followers that was sent to Augustine for his review and response.</note> wrote with zeal, yet taught heretical doctrine—that a man can live without sin—by using a deceptive distinction between "the ability to be" and "actually being." Chapter 7.

An infant overtaken by death, who neither lived as a Christian nor had the opportunity to become one, is not absolved from damnation. <note>This reflects Augustine’s rigorous view on the necessity of baptism for the remission of original sin, even in infants.</note> Chapter 8.

That not only infants, but neither young men nor the elderly to whom the Gospel of Christ has not come, can be justified without the grace of Christ. Chapter 9.

<summary>This page lists the initial chapters of Augustine’s polemic against Pelagianism, outlining his core arguments on the necessity of divine grace, the corruption of human nature through original sin, and the insufficiency of human will for salvation.</summary>

--- Page 8 ---
<meta>This page continues the table of contents (Elenchus) for Augustine’s "On Nature and Grace." There is significant ink bleed-through from the reverse side of the leaf, though the text remains legible. These chapters focus on the necessity of divine help for human weakness.</meta>

-># TABLE OF CONTENTS<-

An objection from Pelagius—presented as if it came from someone else—regarding whether a person who has not been taught the faith can be righteous, and whether they are righteous through God's grace. Chapter 10. <note>Pelagius often used the literary device of quoting an anonymous "other" to voice controversial opinions, a tactic Augustine found suspicious.</note>

The irrelevant examples he brings forward in his argument, which make him suspect to Augustine as the reader proceeds through the text. Chapter 11.

That for those things committed wrongly, one should seek refuge in the grace of the merciful Lord. Chapter 12.

That we must plead with God so that negligence in avoiding sin does not gain mastery over us. Chapter 13.

That, except for Christ alone, we never read of any human being existing without sin. Chapter 14.

How the words of Saint James are to be understood: *“But no human being can tame the tongue.”* <note>original: "Linguā autem nullus hominum domare potest". A reference to the New Testament, James 3:8.</note> Chapter 15.

What true wisdom is that tames the tongue, and how this is not within human power but must be requested from God, as the Apostle advises. <note>The "Apostle" here refers to James, who instructs readers to ask God for wisdom.</note> Chapter 16.

What it means to be "outside the flesh" and "in the flesh," and how wisdom must be sought from God so that destructive ignorance may be removed. Chapter 17.

That we must pray not only for past sins to be <term>expiated</term> <note>original: "expientur". To be cleansed or made right through a ritual or divine act of forgiveness.</note>, but also for future sins to be avoided. Chapter 18.

That sin has both weakened and wounded human nature, proven here by both logic and the authority of Scripture. Chapter 19.

That just as abstaining from food weakens the body, so too the soul withers when it does not "feed" upon the substance that is God. Chapter 20.

That Jesus is the Savior of all people, as much of Adam himself as of... <note>The text cuts off mid-sentence at the bottom of the page.</note>

<summary>This page lists chapters 10 through 21 of Augustine's "On Nature and Grace," focusing on the limitations of human willpower and the necessity of seeking God's help to overcome sin and ignorance.</summary>
<keywords>Pelagius, Saint James, original sin, divine grace, human nature, wisdom, expiation, Adam</keywords>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page continues the table of contents for Augustine’s "On Nature and Grace." The numbering jumps from the previous page's Chapter 17 to Chapter 21, suggesting some chapters were listed on the intervening (unprovided) leaf or column. These chapters delve into the mechanics of "free will" and the psychological root of sin, specifically pride.</meta>

-># OF THE CHAPTERS<-
a 3

...of infants and all his posterity, since they are weak. Chapter 21. <note>original: "uulorũ" likely an OCR error for "parvulorum". Augustine argued that since the Fall of Adam, even infants are born with a weakened nature.</note>

How manifold is the punishment of sin, and the grave retribution of sins through sins. Chapter 22. <note>Augustine often argued that one consequence of sinning is the loss of moral clarity, which leads inevitably to further sins—a "punishment" that is itself a sin.</note>

How God deserts those worthy of being deserted, and how **free will** <term>liberum arbitrium</term> <note>The power of the mind to choose between different courses of action. Augustine argues here that while we can choose to do evil on our own, we cannot choose to do good without divine help.</note> is sufficient for sinning, but to return to righteousness it has need of a **justifying physician**. Chapter 23. <note>original: "iustificatore medico". Augustine frequently uses medical metaphors, portraying God as a doctor who must heal the "disease" of the human soul before it can act rightly.</note>

That free will requires the price of Christ's passion, and how the wonderful mercy of God makes even evils work for good. Chapter 24.

That we ought to acknowledge our misery, and thus seek and implore mercy. Chapter 25.

That God heals not only to erase what we have sinned, but also to provide that we do not sin; proven by examples taken from a wounded person and the eye. Chapter 26. <note>Just as a doctor heals a wound so it stops hurting and also strengthens the body to prevent further injury, or heals an eye to allow sight.</note>

That it is truly said a human cannot be without sin, so that the occasion for **pride** <term>superbia</term> and vainglory—which tends to mingle itself even with good deeds—may be removed. Chapter 27.

That often some things seem to sound "sharp" or clever, which when examined are found to be "blunt" or weak. Chapter 28. <note>original: "acutule... obtusa". A rhetorical critique of the "sharp" arguments used by his opponents, the Pelagians.</note>

That although sins are committed through pride, not everything done wrongly is done proudly; and how the beginning of all sin is pride. Chapter 29.

That the **guilt** <term>reatus</term> <note>The legal or moral liability for an action.</note> of sin belongs to the one sinning, descending from health not used rightly. Chapter 30.

How deep is the counsel of God, who does not quickly heal pride—for which pious souls tearfully pray that it be crushed—as it entangles itself even in good works. Chapter 31.

<summary>This page lists chapters 21 through 31 of the treatise, focusing on the relationship between human weakness, the necessity of God as a "physician" to heal the will, and the persistent danger of pride.</summary>
<keywords>free will, pride, original sin, divine mercy, Christ's passion, justification, spiritual healing</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the table of contents (Elenchus) for Augustine’s treatise "On Nature and Grace." It follows the previous discussion of God as a "justifying physician" and moves into the necessity of humility and the universality of sin, with a significant historical exception made for the Virgin Mary. # TABLE OF CONTENTS
<note>original: "ELENCHVS"</note>

That the mercy of God precedes us by healing, and follows us by glorifying. Chapter 32. <note>Augustine refers to "prevenient grace" (mercy that comes before our efforts to heal us) and "subsequent grace" (mercy that follows to bring us to heaven).</note>

That with fear and trembling, and not with presumption, we ought to work out our salvation. Chapter 33.

That though a man may be claimed to be without sins, he is not for that reason to be compared to God; and that humility is rooted in truth; and how God not only forgives sins already committed, but also helps us to avoid future ones. Chapter 34.

Why the examples of certain people whom we read have sinned were written down, and how we might be able to die without sin, even though we cannot live without it. Chapter 35. <note>Augustine argues that while humans inevitably sin during life, through God’s final grace, they can reach a state of forgiveness at the moment of death.</note>

That except for the most blessed Virgin—concerning whom, when sin is discussed, I wish no question to be raised—it is necessary that all others confess themselves to be sinners. Chapter 36. <note>This is a famous passage where Augustine excludes Mary from the discussion of personal sin out of honor for the Lord, a key text in the development of Mariology.</note>

That not all those of whom Scripture records good things, or does not mention having sinned, were actually without sins. Chapter 37.

That those things were written for which a standard had to be applied, and that the reader was to be instructed in many things by a few examples; and that those are called and are "just" who nevertheless are not without sin. Chapter 38. <term>just</term> <note>In this context, being "just" or "righteous" does not mean being perfect, but rather being in a right relationship with God through faith and repentance.</note>

That we ought not to believe everything we read, and that it is not a sin to assert something we have not read. Chapter 39.

That through the recognition of the name of Christ, we not only learn how to live, but are also helped by the grace by which we [live] well... <note>The text cuts off mid-sentence at the catchword "ui," likely for "uiuimus" (we live).</note>

<summary>This page lists the chapters of Augustine's work focusing on the necessity of humility, the universality of human sinfulness (excepting the Virgin Mary), and the role of grace in both forgiving past sins and preventing future ones.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the table of contents for Augustine’s "On Nature and Grace." The previous page concluded with Chapter 37, discussing how scriptural silence on the sins of certain figures does not mean they were sinless. This page resumes near the end of the entry for Chapter 40 and continues through the theological distinctions between human capacity and divine aid.</meta>

# OF THE CHAPTERS

...as we have shown. <margin>Chapter 40.</margin>

That the authority of the Apostle—*Through one man sin entered the world,* etc., *in whom all have sinned* <note>original: "Per unum hominem peccatum... in quo omnes peccauerunt"; referring to Romans 5:12, a cornerstone of the doctrine of Original Sin.</note>—is to be understood as applying to everyone, both those of ancient times and those of more recent times. <margin>Chapter 41.</margin>

That a human being is able to possess the most perfect righteousness, but only through the grace of God and in no other way; otherwise, the help of the Savior is made void if a "healthy nature" is defended as sufficient. <margin>Chapter 42.</margin> <note>Augustine argues against the Pelagian view that human nature is essentially "healthy" and needs no external rescue.</note>

That God does not command impossible things, but by commanding, He advises us both to do what you are able to do and to ask for what you are not able to do. <margin>Chapter 43.</margin> <note>This is one of Augustine's most famous maxims: God gives the grace to fulfill His own commands.</note>

That even if there have been or are men in this life without sin, they nevertheless could not have been so, nor could they be so now, unless they were justified by the grace of God. <margin>Chapter 44.</margin>

The difference between *actually doing* something and *being able to do* something, based on the sayings of Pelagius. <margin>Chapter 45.</margin> <note>Pelagius was Augustine's primary opponent; he argued that humans have the "natural capacity" to be sinless.</note>

He rejects the saying of Pelagius, that whatever is constrained by natural necessity is deprived of free choice. <margin>Chapter 46.</margin>

Concerning the possibility and power of seeing, hearing, and smelling. <margin>Chapter 47.</margin> <note>Augustine uses these physical senses as analogies for how the "capacity" to act comes from God, even if the "will" to act is ours.</note>

That it is erroneous to say that "not sinning" is independent of grace, while only the "possibility of not sinning" belongs to nature. <margin>Chapter 48.</margin>

Concerning the falsehood of that logical conclusion: "Not sinning is our own doing, therefore we are able both to sin and not to sin." <margin>Chapter 49.</margin>

That the will itself can act against those things which are inseparably implanted in nature, as is proved by the opinion of the Apostle. <margin>Chapter 50.</margin>

That the "possibility of not sinning" is not attributed to the grace of God simply because God happened to be the creator of our nature. <margin>Chapter 51.</margin> <note>Augustine insists that "Grace" refers to a specific, ongoing gift of help, not just the "gift" of being born with a human mind.</note>

# TABLE OF CONTENTS

That the mercy of God precedes us by healing us, and follows after us by glorifying us. Chapter 32. <note>original: "misericordia dei præueniat... subsequatur"; Augustine emphasizes that God's grace acts both at the beginning of faith (prevenient grace) and at the end of life.</note>

That we ought to work out our salvation with fear and trembling, and not with presumption. Chapter 33. <note>A reference to Philippians 2:12, used here to warn against the Pelagian belief in human self-sufficiency.</note>

That although a person may be claimed to be without sins, they are not for that reason to be compared to God; and that humility is to be placed on the side of truth; and how God not only forgives sins already committed, but also helps us to avoid future ones. Chapter 34. <note>Augustine argues that even if someone achieved sinlessness, they would still be a creature and not equal to the Creator.</note>

Why the examples of certain people whom we read to have sinned were recorded in Scripture, and how we might be able to die without sin, even if we cannot live without it. Chapter 35.

That with the exception of the most blessed Virgin—concerning whom I wish to have no discussion at all when the subject is sins—it is necessary that all others confess themselves to be sinners. Chapter 36. <note>This is a significant passage in Catholic theology; while Augustine argues for universal sinfulness, he makes a unique exception for Mary out of "honor for the Lord."</note>

That not everyone who the Scripture records as good, or does not explicitly report as having sinned, lived entirely without sins. Chapter 37.

That those things were written so that a proper measure might be applied, and the reader might be instructed in many things by a few examples; and that those are named "just" who nevertheless are not without sin. Chapter 38. <note>Augustine explains that "righteousness" in the Bible does not always mean absolute perfection.</note>

That we ought not to believe everything we read, and that it is not a sin to assert something that we do not read in the text. Chapter 39.

That through the acknowledgment of the name of Christ, we not only learn how we ought to live, but are also assisted by the grace by which we live well... <note>The text breaks off here at the end of the page.</note>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page continues the table of contents for Augustine's "On Nature and Grace," specifically focusing on the theological debate regarding the necessity of divine grace versus the natural capacity of human beings. It follows the previous page's discussion on the Virgin Mary and the universality of sin.</meta>

-># OF THE CHAPTERS<-
->a 4<-

...we follow. Chapter 40.

That the authority of the Apostle [Paul]: *Through one man sin entered the world* etc., *in whom all have sinned*, is understood to apply to everyone, both the ancients and those of more recent times. <note>original: "Per unum hominem peccatum... in quo omnes peccauerunt"; a reference to Romans 5:12, the foundational text for the doctrine of Original Sin.</note> Chapter 41.

That a person can possess the most perfect righteousness, but only through the grace of God and in no other way; otherwise, the help of the Savior is made void when a "healthy nature" is defended. <note>Augustine argues against Pelagius’s claim that human nature remained "healthy" or uncorrupted enough after the Fall to achieve holiness without special divine help.</note> Chapter 42.

That God does not command impossible things, but by commanding, He warns us both to do what we can and to ask for what we cannot do. <note>original: "facere quod possis, et petere quod non possis"; this famous Augustinian principle suggests that the Law exists to show us our limitations so we will seek God's help.</note> Chapter 43.

That even if there have been or are people in this life who exist without sin, they nevertheless could not have been and cannot be so unless they are justified by the grace of God. Chapter 44.

What the difference is between the act of *performing* something and the *power* to perform it, according to the sayings of Pelagius. <note>Pelagius separated the "capacity" (given by God) from the "will" and "act" (which he claimed were entirely up to the human); Augustine argues grace is needed for all three.</note> Chapter 45.

[The author] rejects the saying of Pelagius, that whatever is constrained by natural necessity is deprived of free choice. Chapter 46.

Concerning the possibility and power of seeing, hearing, and smelling. <note>Augustine uses these physical senses as metaphors; just as eyes need light to see, the human will needs grace to perform good works.</note> Chapter 47.

That it is a mistake to say that "not sinning" is outside the scope of grace, while the "ability not to sin" belongs to our nature. Chapter 48.

Concerning the falsehood of that logical conclusion: "Not sinning is our own doing, therefore we are able to sin and not to sin." Chapter 49.

That the will itself can act against those things which are inseparably implanted in nature, as proved by the opinion of the Apostle. <note>A reference to the Apostle Paul's descriptions of the internal struggle between the "flesh" and the "spirit."</note> Chapter 50.

That the possibility of not sinning is not attributed to the grace of God simply because God happened to be the founder of our nature. <note>Augustine is clarifying that "Grace" refers to a specific, ongoing gift of help, not just the "natural gift" of being created with a will.</note> Chapter 51.

<vocab>Pelagius, grace of God, sin, righteousness, nature, free choice, the apostle, possibility</vocab>

<summary>This page contains a table of contents detailing Augustine's arguments against Pelagianism, specifically addressing how divine grace is necessary for righteousness and how the "capacity" for good is distinct from the "act" of doing good. It emphasizes that human nature alone is insufficient for salvation without the ongoing assistance of God.</summary>
<keywords>Original Sin, Romans 5:12, Pelagius, Divine Grace, Free Choice, Righteousness, Human Nature, Capacity vs. Act</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the table of contents for Augustine's "On Nature and Grace." It moves from the definition of righteousness to the practical state of the Christian after baptism, specifically addressing the persistence of "concupiscence" (disordered desire) and the ongoing need for divine assistance.</meta>

-># TABLE OF CONTENTS<-

That although those who are baptized do not lose their human nature—within which corruption remains—that nature still requires a <term>remedy</term>. <note>The Latin "medicamine" (medicine) reflects Augustine's view of grace as a healing agent for a "sick" nature. original: "medicamine opus habet."</note> Chapter 52.

That for the baptized, the "flesh"—meaning the carnal vices—is an adversary, and how a wounded nature requires a true confession rather than a false defense. Chapter 53.

Whether and how there is opposition within our very substance; and how the word "flesh" should be understood not just as our physical substance (which is good and not opposed to the spirit), but also as our carnal vices. <note>Augustine is careful to distinguish between the physical body, which he considers a "good" creation of God, and "the flesh" as a metaphor for the impulse toward sin.</note> Chapter 54.

That baptism is sought both for the forgiveness of past sins and to gain health for the future; for the "law of sin" exists within our bodies, making it necessary for us to have the grace of Christ. Chapter 55.

Whether those who are baptized perform the good they desire without any resistance from the <term>concupiscence</term> of the flesh. <note>Concupiscence refers to a disordered desire or "lean" toward worldly things rather than God, which Augustine argues remains even after the guilt of sin is washed away in baptism.</note> Chapter 56.

That anyone who refrains from sinful acts only out of fear of punishment, rather than a love for righteousness, has not yet truly abandoned the will to sin. Chapter 57.

That just as we give thanks for the parts of us already healed, so we must pray for those yet to be healed, and how we must resist the devil's opposition. <note>The OCR reads "profanandis" (to be profaned), but in the context of Augustine's work, this is almost certainly a printer's error for "sanandis" (to be healed).</note> Chapter 58.

That the <term>possibility</term> of not sinning depends entirely upon the grace of God. <note>Pelagius used the word "possibilitas" to describe a natural human capacity; Augustine reclaims the word here to say that this "capacity" only functions through divine help. original: "possibilitas ad non peccandum."</note> Chapter 59.

Whether there has ever been, is, or could be anyone in this world living so righteously that they have no [sin] at all... <note>This chapter heading is cut off at the end of the page.</note>

<summary>This page lists chapter headings exploring the post-baptismal state of believers, arguing that even after the forgiveness of sins, humans still struggle with carnal desires and require ongoing divine grace as a "medicine."</summary>
<keywords>Baptism, Grace of Christ, Concupiscence, Righteousness, Sin, Devil, Possibility, Remedy, Human Nature</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the Table of Contents for Augustine's "On Nature and Grace," specifically listing chapters 60 through 70. It completes the list of authorities (church fathers) that Augustine cites to prove his points against Pelagius, and concludes the index.</meta>

## TABLE OF CONTENTS

*...sin* <p style="text-align: right;">*Chapter 60.*</p>
*That man is able to overcome sin, because sinning is not a matter of necessity, but of one’s own will.* <p style="text-align: right;">*Chapter 61.*</p>
*An explanation of the words of Hilary which Pelagius cites in his own writings in his favor.* <note>Hilary of Poitiers was a 4th-century bishop and theologian. Pelagius—the target of this book—had tried to use Hilary's writings to prove that humans could be sinless by their own power.</note> <p style="text-align: right;">*Chapter 62.*</p>
*What Ambrose thinks regarding the power of not sinning and how the human will is prepared by God.* <note>Ambrose was the Bishop of Milan and Augustine's mentor; Augustine cites him here to show that even a "free" will needs God's help to prepare it for good.</note> <p style="text-align: right;">*Chapter 63.*</p>
*What John, Bishop of Constantinople, and a certain Sixtus thought about <term>free choice</term> and grace.* <note>John of Constantinople is better known as John Chrysostom, famous for his preaching. Sixtus refers to the future Pope Sixtus III, who initially seemed sympathetic to Pelagius before being corrected by Augustine.</note> <p style="text-align: right;">*Chapter 64.*</p>
*What the venerable priest Jerome wrote concerning free choice.* <note>Jerome was a contemporary of Augustine and the translator of the Latin Vulgate Bible; he was a fierce opponent of Pelagius.</note> <p style="text-align: right;">*Chapter 65.*</p>
*What kind of necessity exists in men from which we must ask to be delivered.* <p style="text-align: right;">*Chapter 66.*</p>
*What Augustine thought: namely <gloss>that</gloss> sin is guarded against in two ways—just like a bodily disease—so that it does not occur, and if it has occurred, so that it may be quickly healed; also concerning ignorance and difficulty.* <note>Augustine argues that the Fall left humanity with "ignorance" (not knowing the good) and "difficulty" (not being able to do the good), both of which require divine medicine.</note> <p style="text-align: right;">*Chapter 67.*</p>
*How Christian men are to be instructed.* <p style="text-align: right;">*Chapter 68.*</p>
*How easily <term>charity</term> makes the commandments of God.* <note>In Augustine's theology, "charity" (caritas) is a gift of the Holy Spirit that makes a person love the good, turning a "hard" command into a joyful act. original: "charitas"</note> <p style="text-align: right;">*Chapter 69.*</p>
*That the ways of righteousness are harsh to fear, but gentle to love.* <p style="text-align: right;">*Chapter 70.*</p>

<meta>This page marks the transition from the list of chapters to the formal introduction of the work "On Nature and Grace." It features an excerpt from Augustine's "Retractions," a late-life project where he reviewed and corrected his entire body of work. The bleed-through text mentioned in the transcription likely refers to the title of the next section on the reverse side.</meta>

# THE HOLY AURELIUS AUGUSTINE
## *Bishop of Hippo, on the book concerning nature*
## *and grace against the Pelagians, from the second*
## *book of his Retractions,*
### THE ARGUMENT.

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'V' featuring a bust of a figure with long flowing hair, possibly a saint, set within a floral and scrollwork frame.</image-desc>

<margin>Romans 4</margin> <note>A reference to the biblical book of Romans, chapter 4, which discusses how the "ungodly" are justified through faith rather than works—a central theme of this text.</note>
At that time, there also came into my hands a certain book by Pelagius <note>Pelagius (c. 354–418 AD) was a monk who taught that humans could achieve perfection through their own will without the need for divine grace; Augustine became his primary theological opponent.</note>, in which he defended human nature against the grace of God (by which the <term>ungodly is justified</term> <note>original: "iustificatur impius." This is the theological concept of being made righteous in the eyes of God.</note>, and by which we are Christians) with as much argumentation as he could muster. I therefore named the book in which I responded to him—defending grace, not as an opponent of nature, but as the means through which nature is liberated and governed—*On Nature and Grace*. In that work, I defended certain words which Pelagius attributed to Sixtus <note>Sixtus II, a 3rd-century Pope and martyr.</note>, the Roman bishop and martyr, treating them as if they were truly the words of that Sixtus. For I had believed they were his, but I later read that they belonged to Sixtus the philosopher <note>Augustine later realized he had mistaken the "Sentences of Sextus" (a collection of Pythagorean ethical sayings) for the writings of a Christian Pope.</note>, not the Christian.

->THE END<-

<summary>This page serves as a formal introduction to Augustine's "On Nature and Grace," providing his own retrospective context on why he wrote the book to refute the teachings of Pelagius. Augustine also admits to a scholarly error where he misattributed philosophical quotes to a Christian martyr.</summary>
<keywords>Augustine of Hippo, Pelagius, Pelagianism, Grace vs. Nature, Justification, Retractions, Sixtus the Philosopher, Church History</keywords>

The book which you sent, my dearest sons Timasius and James, I have read through—having set aside for a short time the works I had in hand—with great care, though indeed in haste. I saw therein a man <note>Augustine is referring to the monk Pelagius, whose book Timasius and James had sent to him for review.</note> inflamed with a most burning zeal against those who, when they should accuse the human will for their sins, instead accuse human nature, and by that means attempt to excuse themselves. He has flared up excessively against this plague, which even the authors of secular literature <note>original: "literarum secularium." This refers to non-Christian classical Roman authors.</note> have strongly rebuked, exclaiming: *The human race complains of its nature without cause.* <note>Augustine is quoting the Roman historian Sallust (86–34 BC), specifically the opening of his work "The War with Jugurtha."</note> This sentiment he has indeed bolstered with as much intellectual force as he could. Nevertheless, I fear that he may instead be supporting those who "have a zeal for God, but not according to knowledge." <margin>Romans 10</margin> <note>A reference to St. Paul's Letter to the Romans 10:2, which Augustine uses to suggest that Pelagius has passion for God but lacks correct theological understanding.</note> For being ignorant of the <term>justice of God</term> <note>original: "iustitia." In Augustine's theology, this refers to the righteousness that God gives to humans through grace, rather than a person's own moral achievements.</note>—

### THAT GOD INDEED established nature well, but the vice darkening nature was contracted from original sin. Chapter 3.

<margin>Ecclesiastes 7</margin>
*For human nature was originally blameless and created without any vice. But this nature of man by which each person is born from Adam now requires a doctor, because it is no longer healthy. Indeed, all the good things it possesses in its formation—life, the senses, the mind—it has from the supreme God, its creator and craftsman* <note>original: "artifice." Augustine portrays God as a master artisan who creates a perfect "product" (humanity), which then becomes damaged through misuse.</note>. *But the vice, which darkens and weakens these natural goods so that it has need of illumination and healing, was not contracted from the blameless craftsman, but from original sin, which was committed by free will* <note>original: "libero arbitrio." The ability to choose, which Augustine argues was the mechanism by which humanity fell.</note>. *And through this, a punishable nature belongs to a most just vengeance.*
<margin>
2 Corinthians 5
Ephesians 2
</margin>
*For even if we are now a new creature in Christ, we were yet by nature children of wrath, just like the others. ### THAT THE GRACE OF CHRIST is not returned based on merits, but is given freely, yet not to everyone. Chapter 4.

*Therefore, this grace of Christ, without which neither infants nor those of great age can be saved, is not [given] for merits*

<meta>catchword: merits</meta>

<summary>Augustine argues that while human nature was created perfect by God, it has been darkened by original sin and now requires divine healing. He emphasizes that salvation is a gift of grace rather than a reward for human merit.</summary>

--- Page 25 ---
<meta>This page continues from the previous page's discussion on the nature of grace, specifically completing the sentence that grace is not a reward for merit but a free gift from God.</meta>

<header>ON NATURE AND GRACE.</header>

<margin>Romans 11</margin>
<margin>Romans 3</margin>
...is not returned <note>original: "redditur." Augustine is playing with the idea of a debt being repaid; he argues grace isn't something God owes us as payment for our work, but a gift.</note>, but is given freely, which is why it is called grace. "Being justified," he says, "freely through his blood." Therefore, those who are not liberated through grace—whether because they could not yet hear the word, or because they refused to obey it, or even because they were at an age where they could not hear and did not receive the bath of rebirth <note>original: "lauacrum regenerationis." This refers to the sacrament of Baptism, which Augustine viewed as the essential "bath" that washes away original sin.</note> which they might have received to be saved—they are certainly condemned by a just judgment. This is because they are not without sin, whether it is the sin they contracted from their origin <note>original: "originaliter traxerunt." This is the concept of Original Sin, the idea that all humans inherit a fallen state from Adam.</note> or the sin they added through their own evil habits. For all have sinned, whether in Adam or in themselves, and they fall short of the glory of God.

## THAT THOSE LIBERATED BY GRACE
## from the punishment of eternal damnation are
## called vessels of mercy. Chapter 5.

<margin>Romans 9</margin>
<margin>1 Timothy 1</margin>
<margin>Romans 8</margin>
The entire mass <note>original: "uniuersa massa." Augustine famously uses the term "massa peccati" or "massa damnata" (a lump of sin) to describe humanity as a single entity that, through Adam, has become a "mass" deserving of judgment.</note> of humanity, therefore, owes a debt of punishment. If the deserved punishment of damnation were rendered to everyone, it would undoubtedly be rendered without injustice. Therefore, those who are liberated from it by grace are not called "vessels of their own merits," but "vessels of mercy." Whose mercy is it, if not His who sent Christ Jesus into this world to save sinners? These are the ones He foreknew, and predestined, and called, and justified, and glorified. Who, then, could be so madly insane as not to give ineffable thanks to the mercy of Him who liberates those He willed, when no one could rightly find fault with the justice of Him who condemns absolutely everyone?

## THAT WORLDLY WISDOM
## says that infants do not [need] a doctor...

<summary>Augustine argues that humanity is a "mass" of sin deserving of punishment, and that salvation is a free gift of grace rather than a reward for merit. He specifically addresses the fate of the unbaptized and the "vessels of mercy" chosen through divine predestination.</summary>
<keywords>grace, original sin, baptism, vessels of mercy, predestination, justification, Adam, Romans</keywords>

<header>THE BOOK OF ST. AURELIUS AUGUSTINE</header>
<meta>catchword: unknowing</meta>

...to be in need, and that adults can be sufficient for themselves to achieve righteousness.

### Chapter VI

If we are wise according to the Scripture, we are not forced to argue against Christian grace, nor to say those things by which we might attempt to prove that human nature neither needs a physician in the case of infants—because it is healthy—nor that in adults it can be sufficient for itself for righteousness, if it so wills. 
<margin>1 Corinthians 1</margin>
Indeed, these things seem to be said acutely; but they are said in the "wisdom of words" by which the cross of Christ is made void <note>original: "euacuatur crux Christi." Augustine is quoting St. Paul. His point is central to his theology: if human nature were enough to save us, then Christ's death on the cross would have been unnecessary and thus "emptied" of its purpose.</note>. 
<margin>James 3</margin>
This is not the wisdom descending from above. I do not wish to say what follows <note>Augustine is likely alluding to the rest of the verse in James 3:15, which describes such wisdom as "earthly, unspiritual, demonic."</note>, lest I be thought to do an injury to our friends, whose very strong and quick minds we wish to see running not in a perverse direction, but in a straight path.

->THAT THE AUTHOR OF THE SENT<-
->book wrote zealously, but taught heretically,<-
->that a human being can live without sin, using<-
->a fallacious distinction between "being able to be"<-
->and "actually being."<-

### Chapter VII

Therefore, with as much zeal as the author of this book you sent is inflamed against those who seek a defense for their sins by blaming the weakness of human nature, with that much and much more burning zeal must we be inflamed, lest the cross of Christ be made void. For it is made void if it is said that, in some way, one can reach righteousness and eternal life apart from His sacrament <note>original: "sacramentum." In this context, Augustine refers broadly to the mystery of Christ’s redemptive work and the specific rites, like Baptism, that apply it to the believer.</note>. I do not want to say that what is done in this book is done by one who knows <note>original: "ab sciente." Augustine is being strategically charitable here toward Pelagius (the author he is refuting), suggesting he might be teaching error out of ignorance or misunderstanding rather than a deliberate attempt to destroy the faith.</note>, lest I judge the one who wrote it as not even to be considered a Christian; but what I rather believe [is done] by...

<summary>Augustine argues that claiming human nature is self-sufficient for righteousness "voids" the importance of Christ's sacrifice. He addresses a book by Pelagius, suggesting its author is zealously misguided rather than intentionally anti-Christian.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Chapter 7 of Augustine’s "On Nature and Grace." On the previous page, Augustine began to critique a book sent to him (written by Pelagius), noting the author's zeal but warning that his arguments might be heading in a "perverse direction."</meta>

# ON NATURE AND GRACE.
<b>b</b>

...unknowingly, indeed with great strength; but I wish that strength to be healthy, not like the kind that those suffering from a frenzy <note>original: "phrenetici." Augustine uses a medical metaphor here, suggesting that while his opponent is intellectually energetic, it is the frantic, unhealthy energy of someone in a feverish delirium.</note> are accustomed to have. For he first distinguishes that it is one thing to ask whether something *can* be—which pertains only to its **possibility**—and another thing to ask whether it actually **is**. No one doubts that this distinction is true; for it follows that whatever *is* must have been *able* to be, but it does not follow that whatever *can* be actually *is*. For because the Lord raised Lazarus 
<margin>John 11.</margin>
from the dead, He undoubtedly could do so; but because He did not raise Judas, should it be said that He could not? He could, therefore, but He willed not to. For if He had willed it, He would have done this also by the same power, because "the Son also gives life to whom He will." 
<margin>John 5</margin>
But notice where he is going with this true and manifest distinction, and what he is trying to achieve. "We," he says, "are treating only of **possibility**; unless something certain is established about it, we consider it a very serious matter and out of order to move on to anything else." He turns this over in many ways and in a long discourse <note>original: "sermone diuturno"</note>, lest anyone think he is inquiring about anything other than the **possibility of not sinning**. Hence, among the many things by which he argues this, he also says: "I repeat the same thing again. I say it is possible for a human being to be without sin; what do you say—that it is not possible for a human being to be without sin? I do not say," he says, "that a human being *is* without sin, nor do you say that there is *no* human being without sin. We are contending about **being able** and **not being able**, not about **being** and **not being**." Then he mentions that several of those things which are usually brought forward against them from the Scriptures do not pertain to this question (in which the inquiry is whether a human being *can* or *cannot* be without sin). For he says, "No one is clean from filth," 
<margin>John 15.</margin>
and...

<summary>Augustine analyzes Pelagius's logical distinction between the "possibility" of living without sin and the "actuality" of doing so. Pelagius argues that while no one may be perfect, human nature retains the inherent capacity for perfection, a point Augustine begins to challenge.</summary>
<keywords>possibility vs actuality, sinlessness, Pelagius, Lazarus, divine power, human nature, grace, John 5, John 11</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Chapter 7 of Augustine’s "On Nature and Grace." On the previous page, Augustine was quoting Pelagius, who argued that while humans might not actually be perfect, they still possess the inherent "possibility" of being perfect.</meta>

# THE BOOK OF SAINT AURELIUS AUGUSTINE

<margin>Job 25:3, 1 Kings 8 <note>Original: "3.Reg.8." In older Latin Bibles, 1 Kings was titled 3 Kings.</note>, Ecclesiastes 7, Psalm 13.</margin>
"...'there is no man who does not sin,' and 'there is no just man on earth,' and 'there is none who does good,' and other things similar to these," he [Pelagius] says, "pertain to **not being** and not to **not being able**." By examples of this kind, it is shown what sort of men certain individuals were at some time, not that they could not have been something else; hence they are rightly found to be blameworthy. For if they were such because they could not be anything else, they lack fault. See what he has said!

## AN INFANT OVERTAKEN BY DEATH, neither existing as a Christian nor being able to be one, is not absolved from damnation. Chapter VIII.

<margin>John 3, Romans 5</margin>
But I say that an infant born in a place where he could not be helped through Christ's baptism, overtaken by death, was such—that is, he departed without the **laver of regeneration** <term>laver of regeneration: a traditional term for the water of baptism, believed to wash away original sin and "rebirth" the soul</term>—precisely because he could not be anything else. Let him [Pelagius] therefore absolve the child, and open the kingdom of heaven to him against the sentence of the Lord! But the Apostle does not absolve him, who says: *Through one man sin entered into the world, and death through sin, and so it passed into all men, in whom all have sinned.* <note>Augustine is quoting Romans 5:12, the foundational text for the doctrine of Original Sin.</note> Rightly, therefore, by that damnation which runs through the entire **mass** <note>original: "massam." Augustine famously used the term "massa damnata" (condemned mass) to describe all of humanity as a single lump of clay tainted by Adam's sin.</note>, he is not admitted into the kingdom of heaven, even though he was not only not a Christian, but could not even have been one.

## THAT NOT ONLY INFANTS, BUT neither young men nor the elderly to whom the Gospel of Christ has not come, [can be justified] without Christ's grace—

<summary>Augustine argues against the Pelagian idea that sin requires a conscious choice; he uses the fate of unbaptized infants to prove that all humanity is born into a state of condemnation (original sin) regardless of their individual "possibility" to do good.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the argument from Chapter 8, where Augustine challenged Pelagius’s view on the salvation of infants and the nature of sin. Augustine is now addressing Pelagius’s claim that sin is merely the "imitation" of Adam rather than an inherited condition.</meta>

<header>OF NATURE AND GRACE.</header>

...that they might be justified. ->centered text<- Cap. IX.

<margin>Romans 5.</margin>
<margin>Genesis 4.</margin>
<margin>Hebrews 11.</margin>
<margin>Galatians 2.</margin>
But it is not for condemnation, they <note>Pelagius and his followers</note> say, because it was said that all have sinned in Adam, not because of a sin drawn from the origin of one's birth, but because of **imitation** <note>Pelagius argued that humans are born neutral and only sin by following Adam's bad example, rather than inheriting a "fallen" nature.</note>. If, therefore, Adam is called the author of all the sins that followed because he was the first sinner among men, why is Abel not placed as the head of all the righteous instead of Christ, since he was the first righteous man among men? But I am not speaking of an infant; suppose a young man or an old man has died in a region where he could not hear the name of Christ. Could he become righteous through nature and **free will** <term>free will: *liberum arbitrium*, the capacity to choose between good and evil</term>, or could he not? If they say he could: behold what it is to make the cross of Christ void—to contend that someone can be justified through the natural law and the choice of the will without it [the cross]. Let us say here too, "therefore Christ died in vain," <note>Augustine is quoting Galatians 2:21, a key verse in his argument that if human nature alone could save us, Christ’s sacrifice was unnecessary.</note> for all would be able to do this, even if He had not died; and if they were unjust, it would be because they *wanted* to be, not because they *could not* be righteous. If, however, he could not be justified at all without the grace of Christ, let him [Pelagius] dare to absolve even this man according to his own words; because he was such as he was for the reason that he could not be anything else, he lacked fault.

### AN OBJECTION OF PELAGII AS IF IT WERE
### from another, whether one not instructed in the faith may be righteous, and
### whether he is righteous through the grace of God. Cap. X.

But he raises an objection to himself as if it were said by another, and says: "It can indeed be so," you say, "but through the grace of God." Then, as if responding, he adds: "I give thanks to your kindness, because you are not content merely to stop attacking my assertion which you were attacking a moment ago, or merely to confess it..."

<summary>Augustine refutes the Pelagian idea that sin is merely the imitation of Adam, arguing that if humans could be righteous through nature alone, Christ's death would be meaningless. <meta>This page continues Augustine’s critique of Pelagius's book *On Nature*. Augustine is analyzing Pelagius’s rhetorical strategy, where Pelagius argues that by defining *how* a person might live without sin, he is implicitly acknowledging the necessity of grace, even if he focuses primarily on human "possibility."</meta>

# BOOK OF SAINT AURELIUS AUGUSTINE

but even more, you do not shrink from proving it. For to say, "It is indeed possible, but through this or that <note>Pelagius is arguing that the "how" (the means) is less important than the "fact" (the possibility) of being sinless.</note>," what else is this but not only to agree that it can be, but also to show in what manner or how it can be done? Therefore, no one proves the possibility of a thing more than one who also admits its **quality** <term>quality: *qualitas*, referring here to the specific mode or condition by which an action is performed</term>, because a quality cannot exist without the thing itself. Having said these things, he again objects to himself: "But you will say," he says, "at this point you seem to deny the **grace of God** <term>grace: *gratia*, the unmerited assistance of God required for salvation and good works</term>, since you do not mention it." Then he responds: "Do I deny it, I who, by confessing the thing itself, must necessarily confess that by which the thing can be accomplished? Or is it you who, by denying the thing, undoubtedly deny whatever it is through which the thing is accomplished?"

He has already forgotten that he is responding to someone who does *not* deny the thing. He had just a moment ago proposed the objection of one saying: "It can indeed be, but through the grace of God." How, then, does that person deny the **possibility**—for which this author <note>Pelagius</note> labors so much—when he actually says: "It can be, but through the grace of God"? Nevertheless, because he has dismissed the one who already confesses the thing, he still argues against those who deny that it is possible for a man to be without sin. What is that to us? Let him argue against whomever he wishes, provided that he confesses this, which is denied by most wicked impiety: that without the grace of God, a man cannot be without sin. He says therefore: "Whether through grace, or through help, or through mercy, or whatever that thing is through which a man can be without sin, whoever admits the thing itself admits its cause."

<meta>catchword: WHICH ARE IRRELEVANT FROM [QVAM IMPERTINENTIA EX]</meta>

<summary>Augustine catches Pelagius in a rhetorical contradiction, arguing that Pelagius cannot claim to support the necessity of grace while simultaneously arguing that the "possibility" of sinlessness is an inherent human faculty. Augustine insists that any admission of human perfection must explicitly depend on God's grace.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Augustine’s analysis of Pelagius’s treatise. On the previous page, Augustine noted that Pelagius seemed to admit the necessity of grace, but here he begins to suspect Pelagius's true meaning based on the analogies Pelagius uses to describe how "possibility" works.</meta>

->ON NATURE AND GRACE.<-
<sig>b 3</sig>

...examples he brought forward in his discussion, which make him suspect to Augustine as he continues to read. Chapter XI.

I confess to your Charity <note>original: *dilectioni vestrae*, a common respectful form of address to a Christian audience</note> that when I read these things, I was suddenly filled with joy that he did not deny the **grace of God** <term>grace: *gratia*, the divine assistance given to humans for their salvation</term>, through which alone a person can be **justified** <term>justified: *iustificari*, the process by which a sinner is made righteous in the sight of God</term>. For this is the thing I most detest and shudder at in the discussions of such men. But as I proceeded to read the rest, I first began to have suspicions based on the comparisons he provided. For he says: "Now, if I were to say: a human being can debate, a bird can fly, a hare can run—and if I did not also mention the means by which these actions are performed, namely the tongue, the wings, and the feet—have I denied the qualities of the functions <note>original: *officiorum qualitates*</note> when I have acknowledged the functions themselves?" It certainly seems that he has mentioned things that are effective by **nature** <term>nature: *natura*, the inherent constitution of a being as created</term>; for these limbs—the tongue, the wings, and the feet—were created as part of the nature of such beings. He did not propose anything like what we want to be understood as **grace**, without which a person is not justified. In this context, we are dealing with the *healing* of natures, not the *founding* of natures <note>Augustine is making a crucial distinction: Pelagius talks about how God built us with "tools" like reason (the "tongue"), but Augustine argues we are "sick" from sin and need grace as medicine, not just as a biological tool.</note>. From this point on, therefore, I began to read the rest with concern, and I found that I had not been mistaken in my suspicion.

### THAT FOR THOSE THINGS WHICH ARE
### WRONGLY DONE, ONE SHOULD FLEE TO THE
### GRACE OF THE MERCIFUL LORD. Chapter XII.

Before I come to that, see what he said when he was treating the question of the difference between sins, and was raising an objection against himself: that some people say certain slight sins, by their very multitude because they so often break in, cannot all [be avoided]...

<vocab>Natura: Nature, Gratia: Grace, Augustinus: Augustine, iustificatio: justification, peccatum: sin, officium: function/duty</vocab>

<summary>Augustine expresses his initial relief that Pelagius mentions grace, but quickly turns to suspicion when Pelagius uses biological analogies—like a bird's wings—to describe it. Augustine argues that grace is a supernatural healing for a fallen nature, not just an inherent natural faculty.</summary>

--- Page 32 ---
<meta>This page continues Augustine’s critique of Pelagius’s work "On Nature." Augustine is arguing that Pelagius relies too much on human willpower and "nature" while ignoring the necessity of God’s active help (grace) to overcome sin.</meta>

<header>BOOK BY DOCTOR AURELIUS AUGUSTINE</header>

*...to be avoided, he denied that even light sins ought to be rebuked with correction, if they cannot be avoided at all. He certainly did not pay attention to the Scriptures of the New Testament, where we say that this is the purpose of the Law in its rebuking: so that on account of those things which are done wrongly, one may take refuge in the **grace** of the merciful Lord—just as if the Law were a **tutor** <note>original: *paedagogus*, a household servant who escorted children to school; Augustine is referencing Galatians 3:24 where the Law is described as a "schoolmaster" leading us to Christ</note> leading us to that same **faith** which was later revealed. In this way, the things done wrongly are forgiven, and through the help of that same grace, they are no longer done. For the road belongs to those who are making progress, since those making good progress are called "perfect travelers" <note>Augustine often uses the term "viator" (traveler) to describe the Christian’s status on earth—not yet at the destination, but moving toward it</note>. But that is the highest **perfection**, to which nothing can be added, when the destination toward which one tends begins to be possessed.*

## THAT GOD MUST BE ENTREATED, 
## lest negligence in avoiding sin should 
## rule over us. Chapter XIII.

Now, as for the question that is asked of them: *Are you yourself without sin?*—this truly does not pertain to the matter currently under discussion. But as for his statement that it is to be attributed to one's own negligence rather than a lack of ability that one is not without sin, he says this well enough; but let him also deign to pray to God that this wicked negligence might not rule over him. 
<margin>Psalm 118</margin>
A certain man <note>referring to the Psalmist</note> was asking for this very thing when he said: *Direct my paths according to your word, and let no iniquity rule over me* <note>original: *Psalm 118:133* (in modern Bibles, Psalm 119:133)</note>. He prayed this lest, while he trusts in his own diligence as if in his own strength, he fails to arrive either at true **righteousness** <term>righteousness: *iustitia*, the moral state of being right or "justified" before God</term> here in this life, or at that place where perfect righteousness is undoubtedly to be desired and hoped for in the next.

## THAT EXCEPT FOR CHRIST ALONE, 
## it is nowhere read concerning a person without sin. Chapter XIIII.

<summary>Augustine argues that the Law's purpose is to drive people toward God's grace and explains that while humans may strive for perfection as "travelers," ultimate sinlessness is only achieved through divine help. He warns against Pelagius's idea that sin is merely a matter of personal negligence, insisting instead on the need for prayer and reliance on God.</summary>

<header>ON NATURE AND GRACE.</header>

AND regarding that which is said to them by certain people—that it is nowhere written in these exact words that a man can be without sin—he [Pelagius] easily refutes this, because the question is not in what specific words a thought is expressed. <margin>Wisdom 18</margin> However, perhaps it is not without cause that, while in the scriptures men are sometimes described as being "without complaint" <note>original: *sine querela*, often translated as "blameless"</note>, no one is found who is called "without sin," <margin>2 Corinthians 5</margin> except for that one alone of whom it was clearly said: *Him who knew no sin.* <margin>Hebrews 4</margin> And in that place where the subject was the priests: *For he has experienced all things, according to the likeness [of us], yet without sin*—meaning, in that flesh which had the <margin>Romans 8</margin> **likeness of the flesh of sin**, though it was not itself the flesh of sin. <note>Augustine is arguing that while Jesus was truly human, he did not possess the "corrupted" nature that Augustine believes all other humans inherit from Adam</note> Yet he would not have had that likeness unless all other flesh were the flesh of sin. Now, regarding how this passage should be understood: <margin>1 John 3</margin> *Everyone who is born of God does not sin, and cannot sin, because his seed remains in him*, [we must consider this] when the Apostle John himself—as if he himself were not born of God, or were speaking to those not yet born of God—clearly stated: <margin>1 John 1</margin> *If we say we have no sin, we deceive ourselves, and the truth is not in us.* In the books which I wrote on this subject to Marcellinus <note>This refers to Augustine's earlier work, *De peccatorum meritis et remissione* (On the Merits and Remission of Sins)</note>, I took care to explain this as best I could. And regarding the saying, *he cannot sin*, his [Pelagius's] assertion does not seem to me to be rejected when he says it is meant as if one said, "he ought not to sin." For who is so insane as to say that one *ought* to sin, since it is a sin precisely because it *ought not* to be done?

### HOW THE WORDS
### *of Saint James are to be understood: "But the tongue no man*
### *can tame."* Chapter XV.

<summary>Augustine distinguishes between being "blameless" in human eyes and being truly "without sin," a state he reserves for Christ alone. <meta>This page continues Augustine’s rebuttal of Pelagius in his work *On Nature and Grace*. Having previously discussed the nature of sin and Christ’s unique sinlessness, Augustine now addresses the specific problem of human speech and the necessity of God’s help (grace) to control it, using the Epistle of James as his primary source.</meta>

<header>THE BOOK OF SAINT AURELIUS AUGUSTINE</header>

<margin>James 3</margin>
Indeed, regarding what the apostle James says: *But no human being can tame the tongue*, <note>original: "Linguam autem nullus hominum domare potest"</note> it does not seem to me that this should be understood as if he intended to express it as a reproach. It is not as if it were being said, "Can no human being then tame the tongue?" in a rebuking way, as if to say: "You are able to tame wild beasts, yet you cannot tame the tongue," as if it were actually easier to tame the tongue than wild beasts. I do not think that this is the meaning in this passage. For if he wanted it to be understood that the tongue is easy to tame, the rest of the passage comparing it to animals would follow differently. But in fact, it follows: *It is a restless evil, full of deadly poison*, <note>James 3:8</note> which is certainly more harmful than the poison of beasts and serpents. For the latter kills the flesh, but the former kills the soul. 
<margin>Wisdom 1</margin>
For *the mouth that lies kills the soul.* <note>Wisdom 1:11</note> Therefore, Saint James did not pronounce that sentence as if taming the tongue were easier than the taming of beasts, nor did he wish it to be spoken in that tone. Rather, he was showing how great an evil the tongue is in a human, so that it can be tamed by no human—even though even wild beasts are tamed by humans. Nor did he say this so that we might allow the dominion of this evil over us to continue through our own negligence; rather, he said it so that we might ask for the help of divine grace to tame the tongue. For he did not say, "no one can tame the tongue," but *no human being*, so that when it is tamed, we may confess that it is done by the mercy of God, by the help of God, and by the grace of God. Let the soul therefore strive to tame the tongue, and while it strives, let it ask for help; and let the tongue pray that the tongue may be tamed by the taming of Him who said to His own: 
<margin>Matthew 10</margin>
*For it is not you who speak, but the Spirit of your Father who speaks in you.* <note>Matthew 10:20</note> And so...

<summary>Augustine interprets the Epistle of James to argue that taming the tongue is impossible for human nature alone, serving as a proof that divine grace is required for moral life. He contrasts the physical death caused by animal venom with the spiritual death caused by the "poison" of sinful speech.</summary>
<keywords>Augustine, James 3, Divine Grace, Taming the Tongue, Sin, Holy Spirit, Pelagian controversy, Mercy of God</keywords>

### WHAT TRUE WISDOM IS
->Taming the tongue, and how it is not in<-
->man’s power, but must be asked from God, as the<-
->apostle warns. Chapter XVI<-

<margin>James 3</margin>
Accordingly, after he had magnified the evil of the tongue by saying these things, he immediately advised—once he had finished saying "My brothers, these things ought not to be so" <note>James 3:10</note>—by what help those things which he said ought not to happen might be prevented. He asks: "Who is wise and disciplined among you? Let him show by his good conduct his work in the gentleness of wisdom. But if you have bitter jealousy and contentions in your hearts, do not boast and be liars against the truth. This is not the wisdom descending from above, but is earthly, natural, and demonic. For where there is jealousy and contention, there is instability and every wicked work. But the wisdom that is from above is first of all pure, then peaceable, modest, easy to be persuaded, full of mercy and good fruits, beyond price, and without hypocrisy." <note>James 3:13–17</note> This is the wisdom that tames the tongue; it is the wisdom descending from above, not springing forth from human nature. Does anyone dare to take even this away from the grace of God and, with the most arrogant vanity, place it within human power? Why then is it prayed for so that it might be received, if it is within man’s own power to possess it? Or is this prayer also spoken against, lest an injury be done to free will <term>free will: *liberum arbitrium*, the human capacity to choose, which Augustine's opponents, the Pelagians, argued was sufficient for virtue without special divine aid</term>, which [they claim] is sufficient for itself by the possibility of nature to...

<summary>Augustine uses the Epistle of James to argue that "Wisdom"—the force required to control human speech—is a gift from God rather than a natural human faculty. He mocks the idea that praying for such wisdom would "injure" the concept of free will.</summary>

### WHAT IT MEANS TO BE OUTSIDE THE FLESH
->and in the flesh, and how wisdom must be<-
->asked from God so that deadly igno-<-
->rance may be cleared away. Chapter XVII.<-

<margin>Romans 8</margin> Nor should anyone object to these people <note>The Pelagians, who argued that humans could avoid sin by their own natural will</note> regarding the impossibility of not sinning,
based on what was said: "The wisdom
of the flesh is an enemy to God, for it is not subject to the law
of God, nor indeed can it be. And those who are in the flesh cannot
please God." <note>Romans 8:7–8</note> Indeed, he spoke of the "wisdom of the flesh,"
not the wisdom descending from above; and it is manifest that "being in the flesh"
does not refer to those who have not yet departed
from the body, but those who live according to the flesh.
But that is not the question being debated here.
The thing I am waiting to hear from him <note>Referring to Pelagius</note>, if I can, is this:
whether those who live according to the spirit—and because of this, even while still living, are
already in a certain way not "in the flesh"—live according to the spirit by the
grace of God, or whether the <term>possibility</term> <note>original: "possibilitas"; a technical term in this debate referring to the natural capacity to do good</note> given to them when they were created is now sufficient for them.

<summary>Augustine argues that taming the tongue and achieving righteousness requires divine wisdom obtained through faith rather than human effort alone. He clarifies that "living in the flesh" refers to a sinful lifestyle rather than physical existence, questioning whether the "possibility" of doing good is a natural gift or an ongoing act of grace.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Augustine’s critique of Pelagius (referred to as "this man" or "he"). Having previously discussed the inability of humans to "tame the tongue" without divine help, Augustine now argues that nature and will alone cannot fulfill the law without the grace of charity (love) and addresses Pelagius's views on sins of ignorance.</meta>

<header>ON NATURE AND GRACE.</header>

<margin>Romans 13. Romans 5. Psalm 118.</margin>
...by the strength of nature and his own will, since the fullness of the law <note>The "law" here refers to the moral requirements of God, which Augustine argues cannot be met by rules alone.</note> is nothing except charity <note>original: "charitas"; here meaning the divine love of God rather than mere almsgiving.</note>, and the charity of God is poured into our hearts, not by ourselves, but by the Holy Spirit who has been given to us? This man also discusses sins of ignorance, and says that a person ought to be watchful so that they are not ignorant. He argues that ignorance should be blamed because a person, through their own negligence, does not know what they should have known had they applied diligence. Yet he disputes this rather than praying and saying:
> Give me understanding, that I may learn your commandments. <note>Psalm 119:73 (118:73 in the Vulgate numbering used here).</note>

For it is one thing not to have cared to know (which are sins of negligence that even seemed to be expiated through certain sacrifices of the Law). It is quite another to wish to understand but be unable to, and thus to act against the law because of not understanding what it requires. Hence we are admonished to ask God for wisdom, who gives to all abundantly—that is, to all those who ask in such a way, and ask as much as such a great thing ought to be asked for.

### THAT ONE MUST NOT ONLY PRAY
### that past sins may be expiated, but also
### that future ones may be avoided. Chapter XVIII.

<margin>Matthew 6.</margin>
He [Pelagius] confesses, however, that committed sins must be divinely expiated, and the Lord must be entreated for them—namely, to obtain pardon—because the power of nature (which he praises so much) and the human will cannot, as even he admits, make what has been done undone. Therefore, by this necessity, it remains for him to pray for forgiveness. But that he should be helped so as not to sin, he has nowhere said; I have not read it here. It is a wonder that there is total silence on this matter, since the Lord’s Prayer <note>original: "oratio dominica"; the "Our Father".</note> reminds us that both are to be asked for: both that [past debts be forgiven] and that [we be led not into temptation].

<summary>Augustine argues that Pelagius incorrectly relies on human effort to avoid ignorance and fulfill the law, whereas true "charity" requires the Holy Spirit. He further critiques Pelagius for acknowledging the need for God’s forgiveness for past sins while ignoring the need for God’s grace to prevent future ones.</summary>
<keywords>grace, nature, charity, Holy Spirit, ignorance, Pelagius, Lord's Prayer, forgiveness, wisdom</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Augustine’s critique of Pelagius. The previous page ended as Augustine began discussing Pelagius's acknowledgment of the Lord's Prayer. Augustine is now explaining why we pray for both forgiveness and the strength to avoid future sin, countering Pelagius's claim that the human will is sufficient on its own.</meta>

<header>BOOK OF SAINT AURELIUS AUGUSTINE</header>

“forgive us our debts,” and “lead us not into temptation” <note>Referencing the Lord's Prayer from Matthew 6:12-13.</note>; the former is so that past sins may be expiated, the latter so that future ones may be avoided. Although this does not happen unless the will is present, the will alone is not sufficient for it to happen; therefore, for this purpose, a prayer that is neither superfluous nor impudent is offered to the Lord. For what is more foolish than to pray that you may do what you already have in your own power?

### THAT SIN HAS BOTH WEAKENED
### and wounded human nature, which is proved
### by reason and authority. Chapter XIX.

<margin>1 Corinthians 1</margin>
<margin>Psalm 40</margin>
Now see what is most relevant to the matter: how he <note>Pelagius, Augustine's theological opponent.</note> tries to show human nature as if it were entirely without any fault. In doing so, against the clearest scriptures of God, the "wisdom of the word" is used to destroy and make void the cross of Christ <note>original: "sapientia uerbi qua euacuetur crux Christi"; a reference to 1 Corinthians 1:17, where Paul warns against clever rhetoric undermining the gospel.</note>. But clearly that cross will not be made void; rather, this "wisdom" of his will be overturned. For when we have shown this, perhaps God's mercy will be present so that he may even regret having said these things.

"First," he says, "one must debate the point that nature is said to have been weakened and changed through sin. Wherefore before all things I think it must be asked," he says, "what sin is. Is it some substance <note>In ancient philosophy, a "substance" is a thing that exists in itself (like a person or a stone), rather than a quality or an action.</note>, or is it a name entirely lacking substance, by which is expressed not a thing, not an existence, not some body, but an act of doing something wrongly?" Then he adds: "I believe it is so." And if it is so, he says, "how could that which lacks substance weaken or change human nature?"

See, I beg you, how without knowing it he strives to overturn the most healthy words of the divine oracles. I said, "Lord, have mercy on me, heal my soul, for I have sinned against you" <note>Psalm 41:4 (numbered as Psalm 40 in the Latin Vulgate).</note>. What...

<summary>Augustine argues that we pray for divine help because the human will alone cannot overcome sin, and critiques Pelagius's philosophical argument that sin—being an action rather than a physical "substance"—cannot damage human nature.</summary>

...ance <note>Continuing the word "substance" from the previous page.</note>, or a name entirely lacking substance, by which not a thing, nor an existence, nor any body, but only an act of a deed done wrongly is expressed? He answers: "It is as you say. Sin is not some substance, but only the act of a deed done wrongly is expressed by this name." And against this, the other asks: "Why then do you cry out: *Heal my soul, for I have sinned against you*? <note>original: "Sana animam meam, quoniam peccaui tibi"; Psalm 41:4.</note> How could that which lacks substance corrupt your soul?" Is it healed if nothing is wounded, nothing struck, nothing weakened and corrupted? Furthermore, if there is something to be healed, from where was it corrupted? You hear a man confessing; why do you desire a man debating? "Heal," he says, "my soul." Ask him from where that which he asks to be healed was corrupted, and hear what follows: *Because I have sinned against you.* Let this man [Pelagius] question the Psalmist; let him ask what he thinks should be asked, and say: "O you who cry out: *Heal my soul, for I have sinned against you*: what is sin? Is it some substance, or a name entirely lacking substance, by which not a thing, not an existence, not some body, but only the act of a deed done wrongly is expressed?" The Psalmist would answer: "It is as you say. Sin is not some substance; but only the act of a deed done wrongly is expressed by this name." And the opponent would counter: "Why then do you cry out: *Heal my soul, for I have sinned against you?* How could that which lacks substance corrupt your soul?" Would not the Psalmist, consumed by the sorrow of his wound, lest he be turned from prayer by debate, answer briefly and say: "Depart from me, I pray; debate rather with Him if you can, who said:
<margin>Matthew 9.</margin>
*It is not the healthy who need a doctor, but those who are sick; I did not come to call the righteous, but sinners.*" <note>Matthew 9:12-13.</note> Here, did He not clearly call the righteous "healthy" and the sinners "sick"?
