--- Page 40 ---
<meta>This page continues Augustine’s argument against the Pelagian view of sin. Pelagius argued that because sin is not a "substance" (a physical entity), it cannot fundamentally damage human nature. Augustine responds by arguing that just as the absence of food (which is not a substance itself, but a lack) can waste away the body's substance, the "absence" of God caused by sin wastes away the soul.</meta>

# THE BOOK OF ST. AURELIUS AUGUSTINE

<margin>Matthew 1</margin>
...debate: so that it might be thought that this was said entirely in vain: *You shall call his name Jesus; for he himself will save his people from their sins.* <note>original: "Vocabis nomen eius Iesum: ipse enim saluū faciet populū suū à peccatis eorū"; Matthew 1:21. The name "Jesus" literally means "The Lord saves."</note> For how will he make them whole, where there is no sickness? Indeed, sins—from which the Gospel says Christ’s people must be saved—are not <term>substances</term> <note>In ancient philosophy, a "substance" is something that exists in its own right, like a tree or a body. Augustine's opponent, Pelagius, argued that if sin is just a "choice" and not a physical "thing," it cannot "infect" or "break" human nature.</note>; and according to that man [Pelagius], they cannot cause damage. O brother, it is good for you to remember that you are a Christian. Perhaps it would be enough to simply believe these things, but since you wish to debate: it does no harm—indeed, it is beneficial—provided that a very firm faith comes first. Let us not think that human nature cannot be damaged by sin; rather, believing the divine Scriptures that it has been damaged by sin, let us inquire how that could have happened, since we have already learned that sin is not a substance. 

Is it not observed—to set other things aside—that even *not eating* is not a substance? For one withdraws from a substance, since food is a substance. But to abstain from food is not a substance; and yet the substance of the body, if one abstains from food entirely, so languishes, is so corrupted by an imbalance of health, is so drained of strength, and so weakened and broken by exhaustion, that if it should somehow survive in life, it can hardly be brought back to that food from which it was damaged by abstaining. 

In this way, sin is not a substance; but God is a substance—the highest substance, and the only true food of the rational creature. By withdrawing from Him through disobedience, and through weakness not being able to take in what one ought, and to rejoice... 
<margin>Psalm 101</margin>
...you hear how he [the Psalmist] says: *I am smitten like grass, and my heart has withered, because I have forgotten to eat my bread.* <note>original: "Percußus sum sicut fœnū, et aruit cor meū, quoniā oblitus sum comedere panē meū"; Psalm 102:4 in modern Bibles (Psalm 101 in the Vulgate). Augustine uses this to show that "forgetting" God results in a withered soul.</note>

<summary>Augustine refutes the Pelagian idea that sin cannot damage nature because it isn't a physical substance, using the analogy of starvation to show how a lack (of food or God) can destroy a substance (the body or soul).</summary>

# THAT JESUS IS THE SAVIOR
## of all, as much of Adam himself as of small children and all his descendants, since they are infirm.
### Chapter 21.

But notice how that man <note>Augustine is referring to his opponent, Pelagius.</note> still pushes himself forward with plausible-sounding arguments against the truth of the Holy Scriptures. 

<margin>Matthew 1</margin>
The Lord Jesus says—who for that reason is called "Jesus," because he himself saved his people from their sins <note>original: "Iesus... saluum fecit populum suum à peccatis eoru[m]"; the name Jesus is derived from the Hebrew for "The Lord saves."</note>.

<margin>Matthew 9</margin>
Therefore the Lord Jesus says: "It is not the healthy who need a physician, but the sick; I have not come to call the righteous, but sinners." 

<margin>1 Timothy 1</margin>
From which the Apostle also says: "It is a faithful saying, and worthy of all acceptance, that Jesus Christ came into this world to save sinners." 

And yet that man, against this faithful saying and one worthy of all acceptance, says that this <term>infirmity</term> <note>Augustine uses "infirmity" or "sickness" as a metaphor for the state of human nature after the Fall. If we aren't "sick" with sin, he argues, we don't need Christ the "Physician."</note> ought not to have been contracted through sins, nor should that punishment of sin exist in such a way that even more sins might be committed. It is asked, then: does so great a physician help small children? And that man says: "Why do you ask? They too are healthy; why are you looking for a physician for those who are well?" 

He even claims that the first man himself <note>Adam</note> was not condemned to death for that reason. For he says that Adam did not sin afterward—as if he had heard anything regarding the perfection of Adam's righteousness later on, except for what the Church commends: that Adam himself was liberated by the mercy of the Lord Christ. 

That man also says of Adam’s descendants: not only are they not weaker than he was, but they have even fulfilled more commandments, whereas Adam neglected to fulfill even one. He sees these descendants born in such a way—certainly not as that first man was made—not only incapable of understanding a commandment, which they do not perceive at all, but scarcely capa... <note>The text cuts off mid-word ("capaces"), likely continuing "capable of life" or "capable of reason" on the next page.</note>

<summary>Augustine argues that Pelagius’s denial of original sin makes Christ’s role as "Savior" and "Physician" unnecessary. He maintains that all people, including infants and Adam himself, require Christ’s mercy because human nature has been weakened by sin.</summary>

<margin>Psalm 101.</margin> —you hear how the scripture says: *“I am struck down like grass, and my heart has withered, because I have forgotten to eat my bread”* <note>original: "Percussus sum sicut fœnū, et aruit cor meū, quoniā oblitus sum comedere panē meū." <header>FROM THE BOOKS OF ST. AURELIUS AUGUSTINE</header>

...[even those who seek] the breasts when they are hungry, yet He wishes to save them in the womb of Mother Church by His grace—He who "saves His people from their sins" <note>original: "saluum facit populum suum à peccatis eorū." A reference to Matthew 1:21.</note>. Men contradict this, and as if they knew how to look into the creature which was made through Him better than He does, they proclaim with an unhealthy voice that [people] are healthy.

### HOW MANIFOLD IS THE PUNISHMENT
### for sin, and the heavy retribution of sinners
### through their sins. Chapter XXII.

He <note>Referring to Pelagius.</note> says that the matter of sin is a <term>retribution</term> <note>original: "uindictam." Augustine is arguing that "punishment" isn't just an external penalty, but the internal spiritual decay that leads to further sinning.</note>, if the sinner has been so weakened to this point that he might sin more. The transgressor of the law does not consider what worthy light of truth abandons him: once abandoned by it, he surely becomes blind; and it is necessary that he stumble more, and be burdened by falling, and being burdened, he does not rise. He hears the voice of the law only so that he might be warned to implore the grace of the Savior. Or is there no punishment for those of whom the Apostle says: 
<margin>Romans 1</margin> 
*“Because when they had known God, they did not glorify Him as God, or give thanks; but they became vain in their thoughts, and their foolish heart was darkened”*? 

Certainly that darkening was already a retribution and a punishment, and yet through this punishment—that is, through the blindness of the heart, which happens when the light of wisdom departs—they collapsed into more and serious sins. For, *“claiming to be wise, they became fools.”* This is a heavy punishment, if anyone understands it, and see from this punishment where they went. *“And they changed,”* he says, *“the glory of the incorruptible God into the likeness of an image of corruptible man, and of birds, and of four-footed beasts, and of serpents.”* They did these things...

<meta>catchword: [they] expect</meta>

<summary>Augustine argues that sin is not just an act, but a self-perpetuating punishment where the loss of divine light leads to deeper spiritual blindness and more serious sins. He uses the opening of the Epistle to the Romans to show that "darkness of heart" is both a result of and a penalty for rejecting God.</summary>

-># ON NATURE AND GRACE.<-
<sig>c</sig>

...from the punishment of sin, by which their foolish heart was darkened <note>original: "obscuratũ est insipiens cor eorum." A continuation of Romans 1:21 from the previous page.</note>.

*And yet because of these things, because even sins themselves are permitted to be punishments, he adds and says: Therefore God gave them up to the desires of their heart unto uncleanness:* Behold how God has condemned them more severely, giving them up to the desires of their heart unto uncleanness. See also what they do as a result of this punishment, so that, as he says, *they dishonor their own bodies in themselves.* And because this is a punishment of iniquity <note>original: "pœna... iniquitatis." Augustine emphasizes that the consequence of sin is not a separate penalty, but the further degradation of the soul.</note> while also being iniquity itself, he commends it more evidently, saying: *Who changed the truth of God into a lie, and worshipped and served the creature rather than the Creator, who is blessed forever, amen. For this reason,* he says, *God gave them up to shameful passions.* 

Behold how often God exacts <term>retribution</term> <note>original: "uindicat." In this context, divine retribution is not a lightning bolt, but God stepping back and allowing the sinner to face the natural consequences of their choices.</note>, and from that very retribution more and heavier sins arise. *For their women,* he says, *changed the natural use into that use which is against nature. And likewise also the men, leaving the natural use of the woman, burned in their desire toward one another, men with men working that which is shameful.* <note>original: "deformitatem." Literally "deformity" or "lack of form," implying a corruption of the natural order intended by the Creator.</note> And so that he might show that these sins are such that they are also the punishments of sins, he added to these: *And receiving in themselves the mutual reward <note>original: "mercedem mutuam." Often translated as "recompense."</note> that was due for their error.* 

See how many times He takes retribution; and observe further what sins that same retribution brings forth and causes to sprout. *And as they did not see fit,* he says, *to have God in their knowledge, God gave them up to a reprobate mind, to do those things which are not convenient, being filled with all iniquity, with deceit, malice...* <note>The text breaks off here, continuing the list of vices from Romans 1:29.</note>

<summary>Augustine uses Paul’s Epistle to the Romans to argue that sin serves as its own punishment, where God "gives up" the prideful to increasingly degrading behaviors as a consequence of their initial rejection of Him.</summary>

## HOW GOD ABANDONS THOSE
## worthy of being abandoned, and how free

### Chapter XXIII.

Perhaps he might respond that God does not compel anyone toward these evils, but only abandons those who deserve to be abandoned. If he says this, he speaks most truly: for once they are abandoned, as has been said, by the light of righteousness, and thereby <margin>Ephesians 5</margin> shrouded in darkness, what else could they bring forth but all these "works of darkness" that I have mentioned? The Pelagians argued that since sin is an action and not a physical "thing" or substance, it cannot physically or essentially alter or "damage" human nature. Augustine counters that even non-substantial things, like a wound or death, represent a real loss of well-being.</note> No one says to him that man was made in such a way that he could move from righteousness into sin, but could not return from sin to righteousness; rather, we say that to fall into sin, <term>free choice</term> <note>original: "liberum arbitrium." This refers to the human capacity to make decisions. Augustine argues that while we have the freedom to choose wrongly on our own, the choice to return to God requires grace.</note> is enough, for by it he corrupted himself. However, to return to righteousness, he has need...

<summary>Augustine argues that while sin is not a physical substance, it acts as a spiritual death that renders the soul incapable of self-recovery. He maintains that while "free choice" is sufficient for a human to fall into sin, only the grace of Christ (the "physician") can restore a person to righteousness.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Augustine’s argument from the previous section. He is refuting the Pelagian idea that the human will is sufficient to achieve righteousness without divine assistance. The previous page established that when God abandons the sinner to their own darkness, they fall into a spiral of vices. This page argues that just as a sick person cannot heal themselves without a doctor, a soul "dead" in sin cannot return to life without the grace of Christ.</meta>

<header>ON NATURE AND GRACE.</header>
<sig>c 2</sig>

...needs a physician <note>original: "medico"</note>, since he is not healthy; he needs a life-giver <note>original: "uiuificatore"</note> because he is dead. Regarding this grace, that man <note>Augustine refers to his opponent, Pelagius.</note> says absolutely nothing, as if one could heal himself by his own **will** <note>original: "uoluntate"</note> alone, simply because he was able to corrupt himself by that will alone. For we do not say that the death of the body constitutes a sin, where there is only punishment. Indeed, no one sins by the body dying; rather, it is the death of the soul—which has been deserted by its life, that is, its God—that constitutes sin. This soul must necessarily perform "dead works" until it is brought back to life by the grace of Christ. 

Far be it from us to say that hunger, thirst, and other bodily troubles <note>original: "molestias corporales"</note> create a necessity for sinning. Through these very troubles, the life of the righteous, having been tested, has shone more brightly; by overcoming them through **patience** <note>original: "patientiam"</note>, they have secured a greater glory. But they did this aided by the grace of God, aided by the spirit of God, aided by the mercy of God—not by exalting themselves with a proud will, but by obtaining strength through humble confession.

<margin>Psalm 71</margin>
<note>The marginal note says "Psal. 7," but the quote "For you are my patience" is found in Psalm 71:5 (Psalm 70:5 in the Vulgate).</note>
For he knew how to say to God: *For you are my patience.* Regarding this grace, help, and mercy—without which we cannot live well—I do not know why that man says nothing at all. On the contrary, by defending **nature** <note>original: "naturam"</note> as if the will alone were sufficient for **righteousness** <note>original: "iustitiam"</note>, he most clearly contradicts the grace of Christ by which we are justified. 

Now, as for why bodily death remains for the testing of our faith even after the guilt of **sin** <note>original: "peccatum"</note> has been dissolved through grace (even though death originally came from sin), I have already discussed this as best I could in those books written to **Marcellinus** <note>Flavius Marcellinus was a high-ranking Roman official and friend of Augustine. Augustine addressed several works to him, including "On the Merits and Forgiveness of Sins," which dealt with the origins of death and the necessity of infant baptism.</note> of holy memory.

<summary>Augustine argues that the human will can corrupt itself but cannot heal itself without God's grace, comparing the sinner to a dead man needing a life-giver. He distinguishes between the death of the soul (sin) and the death of the body (a trial for the faithful), citing his previous works for Marcellinus.</summary>

### THAT WE MUST ACKNOWLEDGE
### *our misery, and thus seek and*
### *implore mercy.* Chapter 25.

A proud mind has no taste for this at all; but the Lord is great, and He may persuade the soul in the way that He alone knows. For we are more inclined to search for how we might reply against the things charged against our error, than to pay attention to what is healthy for us so that we might be free from error. Hence, with such people, we must proceed not so much with arguments as with prayers—for them as well as for ourselves. For we do not say to them what this man <note>Referring to Pelagius, who accused Augustine of making sin sound "necessary."</note> suggested: that sin was necessary so that there might be a cause for God's mercy. Would that there had been no misery, so that this mercy would not have been necessary! But a most just punishment <note>original: "pœna iustissima"</note> followed the iniquity of sin—which was all the graver because man could have so easily avoided sinning, as he was not yet held by any infirmity. The result was that he received the reciprocal reward of his sin within himself: he lost that obedience of his own body (which was placed beneath him) in the same way that he, as a subordinate, had despised obedience toward his own Lord.
<margin>Romans 7</margin>

# A BOOK OF SAINT AURELIUS AUGUSTINE
## THAT GOD NOT ONLY HEALS,
### so as to erase what we have sinned, but also provides
### that we may not sin; proved by examples
### taken from a wounded man and from the eye. Chapter 26

Pay attention very carefully to what he <note>Augustine is quoting his opponent, Pelagius.</note> has said: "God indeed applies His mercy even to this part, whenever it shall have been necessary, because it is necessary for man after sin to be so helped, not because God desired a cause of this kind of necessity." Do you not see how he does not say that the mercy of God is necessary so that we might not sin, but only because we *have* sinned? Then he adds: "But a physician also ought to be ready to cure a man who is already wounded; he ought not, however, to wish that a healthy man be wounded." 

If this comparison fits the matters we are discussing, then certainly human nature cannot be "wounded" by sin, since sin is not a substance. <note>original: "peccatum nulla substantia est." Augustine frequently argued against the Manichaeans that evil/sin is not a physical "thing" or substance, but rather a lack or perversion of good.</note> Therefore, just as one who limps from a wound, for example, is cured so that—once the past injury is healed—his future stride might be made straight; so also the Heavenly Physician <term>Physician (Medicus)</term><note>A favorite title for Christ in Augustine’s writings, emphasizing His role in healing the "sickness" of the human soul.</note> heals our evils not only so that they might no longer exist, but so that for the rest of our lives we might be able to walk rightly. This is something that we would not be able to do, even if we were "healthy," <note>Referring to the state of being free from the guilt of past sin.</note> except with Him helping us. 

For when a human physician has healed a man, he then leaves him to God to be sustained by the elements and by physical nourishment, so that this same health might grow strong and persist through suitable support. It is God who provides these things to those living in the flesh, and to Him belonged even those medicines which the doctor applied while he was curing the patient. For a doctor does not heal anyone using things he created himself, but rather from the resources of Him who creates all things necessary for both the healthy and the diseased. But God Himself, when through the Mediator <note>Refers to Jesus Christ as the "Mediator between God and men" (1 Timothy 2:5).</note> of God and ma...

<meta>catchword: ho (beginning of "hominum" or "homines")</meta>

<summary>Augustine refutes the idea that grace is only a reactive cure for past sins, using the analogy of a doctor to show that God’s ongoing support is necessary for both healing and maintaining "spiritual health."</summary>

--- Page 51 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument from Chapter 26, where Augustine uses the analogy of a physician to explain that grace is not just a past remedy but a present necessity. It then transitions into Chapter 27, where Augustine addresses Pelagius's critique of the idea that sin might be "permitted" by God to prevent the greater sin of pride.</meta>

->ON NATURE AND GRACE<-

the Lord, the man Jesus Christ, spiritually heals the sick or gives life to the dead—that is, he **justifies the ungodly**—and when he has led him to perfect health, that is, to perfect life and justice, he does not abandon him unless he is first abandoned, so that he may always live piously and justly. For just as the eye of the body, even when perfectly healthy, cannot see unless assisted by the brightness of light, so also a man, even if most perfectly **justified**, cannot live rightly unless he is divinely assisted by the eternal light of justice. God heals, therefore, not only to erase what we have sinned, but also to provide that we may not sin. <note>Augustine is arguing against the "Pelagian" view that grace is only needed for the forgiveness of past sins. He insists that humans need God's "light" constantly to act rightly, just as a healthy eye still needs light to see.</note>

## HOW TRULY IT IS SAID, THAT
## *a man cannot be without sin, so that the*
## *occasion for pride and glory—which is*
## *accustomed to mingle itself even with right deeds—may be removed.*

### Chapter XXVII.

He <note>Pelagius, Augustine's intellectual opponent.</note> certainly treats and turns this over acutely, and as much as it seems to him, he refutes and proves wrong what is said to them: that it was even necessary for man, for the removal of the occasion of pride or glory, that he could not be without sin. Indeed, he thinks it most absurd and foolish that there should be sin lest there should be sin, since **pride** itself is certainly a sin; as if an ulcer is not also painful, and yet a surgical incision <term>incision (sectio)</term> causes pain so that pain might be removed by pain. If we had not experienced this, and we heard of it in some lands where these things never happened, we would without doubt laugh at it, and perhaps we would even use his words and say: "It is most absurd that pain should be necessary so that there should be no pain from an ulcer." But "God," they say, "can heal all things." <note>Augustine uses a medical analogy: sometimes a doctor must cause a small pain (an incision) to heal a greater one (an ulcer). He is suggesting that God might allow a person to struggle with small failings to prevent them from falling into the "ulcer" of pride.</note>

<margin>2 Cor. 12</margin>

<margin>Philip. 2</margin>

It is because our will—without which we cannot work well—can quickly steal away the human mind, leading it to think that what it works well is its own doing alone. It might then say in its abundance: *I shall never be moved.*

For if he notices that sometimes the heat of fevers is broken by certain medicinal heats, he might perhaps even concede that fires can be extinguished by fires.

...that to be proud is to sin. For ask what any sin whatever is, and see if you can find any sin that can be called anything other than pride. He [Pelagius] pursues this opinion in this way, and tries to prove it thus: "Every sin," he says, "unless I am mistaken, is contempt of God, and all contempt of God is pride. For what is so proud as to despise God? Let him search diligently, and he will find in the law that the sin of pride is greatly distinguished from other sins. For many sins are indeed committed through pride, but not all things done wrongly are done proudly—certainly not by those who are ignorant, certainly not by the weak, and certainly, for the most part, not by those who are weeping and groaning. <meta>This page continues Augustine’s rebuttal of Pelagius. In the previous section, Augustine agreed that while pride is the root of sin, not every individual sin is an act of prideful contempt; many arise from human weakness. Here, he addresses the Pelagian argument that if a sin is "necessary" (unavoidable), it cannot be blamed on the individual, and explores why God allows pride to persist even in the hearts of the virtuous.</meta>

<header>ON NATURE AND GRACE.</header>

Could he be held subject to the guilt of that sin which he knows is not his own? "For it is not his own," he [Pelagius] says, "if it is necessary. Or if it is his own, it is voluntary; and if it is voluntary, it can be avoided." We respond that it is entirely his own. But the <term>vice</term> <note>Latin: *vitium* — here referring to a moral defect or a corrupted state of human nature resulting from the Fall</note> by which it is committed has not yet been healed in every part; this vice, in order to take root, descended from <term>health</term> <note>Latin: *sanitate* — referring to the original state of integrity and free will possessed by Adam before the Fall</note> that was not used rightly. From this vice, being now in poor health, a person commits many things, either through weakness or blindness. For this, one must pray to be healed, and hereafter live in perpetual health; one must not be proud, as if a human being is healed by the same power by which he was corrupted.

### HOW DEEP IS THE COUNSEL OF
### God, who does not quickly heal pride—though pious souls tearfully
### pray for its destruction—as it entangles itself 
### even within good works.

### Chapter XXXI.

And indeed, I say these things while confessing that I am ignorant of the deeper <term>counsel of God</term> <note>original: *consilium Dei* — a reference to the mysterious and unsearchable plans of God, often cited by Augustine when human logic fails to explain divine timing</note>. Why does God not quickly heal that very pride which lies in wait for the human soul even in deeds done rightly? For its healing, those pious souls pray to Him with tears and great groans: that He might extend His right hand to those striving to overcome it, and in some way to trample and crush it. For when a man has rejoiced in some good work, pride raises its head out of that very joy even as he seems to have overcome pride, and says: "Behold, I live because you triumph; and I live precisely because you are triumphant." For perhaps it is delightful to triumph over pride as if it were already conquered before the proper time, whereas its final shadow will, as I believe, be swallowed up in that "noon day." This "noon day" of Scripture <note>A reference to the biblical "midday" or "noon," often interpreted by Church Fathers as the full light of eternal life or the presence of Christ where no shadows of sin remain</note>...

<summary>Augustine argues that sin remains "ours" even when caused by a corrupted nature, and explores the psychological paradox where one feels prideful about successfully overcoming pride.</summary>

### THAT THE MERCY OF GOD 
### precedes us by healing, and follows us

For he seems to have had no one else in mind when he said, *and he himself will do it,* except those who say: "we do it," that is, we justify ourselves. In this matter, we certainly work as well, but we <term>cooperate</term> <note>original: *cooperamur* — a key theological term where Augustine acknowledges human agency, but only as a response enabled by divine grace</note> with him as he works; for his mercy precedes us. It precedes us so that we may be healed, and it follows us so that we may be glorified; it precedes us so that we may live piously, and it follows us so that we may live with him forever, because without him we can do nothing. Both are written: *And my God, his mercy shall precede me;* and *Your mercy shall follow me all the days of my life.* Let us therefore reveal our way to that mercy by <term>confession</term> <note>original: *confessione* — here meaning the humble acknowledgment of sin, contrasted with "defense," which is the prideful excusing of sin</note>, not praise it by defense. For if the way is not his, but ours, it is undoubtedly not the right way. Let us reveal it by confessing, because it is not hidden from him even if we attempt to cover it. For *it is good to confess to the Lord.*

This is a central part of Augustine's argument against Pelagius, asserting that even the righteous must fear falling through pride.</meta>

# ON NATURE AND GRACE.

<note>original: "scriptum est" (it is written) — the text begins mid-word from the previous page's "confessione"</note> ...is written, that He may turn our paths from our own way and make our way be that which is His. For this is provided by Him to those believing in Him and hoping in Him, so that He Himself may act. For this is the righteous way, 
<margin>Romans 10</margin>
which those who have a zeal for God, but not according to knowledge—and wishing to establish their own <term>righteousness</term> <note>original: *iustitiae* — here referring to the Pelagian idea that humans can be righteous by their own willpower</note>—have not submitted to the righteousness of God. For the end of the law is Christ for righteousness to everyone who believes, who said:
<margin>John 14</margin>
> I am the way.

Even for those already walking in this way, the divine voice brings fear, lest they be lifted up as if by their own strength. To those for whom the Apostle said:
<margin>Philippians 2</margin>
> Work out your own salvation with fear and trembling; for it is God who works in you, both to will and to work according to his good pleasure.

For this same reason, the Psalm also says:
<margin>Psalm 2</margin>
> Serve the Lord in fear, and rejoice in Him with trembling: embrace discipline, lest the Lord ever be angry, and you perish from the righteous way, when His wrath shall be kindled against you in a short time.

The text does not say: "Lest the Lord ever be angry and not show you the righteous way," or "not lead you into the righteous way." Rather, for those already walking there, He sought to terrify them by saying: "lest you perish from the righteous way." Why is this? It is because <term>pride</term> <note>original: *superbia* — Augustine identifies pride as the root of all sin and the specific failing of those who believe they can achieve holiness without constant divine help</note>—which I have said so many times and must say often—must be guarded against even in those very deeds done rightly (that is, in the righteous way itself). This is so that a man, while he considers that which is God's to be his own, does not lose what is God's and return to what is merely his own. Therefore, let us do as this Psalm concludes:
> Blessed are all they who trust in Him.

This is certainly so that He Himself may act, and He Himself may show His way to the one who says:
<margin>Psalm 84</margin> <note>Modern Bibles: Psalm 85:7</note>
> Show us, O Lord, Your mercy,

and that He Himself may give salvation, so that [we may] walk...

<summary>Augustine argues that even those already living righteously must remain humble and fearful of pride, as salvation and the ability to "will and work" are gifts from God rather than personal achievements. He uses the warning from Psalm 2 to show that one can "perish from the way" if they begin to attribute their good works to their own strength instead of to Christ.</summary>

### THAT ALTHOUGH A MAN MAY BE ASSERTED TO BE WITHOUT SIN, HE IS NOT THEREFORE COMPARED TO GOD; AND THAT HUMILITY IS LOCATED ON THE SIDE OF TRUTH; AND HOW GOD NOT ONLY FORGIVES COMMITTED SINS, BUT ALSO ASSISTS IN AVOIDING FUTURE ONES. CHAPTER 34.

<header>ON NATURE AND GRACE:</header>
<meta>catchword: Therefore he ought not</meta>

to God. <note>The text completes the sentence from the previous page: "as if indeed an angel... were compared to God."</note> Although they strongly support their opinion, I confess that I am not persuaded by this. Now indeed, I strongly favor the author of this book <note>Augustine is referring to Pelagius, whose work he is critiquing, though he often refrains from naming him directly.</note> in this respect: against those who say, "What you assert seems reasonable, but it is proud to say that a man can be without sin," he responds in such a way that if it is entirely true, it should by no means be called proud. For he says most sharply and truly:
> In which part should humility be placed? Without doubt, it belongs to the side of falsehood if pride is proven to be on the side of truth.

And through this, it pleases him—and rightly so—that humility should be placed on the side of truth rather than on the side of falsehood. From this it follows that he <note>Referring to the Apostle John.</note> who said:
<margin>1 John 1</margin>
> If we say that we have no sin, we deceive ourselves, and the truth is not in us:

should not be doubted to have spoken the truth, nor should he be seen to have said this falsely for the sake of humility. For this reason he added, "and the truth is not in us," when perhaps it might have been enough to say, "we deceive ourselves," unless he noticed that some people think it is said "we deceive ourselves" because even one who praises himself for a <term>true good</term> <note>original: "vero bono" — Augustine distinguishes between lying about having a virtue and being proud of a virtue one actually possesses.</note> is puffed up. Therefore, by adding:
> And the truth is not in us:

he clearly shows—just as this author most rightly holds—that it is not true at all if we say we have no sin; otherwise, humility, if placed on the side of falsehood, would lose the reward of truth. Furthermore, however, because he <note>The author Pelagius.</note> seems to act as God's advocate by defending <term>nature</term> <note>original: "naturam" — in this context, the inherent capacities of human beings after the Fall.</note>, he does not notice that by saying this same nature is healthy, he rejects the mercy of the **Physician**. <note>original: "medici" — a favorite metaphor of Augustine, where Christ is the Physician who heals the "disease" of sin.</note> Yet He who is its **Creator** is the same one who is its **Savior**.

<summary>Augustine argues that humility must be rooted in truth rather than "pious lies," citing the Apostle John to prove that humans are indeed sinners. --- Page 60 ---
<meta>This page continues Augustine’s rebuttal of the Pelagian view. The text carries over from the previous page where Augustine warns against praising nature so much that we forget our need for God's grace.</meta>

<header>THE BOOK OF LORD AURELIUS AUGUSTINE</header>

...[we must] not so praise the Creator that we are forced to say—or rather, truly convinced to say—that the Savior is superfluous. Therefore, let us honor human nature with worthy praises, and let us refer those praises to the glory of the Creator. But because He created us, let us be so grateful that we are not ungrateful for the fact that He heals us. Indeed, let us attribute our <term>vices</term> <note>original: "vitia" — here referring to moral defects or sins that wound human nature.</note> which He heals, not to the divine work, but to the human will and His just punishment. But just as we confess that it was in our power that these sins should not happen, so let us admit that their healing lies more in His mercy than in our power. 

This man <note>Augustine is referring to his opponent, Pelagius.</note> places this mercy and the medicinal help of the Savior only in this: that He forgives past sins, not that He helps us to avoid future ones. Here he is most dangerously deceived; here, even if unknowingly, he prevents us from watching and praying lest we enter into temptation, since he contends that it is in our power alone that this should not happen to us.

### WHY THE EXAMPLES OF CERTAIN PEOPLE 
### WHOM WE READ HAVE SINNED WERE WRITTEN DOWN, AND 
### HOW WE MIGHT BE ABLE TO DIE WITHOUT SIN, ALTHOUGH 
### WE CANNOT LIVE WITHOUT IT. Chapter 35.

Anyone of sound mind says that the examples of certain people who have sinned—which we read in Scripture—were not written so that they might encourage despair of avoiding sin, or seem to provide us with a kind of security in sinning. Instead, they were written so that we might learn either the humility of <term>repentance</term> <note>original: "pœnitēdi" — the act of seeking forgiveness and changing one's heart.</note>, or that we should not despair of salvation even in such falls. For when those who have fallen into sins perish more through despair, they neglect not only the medicine of repentance...

...[not as] medicine, but they become slaves to their lusts and wicked cravings to fulfill dishonest and shameful desires. They act as if they would lose out if they did not do what their lust urges, even though certain <term>damnation</term> <note>original: "damnatio" — in this context, the state of being condemned for unrepented mortal sin.</note> already awaits them. Against this disease, which is exceedingly dangerous and deadly, it is helpful to remember the sins into which even the just and holy have fallen. 

But he <note>Augustine is referring to his opponent, Pelagius.</note> seems to ask shrewdly how we should believe those saints departed from this life: were they with sin, or without sin? He does this so that if the answer is "with sin," it might be thought that damnation followed them—which is a wicked thing to believe. But if it is said they exited this life without sin, he would use this to prove that a person could at least be without sin in this life when death is approaching. 

In this matter, though he is very sharp, he pays too little attention to the fact that it is not in vain that even the just say in prayer <margin>Matthew 6</margin>: "Forgive us our debts, as we also forgive our debtors." And when the Lord Christ had explained this same prayer while teaching, he most truthfully added: "For if you forgive men their sins, your Father will also forgive you your sins." For through this daily spiritual incense, as it were, which is offered before God on the altar of the heart—which we are exhorted to "lift up" <note>This refers to the *Sursum Corda* ("Lift up your hearts"), an ancient liturgical invitation used during the Mass.</note>—it is possible to die without sin, even if one does not live here without sin. This is possible provided that whatever is committed from time to time through ignorance or weakness is immediately blotted out by <term>pardon</term> <note>original: "venia" — small or "venial" sins that do not destroy the life of grace in the soul but require forgiveness.</note>.

Next, he <note>Augustine refers to his opponent, the theologian Pelagius.</note> commemorates those who are reported not only to have avoided sin but even to have lived justly: Abel, Enoch, Melchizedek, Abraham, Isaac, Jacob, Joseph, Joshua <note>original: "Iesus Naue"—the Latin form of Joshua, son of Nun.</note>, Phinehas, Samuel, Nathan, Elijah, Elisha, Micah, Daniel, Ananias, Azarias, Mishael, Ezekiel, Mordecai, Simeon, Joseph (to whom the Virgin Mary was betrothed), and John. For how do we know what more grace was bestowed upon her to overcome sin in every respect, she who merited to conceive and give birth to him who it is certain had no sin? 

With this Virgin excepted, if we could gather all those holy men and women while they lived here and ask them whether they were without sin, what do we think they would have answered? Would it be what this man <note>Pelagius.</note> says, or what the Apostle John says? I ask you, however great the excellence of their holiness was in this body, if they could have been asked this question, <margin>1 John 1</margin> they would have cried out with one voice: "If we say that we have no sin, we deceive ourselves, and the truth is not in us."

## THAT NOT ALL THOSE OF WHOM SCRIPTURE RECORDED GOOD THINGS, OR OF WHOM IT DID NOT REPORT THAT THEY SINNED, LIVED WITHOUT SIN.

### Chapter XXXVII.

"Perhaps," he <note>Augustine refers to Pelagius, who argued that humans could live without sin through their own will.</note> says, "Scripture could not record the sins of everyone?" And those who say this indeed speak the truth; nor do I see that any valid response has been made against this, although I see that he <note>Pelagius.</note> did not wish to remain silent. I ask you to pay attention to what he says next. "This," he says, "can be rightly said of those whose good or evil deeds Scripture does not mention. But concerning those whose righteousness it remembers, it would undoubtedly have remembered their sins also, if it had perceived them to have sinned."

Let him say, then, that such great faith did not belong to the righteousness of those people—that massive multitude who, both those going before and those following after with praises of the Lord, also cried out to the donkey <note>original: "asellum"—the foal of a donkey on which Christ rode into Jerusalem.</note> even among their raging enemies who asked why they did this, shouting: "Hosanna <note>A Hebrew expression of praise, meaning "Save, we pray."</note> to the Son of David! Blessed is he who comes in the name <margin>Matthew 21</margin> of the Lord!" Let this man dare to say, if he can, that there was no one in that great multitude who had any sin at all. But if it is most absurd to say that, why did Scripture record none of their sins, when it took care to record such a great good as their faith?

But perhaps he saw this himself, and therefore he added and said: "But granted that at other times, because of the vast size of the crowd, it [Scripture] avoided weaving together the sins of everyone; yet in the very beginning of the world, where there were only four human beings <note>Adam, Eve, Cain, and Abel.</note>, what," he says, "do we say? Why did it not wish to record the transgressions of all? Was it because of a huge multitude, which did not yet exist? Or was it because only those who commit..."

## THAT THOSE THINGS WERE WRITTEN BY WHICH
### *a limit had to be set, and the reader instructed*
### *about many things by a few examples; and that those called*
### *righteous, though they are righteous, are yet*
### *not without sin. Chapter XXXVIII.*

In saying these things, he pays little attention to what he himself had said just before: that once the multitude of the human race had arisen, Scripture could have ignored recording the sins of everyone because of the sheer number of the crowd. For if he had paid enough attention to this, he would see that even in a single person there is a crowd and multitude of minor sins, which either could not have been written down, or even if they could, should not have been. Indeed, those things were written where a limit had to be observed, and where a reader should be instructed in many necessary things through a few examples. ### THAT WE OUGHT NOT TO BELIEVE EVERYTHING
#### that we read, and that it is not a sin to assert
#### something we have not read. Chapter 39.

He [Pelagius] concluded this passage with a truly grand sentence when he said:
> Let us believe, therefore, what we read; and what we do not read, let us consider it a sin to assert—which may suffice to have said about all [these matters].

<margin>1 Thessalonians 5</margin> On the contrary, I say that we ought not to believe everything we read, because of what the apostle says: "Read all things, hold fast to what is good" <note>original: "Omnia legite, quae bona sunt tenete" — Augustine is likely quoting a variant of 1 Thessalonians 5:21, which usually says "Test all things."</note>; and that it is not a sin to assert something even if we have not read it. For we can assert something in good faith to witnesses based on what we have experienced, even if perhaps we have not read it. Here he [Pelagius] might answer: "When I said this, I was dealing with the Holy Scriptures." Oh, would that he wished to assert nothing—I do not say other than what is read in those writings—but rather truly nothing *contrary* to what is read there; and would that he would faithfully and obediently hear what is written:
<margin>Romans 5</margin> "Through one man sin entered into the world, and through sin death, and so it passed into all men, in whom all have sinned" <note>original: "Per unum hominem peccatum intravit in mundum" — This is the core biblical text Augustine uses to support the doctrine of original sin.</note>; and would that he would not weaken the grace of so great a Physician while failing to confess...

<meta>This page continues Augustine’s rebuttal of Pelagius. Here, he moves to a core theological dispute: whether the "name of Christ" (the Gospel) is merely a set of instructions on how to live, or if it provides the actual power (grace) to live righteously.</meta>

->DE NATURE AND GRACE.<-
->d 4<-

[he] wants human nature to be corrupted. <note>original: "uult naturā humanā esse uitiatā" — Augustine is critiquing Pelagius's view. Pelagius argued that human nature remained "pure" after the Fall, whereas Augustine argues it was "vitiated" or damaged by original sin.</note> Oh, I wish that as a Christian <margin>Acts 4</margin> he would read that there is no other name under heaven given to men in which we must be saved <note>original: "nullū esse nomē sub cælo in quo oporteat saluos fieri nos" — A direct quote of Acts 4:12.</note>, and that he would not so defend the <term>possibility</term> <note>original: "possibilitatē" — In this context, a technical term for the innate human capacity to do good without divine help.</note> of human nature that he believes a man can be saved through <term>free will</term> <note>original: "liberum arbitrium"</note> even without that name.

### THAT THROUGH THE KNOWLEDGE OF
### the name of Christ, we not only learn how to live, but are
### also helped by grace so that we may live well.

## Chapter 40.

But perhaps he thinks the name of Christ is necessary for this reason: so that we might learn through the Gospel how we ought to live, but not also so that we might be helped by His grace to live well. At the very least, let him confess from this that there is a wretched darkness in the human soul which [needs to] know how to live. Or is <term>free will</term> and the <term>natural law</term> <note>original: "lex quæ naturalis" — The moral law Pelagius believed was inherent to human reason.</note> sufficient for it to know this? This is the <term>wisdom of word</term> <note>original: "sapientia uerbi" — Often translated as "wisdom of words" or "eloquence," referring to human sophistry that obscures the Gospel (1 Corinthians 1:17).</note> by which the cross of Christ is made void. But He who <margin>Obadiah 1</margin> <note>The margin cites Obadiah 1:8, which speaks of destroying the wisdom of the wise.</note> said: <margin>1 Corinthians 1</margin> *I will destroy the wisdom of the wise*—because that cross cannot be made void—surely overturns that wisdom through the "foolishness of preaching" by which those who believe are healed. For if natural possibility through free will is sufficient both for knowing how one ought to live and for living well, then Christ died in vain; therefore, the <term>stumbling block of the cross</term> <note>original: "scandalum crucis" — A reference to Galatians 5:11.</note> has been made void. Why should I not also cry out here? Indeed, I shall cry out with the fearless sorrow of a Christian: <margin>Galatians 5</margin> *You have been made void of Christ, you who are justified by nature; [you have fallen from grace...]* <note>original: "Euacuati estis à Christo, q ī natura iustificamini à" — Augustine is adapting Galatians 5:4. Where the Apostle Paul says "justified by the law," Augustine substitutes "by nature" to directly address Pelagius's error.</note>

<summary>Augustine argues that Pelagius reduces Christ's role to a mere teacher of ethics, ignoring the necessity of grace for the actual performance of good works. He warns that relying on natural human capacity "empties the cross" of its power and necessity for salvation.</summary>
<keywords>grace vs nature, Pelagius, free will, wisdom of the world, justification, cross of Christ, Acts 4:12, Galatians 5:4</keywords>

<header>BOOK OF SAINT AURELIUS AUGUSTINE</header>

<margin>Romans 10</margin> you have fallen away from grace; for being ignorant of the <term>righteousness of God</term> <note>original: "iustitiā dei" — Augustine uses this to mean the righteousness that comes from God as a gift, rather than one's own moral achievements.</note>, and seeking to establish your own, you have not submitted yourselves to the righteousness of God. For just as Christ is the end of the law, so He is also the savior of a corrupted human nature, leading to righteousness for everyone who believes.

### THAT THE AUTHORITY OF THE APOSTLE, 
### "All have sinned" etc., should be understood regarding everyone, 
### both of old and more recent times.

### Chapter 41.

<margin>Romans 3</margin> But what did he [the Apostle Paul] present as being said by those against whom he speaks? "For all have sinned." <note>original: "Omnes enim peccauerunt" — A quote from Romans 3:23, a central pillar of Augustine's argument for original sin.</note> It is clear that the Apostle was speaking about those who lived then, that is, about the Jews and the <term>Gentiles</term> <note>Non-Jewish peoples.</note>. <margin>Romans 5</margin> But clearly, that which I mentioned—"Through one man sin entered into the world, and death through sin, and so it passed into all men, in whom all have sinned"—this sentence encompasses both those of old and more recent times, both us and our descendants. He [Pelagius] also puts forward a testimony to prove that when "all" are mentioned, it is not always necessary to understand "absolutely all," with no one omitted. "Just as," he says, "through one man's offense judgment came upon all men to condemnation, so also through one man's righteousness it came upon all men to the <term>justification</term> <note>The process by which a person is made right or "just" before God.</note> of life." Since, he argues, there is no doubt that through Christ's righteousness, not everyone, but only those who were willing to obey Him and were cleansed by the washing of His <term>baptism</term> <note>The Christian rite of initiation and purification.</note>, have been <term>sanctified</term> <note>Made holy.</note>. <margin>Romans 5</margin> Clearly, he does not prove what he wants with this testimony. For just as it was said, "As through one man's offense judgment came upon all men to condemnation," so that no one was left out; so also in that which was said, "through one man's righteousness it came upon all men to the justification of life," no one was omit— <note>The sentence breaks off here and continues on the following page.</note>

<summary>In this chapter, Augustine argues that the Apostle Paul’s statement that "all have sinned" applies to all humans throughout history due to the Fall of Adam. He addresses a counter-argument where "all" might not mean "everyone," but concludes that the universality of sin is absolute.</summary>

<sig>d 5</sig>

...died, not because all believe in Him and are washed by His baptism, but because no one is <term>justified</term> <note>The process by which a person is made righteous in the eyes of God.</note> unless they believe in Him and are washed by His baptism. Therefore, "all" are mentioned, so that no one may believe they can be saved in any other way except through Him. For just as when there is a single teacher of literature established in a city, we say most correctly: "This man teaches literature to everyone here," not because all the citizens are learning literature, but because no one learns unless he has been the one to teach them. In the same way, no one is justified except the one whom Christ has justified.

## THAT A HUMAN BEING CAN HAVE MOST PERFECT
## righteousness, but through the grace of God, and not in
## any other way, lest the Savior's help be made
## void when nature is defended as healthy.

"But so be it," he [Pelagius] says, "I agree, because [Scripture] testifies that all have been sinners." He says what they *were*, not that they could not have been something else. "Therefore," he says, "even if all men could be proven to be sinners, it would in no way go against our definition, for we are defending not so much what men *are*, as what they *can* be." 
<margin>Psalm 143</margin> <note>original: "Psal. 142" — Augustine uses the Vulgate numbering; in modern Bibles, this is Psalm 143:2: "For in your sight no one living is righteous."</note>
Here he does well to finally agree, because "no one living will be justified in the sight of God." <note>original: "non iustificabitur in conspectu dei omnis uiuens"</note> However, he contends that the question does not lie there, but rather in the very *possibility* of not sinning. On this point, we do not even need to struggle against him. For this is the truest, fullest, and most perfect righteousness; because that which is achieved by the human will, when aided by [the grace of God...]

<meta>Continuing from the previous page, Augustine is responding to Pelagius's distinction between "what a person is" (a sinner) and "what a person is capable of being" (sinless by nature). This page concludes Chapter 42 and provides the famous "Good Samaritan" defense in Chapter 43.</meta>

<header>THE BOOK OF SAINT AURELIUS AUGUSTINE</header>

<margin>Romans 5</margin>
I confess and defend that sinlessness can happen by the grace of God, but when or where or in whom it happens, I ought not to contend too much. Nor do I dispute the possibility itself, since, once the human will is healed and helped, that very possibility comes together with the actual result in the saints through the love of God. This occurs while the love of God—as fully as our healed and purged nature can receive it—is poured into our hearts through the Holy Spirit who has been given to us. <note>original: "diffunditur in cordibus noſtris p ſpũm ſanctũ"</note> Therefore, God's cause is better served than by what this man <note>Referring to Pelagius, Augustine's theological opponent.</note> claims to do in "defending nature"; for the Creator and Savior is recognized, whereas when a creature is defended as if healthy and with unimpaired strength, the assistance of the Savior is made void.

### THAT GOD DOES NOT COMMAND IMPOSSIBILITIES, BUT BY COMMANDING HE ADMONISHES US BOTH TO DO WHAT WE CAN AND TO ASK FOR WHAT WE CANNOT DO. Chapter 43.

<margin>Luke 10</margin>
It is true, however, what he [Pelagius] says: that God, who is as good as He is just, made man in such a way that it would have been sufficient for him to be free from the evil of sin, but only if he willed it. For who does not know that man was made healthy and blameless, established with <term>free will</term> <note>The capacity to choose between different possible courses of action unimpeded.</note> and free power to live justly? But now we are discussing the person whom the robbers left half-dead on the road; <note>An allegorical reference to the Parable of the Good Samaritan. Augustine views the "half-dead" traveler as a symbol of human nature damaged by Original Sin.</note> who, pierced and wounded by heavy blows, cannot ascend to the heights of righteousness in the same way he was able to descend from them. Even if he is already in the inn <note>Representing the Church or a state of recovery under grace.</note>, he is still being treated. Therefore, God does not command impossible things, but by commanding He reminds us both to do what you are able and to ask for what you are not able. Now let us see from where he has ability, and from where he does not. This man [Pelagius] says: "It is not by the will that nature is capable." <note>Pelagius argued that the 'capacity' for good is a fixed part of human nature, whereas the 'will' is our choice to use it.</note> I say: a man is not righteous by his will if nature is capable [on its own], but medicine will make possible what a defect makes impossible.

<summary>Augustine argues that while human nature was created good, it is now "wounded" by sin and requires the "medicine" of God's grace to achieve the righteousness that God commands. He famously asserts that God's commands are intended to drive humans to pray for the help they cannot provide for themselves.</summary>

<meta>Continuing from the previous page, Augustine focuses on the core of the dispute with Pelagius. ->ON NATURE AND GRACE.<-

## THAT EVEN IF MEN BOTH HAVE BEEN AND ARE WITHOUT SIN IN THIS <gloss>life</gloss>, THEY NEVERTHELESS COULD NOT HAVE BEEN NOR CAN THEY BE SO, EXCEPT BY BEING JUSTIFIED BY THE GRACE OF GOD.
### Chapter 44.

<margin>1 Timothy 2</margin>
<margin>2 Corinthians 4</margin>

Why then is there now a need to linger on more points? Let us come more deeply to the core issue, which in this specific question is either the only one, or almost the only one, we have with these men <note>Augustine is referring to the Pelagians, who argued that human beings could achieve sinlessness through their own natural willpower.</note>. For just as he <note>Pelagius</note> says that it does not pertain to his current argument to ask whether there have been or are any men in this life without sin, but rather whether they *could* have been or *can* be: so I, even if I were to agree that some have been or are sinless, nevertheless firmly maintain that they could not have been or be so in any way, except by being justified by the grace of God through Jesus Christ our Lord, and him crucified. For it was that very faith which healed the righteous ancients, the same faith which heals us: that is, faith in the <term>mediator</term> <note>A go-between; in Christian theology, Jesus is seen as the one who reconciles God and humanity.</note> of God and men, the man Jesus Christ. It is faith in his blood, faith in his cross, faith in his death, and his resurrection. Having, therefore, the same spirit of faith, we also believe, and for that reason we also speak. But let us observe what he responds to the objection raised against him—an objection in which he truly seems intolerable to Christian hearts. For he says: "But this is what disturbs many," you will say, "that you do not defend the idea that men can be without sin through the grace of God." That is precisely what disturbs us; that is what we object to. He identifies the very point; we endure this with the greatest difficulty. We cannot bear to have such things disputed by Christians, because of the love we have for others and for those very men themselves. Let us hear, then, how he frees himself from the objection of this question.

<summary>Augustine argues that sinlessness is only possible through the grace of Jesus Christ and faith in his sacrifice, rejecting Pelagius's claim that human nature alone is sufficient. He emphasizes that even the righteous figures of the past (the "ancient righteous") were saved by this same faith in the Mediator.</summary>

<meta>The translation continues from the previous page where Augustine was setting the stage for his debate with Pelagius. Augustine had just mentioned that while Pelagius claims he is only discussing the "possibility" of being sinless, this distinction is where the danger lies. On this page, Augustine quotes Pelagius directly and begins to dismantle his opponent's definitions of "ability" versus "action."</meta>

<header>BOOK BY THE LORD AURELIUS AUGUSTINE</header>

"O the blindness of ignorance," he <note>Pelagius, the British monk whose views on human nature Augustine is refuting.</note> says, "O the laziness of an unlearned mind, which thinks that what belongs only to God can be defended without the grace of God." If we did not know what followed, and had only heard these words—perhaps believing a false rumor spread about them or relying on the testimony of certain reliable brothers—we would think we had found common ground. For what could be said more briefly or more truly than that the **possibility** of not sinning, however much it exists or will exist in man, must be attributed to no one but God? This is exactly what we say; let us join hands <note>The Latin phrase *iungamus dexteras* literally means "let us join right hands," an idiom for reaching an agreement or shaking hands.</note>.

## WHAT THE DIFFERENCE IS BETWEEN 
## doing something and being able to do it, from the words 
## of Pelagius. Chapter 45.

But should we hear the rest? We certainly must hear them, so they may be corrected or at least avoided. For when he says that the **ability** itself <note>The "possibility" or "capacity" to act.</note> belongs not to human <term>choice</term> <note>original: *arbitrij*. Often translated as "free will," it refers to the human faculty of making decisions.</note> at all, but rather to the Author of nature—that is, God—and asks how it could be understood to happen without God’s grace when it is deemed to belong properly to God: now his meaning begins to appear. But lest we be deceived by chance, he explains it more broadly and more clearly. "But so that this may become more manifest," he says, "we must discuss it a little more extensively. For we say that the **possibility** of anything consists not so much in the power of human choice as in the necessity of **nature**." He even illustrates what he means with examples and analogies: "For instance," he says, "I am able to speak. That I am *able* to speak is not mine; but that I *do* speak is mine—that is, it belongs to my own will. And because the fact that I speak is mine, I can do both: that is, I can both speak and not speak. But because the fact that I *can* speak..." <note>The sentence cuts off here as it moves to the next page; Pelagius is arguing that God gives us the hardware (the ability to speak), but we control the software (what we actually say).</note>

<summary>Augustine quotes Pelagius, who argues that the "capacity" to be sinless is a gift from God (nature), but the actual "act" of being sinless is entirely up to human will. Augustine begins to point out how this definition of "grace" ignores the need for ongoing divine help in our specific actions.</summary>

<header>ON NATURE AND GRACE.</header>

...pear. <note>original: *sum*. This completes the word *apparere* (to appear) from the previous page.</note> If it is not mine—that is, if it does not belong to my <term>choice</term> <note>original: *arbitrij*. The faculty of making decisions.</note> and will—it must be that I am always able to speak. And even if I were to wish to be unable to speak, I still cannot be unable to speak, unless perhaps I remove that limb by which the function of speaking can be fulfilled. Many things indeed could be mentioned by which, if a person wished, they might take away from themselves the possibility of speaking without removing the limb we use for speech. For example, if something were to happen that took away the voice itself, no one would be able to speak even while the limbs remained. For the human voice is not a limb, though it can certainly be lost if some internal organ is damaged, even if it is not removed. But lest I seem to press him too hard on a word, and it be said to me in a contentious way: "to damage is also to take away," we can indeed bring it about that the mouth is so closed and locked by certain bonds that we are by no means able to open it; nor would it be in our power to open it, even though it was in our power to close it while the health and integrity of the limbs remained.

### HE DISPROVES THE SAYING OF PELAGIUS,
### that whatever is constrained by natural necessity 
### is deprived of choice. Chapter 46.

But what is that to us? Let us see what he weaves from there: "For," he says, "whatever is constrained by natural necessity is deprived of the **choice** and deliberation of the will." And here there is a significant question. For it is utterly absurd for us to say that our desire to be happy does not belong to our will simply because we are entirely unable to *not* want it, due to some good constraint of nature; nor do we dare to say that God possesses justice not by will but by necessity, simply because He cannot will to sin.

<summary>Augustine challenges Pelagius’s logic by arguing that natural necessity (like the desire for happiness or God's inability to sin) does not negate the involvement of the will. He uses physical examples, like the ability to speak or bind one's own mouth, to illustrate the complex relationship between capacity and choice.</summary>

# FROM THE BOOK OF SAINT AURELIUS AUGUSTINE
## ON THE POSSIBILITY AND 
## POWER of seeing, hearing, and smelling. XLVII. <note>Chapter 47 of "On Nature and Grace."</note>

**P**ay attention also to what follows: "This," he <note>Pelagius, the theologian Augustine is debating.</note> says, "is also possible to perceive concerning hearing, smelling, or seeing: that to hear, to smell, and to see is within our <term>power</term> <note>original: *potestas*. The capacity to exercise one's will.</note>; but to be *able* to hear, or smell, or see is not within our power, but consists in natural necessity." Either I do not understand what he says, or he himself does not. For how is the <term>possibility</term> <note>original: *possibilitas*. The latent "ability" Pelagius claims is a gift from God's nature.</note> of seeing not in our power if the necessity of *not* seeing is in our power? For blindness is in our power, by which we may take away from ourselves the very ability to see, if we so wish. 

On the other hand, how is it in our power to see if we wish, when even if the health and nature of our body and our eyes remains intact, we cannot see even if we want to—whether because at night the lights provided from the outside are removed, or if someone shuts us up in some dark place? 

Likewise, if whether we *can* or *cannot* hear is not in our power but in the constraint of nature, while what we *actually* hear or do not hear belongs properly to the <term>will</term> <note>original: *uoluntas*. The faculty of choice.</note>—why does he not notice how many things we hear against our will? These sounds penetrate our senses even when our ears are plugged, such as the nearby screeching of a saw or the grunting of a pig. 

Although the plugging of ears shows that it is not in our power *not* to hear when our ears are open; perhaps such a plugging also acts to take away our sense itself, so that it is in our power even to be *unable* to hear. Regarding what he says about smelling, does he not pay too little attention to the fact that it is not in...

...is in our power to be able to smell, or not to be able; but rather that it is in our power—that is, in our <term>free will</term> <note>original: *libera uoluntate*</note>—to smell or not to smell. ### HOW ERRONEOUS IT IS TO SAY
### that not sinning belongs to us without grace, 
### but the ability not to sin belongs to nature. Chapter XLVIII.

<meta>This page continues Augustine’s rebuttal of the theologian Pelagius in the treatise "On Nature and Grace." The previous page concluded with an analogy about the sense of smell and began to address the "help of God" (grace). This page picks up mid-sentence, critiquing Pelagius’s claim that while the *act* of sinning is ours, the *capacity* to avoid sin is a fixed part of our nature that requires no special grace.</meta>

<header>THE BOOK OF BISHOP AURELIUS AUGUSTINE</header>
<meta>catchword: -ty</meta>

...of our Savior, he [Pelagius] thinks that not sinning is our own doing, but he contends that the *ability* not to sin belongs to our nature. Yet it is so apparent that this nature has been corrupted <note>original: *vitiatam*. Augustine emphasizes that human nature is "vitiated" or damaged by original sin, whereas Pelagius argued it remained basically sound.</note> that it is an even greater fault not to see it.

### ON THE FALSITY OF THAT CONCLUSION: 
### "Not sinning is our own, therefore, we 
### are able to sin and not to sin." XLIX.

"Because not sinning," he says, "is our own doing, we are able both to sin and not to sin." What if someone else were to say: "Because *not* wanting <term>unhappiness</term> <note>original: *infelicitatem*. This refers to a state of misery or the lack of eternal blessedness.</note> is our own doing, we are able both to want it and not to want it"? And yet, we are completely unable to want it. For who could, in any way, wish to be unhappy—even if he wants something else that results in unhappiness following him against his will? 

Furthermore, since it belongs much more to God not to sin, shall we dare to say that He is able both to sin and not to sin? Far be it from us to say that God is able to sin. For He will not be any less <term>omnipotent</term> <note>All-powerful</note> (as the foolish think) simply because He cannot die and "He cannot deny Himself" <note>A reference to 2 Timothy 2:13</note>. What is it, then, that he speaks? By what rules of speech does he try to persuade us of what he refuses to consider?

He adds further and says: "But because *being able* not to sin is not our own, even if we wished not to be able to avoid sin, we cannot *not* be able to avoid sin." He has spoken this in a convoluted and therefore obscure way. But it could be stated more clearly like this: because "being able not to sin" is not our own, whether we want to or not, we *possess* the ability not to sin. For he does not say "whether we want to or not, we do not sin"—for without a doubt we do sin if we want to—but he asserts that whether we want to or not, we possess the <term>possibility</term> <note>original: *possibilitatem*. Pelagius argued that the "possibility" of being sinless is a hard-wired faculty given by God at creation, which cannot be lost.</note> of not sinning...

<summary>Augustine critiques Pelagius's logic by arguing that certain "abilities"—like the ability to avoid unhappiness or God's inability to sin—do not imply a choice to do the opposite. He argues that Pelagius's definition of "natural ability" ignores the reality of human corruption.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Augustine’s line-by-line critique of the theologian Pelagius. <header>ON NATURE AND GRACE.</header>

...possibility, which he says is implanted in our nature. But concerning a man with healthy feet, it can be said reasonably that, whether he wills it or not, he has the capacity for walking. If his feet are broken, however, even if he wills it, he does not have that capacity. The nature of which he speaks is corrupted <note>original: *vitiata*. Augustine uses this term to describe nature as "vitiated" or "spoiled" by original sin, like a physical injury that prevents normal function.</note>. 
<margin>Ecclesiasticus 10</margin> *Why are earth and ashes proud?* <note>original: *Quid superbit terra et cinis?* Augustine cites Sirach 10:9 (often called Ecclesiasticus) to rebuke the pride of those who believe they can be perfect by their own nature.</note> It is corrupted; it begs for a doctor. 
<margin>Psalm 41</margin> *Save me, O Lord,* it cries out. *Heal my soul,* <note>original: *Sana animam meam*. Augustine quotes Psalm 41:4 to show that the human soul recognizes its own "sickness" and need for God's healing grace.</note> it cries out. Why does he block these voices? Why does he, by defending a supposed "present capacity," prevent the patient's "future health"?

### CHAPTER 50: THAT THE WILL ITSELF CAN ACT 
### against those things which are inseparably implanted in 
### nature is proven by the statement of the Apostle.

And see what he adds, by which he thinks he confirms his point: "because," he says, "no will can take away what is proven to be inseparably implanted in nature." 
<margin>Galatians 5</margin> From where, then, comes that voice: *So that you do not do the things that you want*? <note>Galatians 5:17. Augustine uses St. Paul's letters to prove that our will and our nature are often in conflict because of sin.</note> 
<margin>Romans 7</margin> From where also comes that other: *For I do not do the good I want, but I do the evil I hate*? <note>Romans 7:19. This is Augustine's "smoking gun"—St. Paul describing the internal struggle where the "capacity" to do good seems missing despite the desire.</note> Where is that "possibility" <term>possibilitas</term> <note>The technical term used by Pelagius to describe the innate human capacity to live without sin.</note> which is proven to be inseparably implanted in nature? Behold, men do not do the things they want; and the Apostle was certainly dealing with *not sinning*, not with *flying*, because humans were not birds. Behold, a man does not do the good he wants, but he does the evil he does not want; to will is present with him, but to perform the good is not present. Where is the "possibility" which is proven to be inseparably implanted in nature? For whomever he may be personifying—if the Apostle is not saying these things about himself—he is certainly personifying a human being. From this person, human nature itself [is denied] that inseparable capacity of not sinning...

<summary>Augustine uses the writings of St. Paul (Romans and Galatians) to refute the idea that humans have an "inseparable" natural capacity to avoid sin. He argues that human nature is currently "sick" or "broken" and requires a physician (divine grace) rather than a defense of its own strength.</summary>

Now, in order to avoid the resentment <note>original: *invidia*. Now the opinion that was hiding has been expressed! It can no longer be concealed. He attributes the "possibility of not sinning" to the grace of God only because God is the author of that nature, into which he claims this possibility is inseparably implanted. He implies that when a person wills to act, they do so; and because they do not will it, they do not do it. For where there is an "inseparable possibility," no weakness of the will can occur—or rather, there can be no "presence of the will" alongside a "lack of perfection."

<header>THE BOOK OF SAINT AURELIUS AUGUSTINE</header>

He had said above: "I see another law in my members warring against the law of my mind, and leading me captive in the law of sin which is in my members." <note>original: "Video aliam legem in membris meis..." Augustine is quoting Romans 7:23 to demonstrate the internal struggle against sin.</note> Behold what vice the disobedience of the will has inflicted upon human nature. It is wounded, hurt, vexed, and lost; it has need of a true confession, not a false defense. Therefore, let that grace of God be sought—not the kind by which nature is established <note>original: *instituatur*; referring to the grace of creation</note>, but the kind by which it is restored <note>original: *restituatur*; referring to the grace of redemption/healing</note>—the necessity of which he [Pelagius] only denies by his silence. If he had said nothing at all about the grace of God, and had not proposed that question to be solved by himself in order to remove the resentment against him regarding this matter, it might be thought that he indeed held the view that truth holds, but had simply not spoken of it, since one does not have to say everything everywhere. But he proposed the question of grace and answered it with what he held in his heart; the question is now settled—not in the way we would have wished, but in the way we feared he felt.

### exists in substance, and how "flesh" is 
### understood not only for substance (which is good)

<meta>This page continues Augustine’s rebuttal of Pelagius. # ON NATURE AND GRACE.
c 4

What is it that he <note>Pelagius, the British monk whose views on human nature Augustine is opposing.</note> asks: "Who made the spirit of man?" and he answers himself: "Without doubt, God." And again he asks, "Who created the flesh?" and again he responds, "I believe the same, God." He asks a third time: "Is the God who created both good?" He responds: "No one doubts it." He asks once more: "And is both that which a good author created good?" and to this he responds: "It must be confessed." Then he concludes: "If therefore both the spirit is good and the flesh is good, as they were fashioned by a good author, how can it be that two good things can be contrary to one another?"

I omit saying that his whole reasoning would be thrown into confusion if someone were to ask him: "Who made heat and cold?" for he would answer without doubt, "God." I do not ask many things; let him conclude for himself whether these can be called good, or whether they do not appear to be contrary to each other. Here he perhaps says: "These are qualities <term>qualitates: properties or characteristics of a thing, rather than the thing itself</term> of substances, not substances themselves." That is so, it is true; but they are natural qualities, and without doubt pertain to God’s creation. For substances are not said to be contrary to each other through themselves, but through their qualities, just as water and fire are said to be contrary. What if flesh and spirit are like this? This we do not indeed affirm, but we have said this to show that his reasoning was not concluded by a necessary inference. For contraries can also not oppose one another, but can be tempered by one another and produce good health, just as in the body dryness and moisture, cold and heat—from the tempering of all of which a good bodily health consists. <margin>Galatians 5</margin> But because the flesh is contrary to the spirit,

<summary>Augustine challenges Pelagius’s logic that two "good" creations (flesh and spirit) cannot be contrary, using the analogy of heat and cold to show that even things created by God can have opposing qualities.</summary>
