<meta>This page continues Augustine’s rebuttal of Pelagius. Having previously explored the idea that human nature is currently "wounded" by sin, Augustine now scrutinizes a specific logical slip made by Pelagius. Pelagius argued that for a baptized person, the "flesh" (bodily desires) cannot be contrary to the "spirit" because God created both. Augustine uses this logic against him, pointing out that if this were true, it would have to apply to everyone, not just the baptized.</meta>

# THE BOOK OF SAINT AURELIUS AUGUSTINE

That we do not do the things we wish to do is a defect <note>original: *vitium*; Augustine distinguishes between our original, "good" nature and the "defect" introduced by sin.</note>, not our original nature. Let healing grace <term>gratia medicinalis: a central concept for Augustine, where God's grace acts as a medicine to heal the soul's inability to do good.</term> be sought, and let this controversy be ended. For since these two goods <note>the spirit and the flesh</note> were established by a good God, how can they—according to his own reasoning—be contrary to each other even in non-baptized men? Or will he regret having said this, even though he spoke it with some regard for the Christian faith? 

For when he said, "How can it be that the flesh is contrary to anyone *already baptized*?", he implied that the flesh *can* be contrary to those who are not baptized. For why did he add the phrase "already baptized," when he could have simply said, without that addition: "How can it be that the flesh is contrary to anyone?" and then used his own logic to prove it—arguing that because both are good things created by a good God, they cannot be contrary to one another? 

If, therefore, the un-baptized—to whom he admits the flesh is indeed contrary—should press him with his own line of questioning and ask: "Who made the spirit of man?" He will answer, "God." And then they ask: "Who created the flesh?" He responds, "The same God, I believe." They ask a third time: "Is the God who created both good?" and he answers, "There is no doubt of it." And finally, they inquire about the one point remaining: "Is everything that a good author created also good?" This he will have to admit. 

Then they will cut his throat with his own sword <note>A metaphor for using a speaker's own logic to defeat their argument.</note>, insisting upon his own conclusion and saying: "If, then, the spirit is good and the flesh is good, as they were fashioned by a good author, how can it be that two good things are contrary to one another?" To this he might perhaps respond: "Forgive me; I should not have said that the flesh cannot be contrary to anyone *baptized*, as that would be confessing it is contrary to you who are un-baptized; rather, I should have said without any...

<summary>Augustine catches Pelagius in a logical contradiction, arguing that if Pelagius believes the flesh and spirit are inherently compatible because God created them both, he cannot limit this compatibility only to the baptized.</summary>

He moves from discussing the unbaptized to the state of those already baptized, who still experience internal conflict.</meta>

->ON NATURE AND GRACE.<-

Then, against those who argue otherwise, he hears that man crying out, to whom it was answered: *The grace of God through Jesus Christ our Lord.* <note>original: *Gratia dei per Iesum Christum dominum nostrum*; referring to the resolution of the struggle described in Romans 7:25.</note> Moreover, if we can obtain even this much from these people <note>The "people" Augustine refers to are the Pelagians, who believed humans could be good through their own natural willpower.</note>—that the unbaptized should implore the help of the Savior's grace—this is no small victory against that false defense of a nature that is supposedly sufficient for itself and the power of <term>free will</term> <note>original: *liberi arbitrij*; the human capacity to choose. Augustine argues that without grace, this choice is hampered by sin.</note>. For he does not suffice for himself who says: *Wretched man that I am! Who will deliver me?* <note>original: *Infelix ego homo quis me liberabit*; from Romans 7:24.</note> Nor should he be said to have full freedom who still asks to be set free.

## WHETHER THE BAPTIZED DO the good they wish, with no lust of the flesh resisting. Chapter 56.

<margin>Galatians 5</margin>
<margin>Romans 6</margin>
However, let us also look at this: whether those who have been baptized do the good things they want, with no <term>lust</term> <note>original: *concupiscentia*; often translated as "concupiscence," it refers to the disordered desires or "cravings" that remain in human nature even after the guilt of sin is washed away in baptism.</note> of the flesh resisting. But what we should say here, the Apostle himself mentions where, concluding this passage as we have said, he includes that which is contained: *The flesh lusts against the spirit.* <note>original: *Caro concupiscit aduersus spiritum*; from Galatians 5:17.</note> It is necessary to understand this not as being about the physical substance of the flesh, but about the works of that substance. This is what we also say: it was said not regarding the substance of the flesh, but its works, which come from the lust of the flesh—namely, from sin, about which he commanded *that it should not reign in our mortal body to obey its desires.* <note>Augustine is careful to clarify that the body itself is not "evil," but rather the sinful impulses ("works") that operate through it. This refers to Romans 6:12.</note>

## THAT ONE ABSTAINING FROM THE WORK of sin only out of fear of punishment, not out of love for justice, is not yet a stranger to the will to sin. Chapter 57.

<summary>Augustine argues that the need for grace is proven by the human cry for deliverance and explains that even the baptized must struggle against "concupiscence" (sinful desire). He clarifies that the conflict is not with the physical body itself, but with the sinful impulses that remain within it.</summary>
<keywords>Grace, Nature, Free Will, Baptism, Concupiscence, Original Sin, Romans 7, Galatians 5, Pelagianism</keywords>

--- Page 88 ---
<meta>This page continues Augustine’s rebuttal of Pelagius. In the previous page, Augustine discussed the struggle of the unbaptized. Here, he moves to the baptized, arguing that even after the sacrament, believers still experience an internal conflict between their physical desires and their spiritual will, requiring the ongoing help of grace rather than just the "letter" of the law.</meta>

->BOOK OF BISHOP AURELIUS AUGUSTINE<-

He <note>Referring to Pelagius, the theologian Augustine is arguing against.</note> does not even notice that it was said to those who were already baptized: *The flesh lusts against the spirit, and the spirit against the flesh, so that you do not do the things that you want.* <note>original: *Caro concupiscit aduersus spiritum, & spiritus aduersus carnem, ut non ea quæ uultis faciatis*; from Galatians 5:17.</note> And so that he would not make them lazy in this very struggle, and lest he should seem to have given a license for sinning through this statement, he adds: *But if you are led by the spirit, you are no longer under the law.* <note>original: *Quod si spiritu ducimini, non adhuc estis sub lege*; from Galatians 5:18.</note> For a person is "under the law" when they refrain from the act of sin only because they fear the punishment the law threatens, rather than because they love justice. Such a person is not yet free, nor is their will truly estranged from the desire to sin. In their very will they are guilty, for they would prefer, if it were possible, for that which they fear to not exist, so that they might freely do what they secretly desire.

Therefore, he says, *if you are led by the spirit, you are no longer under the law*—certainly meaning the law <margin>Romans 5.</margin> which strikes fear but does not grant <term>charity</term> <note>original: *charitas*; specifically divine, selfless love. Augustine argues that the Law can tell you what is right, but only "charity" (given by the Spirit) makes you *want* to do it.</note>. This charity of God has been *poured out into our hearts,* not through the "letter" of the law, but *through the Holy Spirit who has been given to us.* <note>original: *charitas dei diffusa est in cordibus nostris... per spiritum sanctum*; from Romans 5:5.</note> This is the law of liberty, not of slavery, because it is the law of love, not of fear. Regarding this, <margin>James 1.</margin> the Apostle James also says: *But he who has looked into the perfect law of liberty.* <note>original: *Qui autem perspexerit in legem perfectam libertatis*; from James 1:25.</note> This is why that man <note>The Apostle Paul, in his persona as the "struggling believer."</note> was no longer terrified by God’s law like a slave; instead, he *delighted in it according to the inner man.* <margin>Romans 7.</margin> <note>original: *condelectabatur ei secundum interiorem hominem*; from Romans 7:22.</note> Nevertheless, he still sees *another law in his members* warring against the law of his mind. So it is here: *If you are led by the spirit,* he says, *you are no longer under the law.* To the extent that anyone is led by the spirit, they are not under the law; for to the extent that they delight in the law of God, they are not under the fear of the law. This is because fear brings <term>torment</term> <note>original: *tormentum*; referring to the internal punishment of a guilty conscience or fear of hell.</note>, not delight.

### THAT JUST AS FOR THE MEMBERS

<vocab>Augustine, spirit, flesh, law, charity, liberty, grace, sin</vocab>

<summary>Augustine explains that baptized Christians still struggle with "the flesh" and argues that true righteousness comes from a spiritual love (charity) that transcends the mere fear of legal punishment.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Augustine’s rebuttal of Pelagius in his treatise *De Natura et Gratia*. While the previous page focused on how the "law" only creates fear without the help of the Spirit, this page addresses the necessity of prayer in the struggle against sin and the devil. Augustine transitions from discussing the internal conflict of the flesh to the external opposition of the devil, arguing that human will alone is insufficient to win this battle.</meta>

->ON NATURE AND GRACE<-

...give thanks for those who have been healed, so we ought to pray for those yet to be healed, and consider how the opposing devil must be resisted.

### Chapter LVIII.

Accordingly, if we judge rightly, just as we must give thanks for the members of the body already healed, so we must pray for those still to be healed, so that we may enjoy the most absolute health—to which nothing can be added—the perfect sweetness of God, and full freedom. For we do not deny that human nature can be without sin <note>Augustine is making a subtle point here: he agrees with his opponent Pelagius that human nature *could* theoretically be sinless, but he argues this is only possible through God's active help, not through human effort alone.</note>, nor should we in any way deny that it can be perfected (since we do not deny its perfection is possible), but we say it happens by the **grace** of God through **Jesus Christ** our Lord; it is by His help that the soul becomes just and blessed, the same One from whom the soul receives its very existence. Therefore, what some people claim is an objection to his <note>Referring to Pelagius.</note> view is easily refuted: "The devil opposes us." Indeed, to this objection, we respond with the same words that he himself used.

<margin>James 4</margin>
"Let us resist him, and he will flee." The blessed apostle says: *Resist the devil, and he will flee from you.* <note>original: *Resistite diabolo, & fugiet à uobis*; from James 4:7.</note> From this, we should consider what harm he can do to those from whom he flees, or what power he should be understood to have, seeing as he can only prevail against those who do not resist. These are also my words, for nothing more true can be said. But this is the difference between us and those people <note>The Pelagians, who emphasized human free will over divine grace.</note>: that even when we resist the devil, we not only do not deny that God's help must be requested, but we actually preach it. These men, however, attribute such great power to the **will** that they take away **prayer** from the life of piety. For indeed, so that we might resist the devil and he might flee from us, we say in prayer: *Lead us not into temptation.*

<margin>Matthew 6</margin>
Therefore we have also been admonished like soldiers exhorted by their commander <note>Augustine often uses military metaphors to describe the Christian life as a struggle or "warfare" against sin.</note>, and...

<summary>Augustine argues that while human nature can be perfected, this only happens through divine grace and prayer, not human will alone. He refutes the idea that resisting the devil is a matter of simple effort, insisting that we must pray "Lead us not into temptation" to succeed.</summary>

He <note>Augustine refers here to his opponent, Pelagius.</note> did not even notice that it was said to those who were already baptized: *The flesh lusts against the spirit, and the spirit against the flesh, so that you do not do the things that you want.* <note>original: "Caro concupiſcit aduerſus ſpiritū, & ſpiritus ad uerſus carnem, ut nō ea quæ uultis faciatis"; from Galatians 5:17.</note> And lest he should make them idle in this very struggle, and lest he should seem to have given an excuse for sinning through this statement, he adds: *But if you are led by the spirit, you are no longer under the law.* <note>Galatians 5:18.</note> For one is "under the law" who refrains from a sinful act out of fear of the punishment which the law threatens, rather than out of a love for justice. Such a person is not yet free, nor has he truly moved away from the desire to sin. In his very will he is guilty; for he would prefer, if it were possible, that the thing he fears did not exist, so that he might freely do what he secretly desires. Therefore, he says, "if you are led by the spirit, you are no longer under the law"—certainly not under that law which instills fear but does not grant <term>charity</term> <note>original: "charitatem"; here meaning the selfless, divine love of God.</note>. This <term>charity</term> of God is poured into our hearts, not through the letter of the law, but through the Holy Spirit who has been given to us. This is the law of liberty, not of slavery, because it is the law of love, not of fear. Yet he still sees another law in his members fighting against the law of his mind. So also here: "If you are led by the spirit," he says, "you are no longer under the law." To the extent that anyone is led by the spirit, he is not under the law; because insofar as he delights in the law of God, he is not under the fear of the law—for fear brings torment, not delight.

Therefore, if we think correctly, just as we must give thanks for the members of the body that are healthy <note>The OCR reads "fanatis," but context and the Latin root indicate "sanatis" (healed/healthy).</note>, so we must pray for those needing to be healed, so that we may enjoy a most complete health to which nothing can be added, a perfect sweetness of God, and full freedom. <header>BOOK BY SAINT AURELIUS AUGUSTINE</header>

*...saying: Watch and pray, so that you do not enter into temptation.* <note>original: "Vigilate & orate, ne intretis in tentationem" — a quote from the Gospel of Matthew 26:41 or Mark 14:38.</note>

### THAT THE POSSIBILITY OF NOT
### sinning relates to the grace of God. Chapter 59.

<margin>Mark 14.</margin>
*Now, as for his argument against those who say: "And who would not wish to be without sin, if this were placed within a person's power?" He argues correctly that by this very statement they confess it is not impossible, because many, or even all, desire this. But let him confess the source of this possibility, and then there is peace. For it is the very **grace** <term>grace: the divine help or unmerited favor of God, which Augustine argues is necessary for every good act.</term> of God through Jesus Christ our Lord by which he <note>Referring to Pelagius.</note> has never been willing to say that we are absolutely helped when we pray, so that we may not sin. But if perhaps he feels this way secretly, let him forgive those who suspect otherwise. For he himself causes this suspicion, since while he suffers so much ill-will regarding this matter, he wants to hold this view but does not want to confess or openly profess it. What great thing would it have been for him to say this, especially since he took it upon himself to treat and reveal this as an objection raised from the persona of his adversaries? Why did he wish to defend only nature there? And why did he assert that man was created in such a way that he could choose not to sin, if he did not wish to sin? And by this fact—that man was created so—he defined that this "possibility" belongs to the grace of God: a possibility by which, if he does not want to sin, he does not sin. But did he not want to say anything about how this very nature is either healed by the grace of God through Jesus Christ our Lord because it has been corrupted, or helped because it is not sufficient for itself?*

### WHETHER THERE HAS BEEN IN THIS
### world, or is, or can be anyone living so righteously
### that they have no sin at all. Chapter 60.

<note>This heading introduces a classic question of the Pelagian controversy: is total human perfection possible in this life?</note>

<summary>Augustine argues that while Pelagius admits sinless living is "possible," he wrongly attributes this to human nature alone rather than the continuous, healing grace of God. He challenges Pelagius to clarify whether he believes humans need divine help to actually use their "possibility" for good.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Augustine’s response to Pelagius. The previous page discussed whether the "possibility" of not sinning is a natural gift or requires constant divine help. Here, Augustine addresses whether any human has actually achieved a sinless life and concludes that regardless of the answer, such a feat is impossible without the grace of Christ.</meta>

<header>ON NATURE AND GRACE</header>

Whether there has been, is, or could be anyone in this world who lived so justly that they had no sin at all: this may be a matter of debate among true and pious Christians. However, that such a state is certainly possible after this life, no one doubts without being foolish. But I do not even wish to argue about this present life. For although it seems to me that we should not understand what is written in any other way than this: 
<margin>Psalm 142</margin>
*No one living shall be justified in your sight,* <note>original: "Non iustificabitur in conspectu tuo omnis vivens" — from Psalm 143:2 in modern numbering. To be "justified" <term>justified: to be made righteous or right in the eyes of God</term> means to be free of the guilt of sin.</note> and other similar passages; yet, if it could be shown either that these testimonies can be better understood, or that a perfect and full justice—to which absolutely nothing needs to be added—existed in someone yesterday while they lived in this body, exists today, or will exist tomorrow; nevertheless, there are far more who do not doubt that until the last day of this life it is necessary for them to say truthfully: 
<margin>Matthew 6</margin>
*Forgive us our debts, as we also forgive our debtors.* <note>The central petition for forgiveness from the Lord's Prayer.</note> They trust that their hope in Christ and in His promises is true, certain, and firm. Yet, if anyone denies that people reach the fullest perfection, or even make any progress at all in true and pious justice, by any means other than the helping **grace** of the Savior, the crucified Christ, and the gift of His Spirit—I do not know if such people can rightly be counted among the number of Christians of any sort.

### THAT A HUMAN BEING CAN CONQUER
#### sin, because sinning is not a matter of necessity, but of
#### one's own will. Chapter 61.

And through this, he <note>Referring to Pelagius, whom Augustine is quoting or summarizing.</note> also addresses those testimonies which he took not from the canonical scriptures, but from certain works of Catholic commentators <term>commentators: early Christian writers and theologians, often called Church Fathers, who interpreted the Bible</term>, wishing to answer those who said he was the only one saying such things...

<summary>Augustine argues that while Christians might debate if a sinless life is possible on earth, it is undeniable that any level of righteousness can only be achieved through the grace of the crucified Christ. He asserts that denying the necessity of this divine help is a rejection of the Christian faith itself.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Augustine’s response to Pelagius in "On Nature and Grace." Augustine is addressing specific quotations Pelagius used from other authors—and even from Augustine himself—to argue that a sinless life is possible through human will.</meta>

<header>THE BOOK OF SAINT AURELIUS AUGUSTINE</header>
<meta>catchword: Gospel</meta>

...to defend, are so neutral: that they are neither against our opinion, nor against his. In these he even wanted to insert something from my own books, considering me as someone who seemed worthy to be mentioned alongside them. <note>Augustine is referring to his opponent Pelagius, who had cited Augustine's own earlier writings to try and prove they agreed on human nature.</note> Therefore I ought not to be ungrateful, and with a more familiar affection, I do not wish him to err, who has bestowed this honor upon me. As for the first things he set down—because I did not read there the name of the person who said them (either because the author did not write them, or because the manuscript you sent perhaps lacked it due to some error)—what need is there to investigate? Especially since I am free regarding the writings of any men of this sort (because I owe unhesitating consent to the **canonical** <term>canonical: referring to the books of the Bible officially recognized as divinely inspired and authoritative</term> [Scriptures] alone), I am somewhat moved by what he cited from the writings of that person whose name I did not find there. It was necessary for the master and teacher of virtues to become most like a human being, so that by overcoming sin, he might teach humanity that it is able to overcome sin. How this was said, the author of this opinion may see how he can explain it, provided however that we do not doubt in the least that Christ had no sin in himself 
<margin>Romans 8</margin> 
which he would [have to] overcome, he who was born *in the likeness of sinful flesh*, <note>original: "in similitudine carnis peccati" — a reference to Romans 8:3, expressing that Christ had a real human body but without the stain of sin.</note> not in sinful flesh. He set down another [quote] from the same author in this way: "And again, that with the desires of the flesh subdued, he might teach that to sin is not of necessity, but of one's own will." I take "desires of the flesh" here to mean either illicit **cravings** <term>cravings: often translated as 'concupiscence,' referring to desires that pull a person away from God’s will</term> or natural needs, such as hunger, thirst, the refreshing of weariness, and if there is anything of this kind. For through these, some fall into faults although the desires themselves are blameless; this was absent from that Savior, even if this was in him because of the likeness of sinful flesh...

<summary>Augustine addresses Pelagius’s use of various citations (including Augustine's own) regarding Christ's human nature. He argues that while Christ took on human form and its natural needs, he did not possess the "sinful flesh" or the internal necessity to sin that characterizes fallen humanity.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Augustine’s rebuttal of Pelagius’s use of historical authorities. <header>ON NATURE AND GRACE.</header>
<sig>f</sig>

Let us see, with the Gospel as our witness.

## HE EXPLAINS THE WORDS OF HILARY,
### *which Pelagius cites in his own writings in his own favor.*
### Chapter LXII.

But as for the blessed Hilary, <note>Hilary of Poitiers (c. 310–367 AD), a prominent bishop and Doctor of the Church.</note> whose words he has placed here: *“For it is only those perfected in the spirit and changed by immortality who shall see that which is prepared only for the pure in heart—that which is immortal in God,”* <note>original: "Non enī nisi spiritu pfecti & imortalitate mutati qd’ solis mūdis corde dispositiū est, hoc qd’ in deo imortale cernemus"</note> I truly do not know what he said that goes against what we are saying, or how it helps that man <note>Referring to Pelagius.</note>—unless it is because he testified that it is possible for a human being to have a pure heart? Who denies this? But this happens by the grace of God through Jesus Christ our Lord, not by the freedom of choice alone.

Likewise, regarding what he mentions Hilary to have said: *“What books of scripture had Job read, that he should abstain from every evil thing? For he worshipped God with a mind alone, unmixed with vices; and to worship God is the proper duty of justice.”* <note>original: "Quas Iob literas legerat, ut abstineret se ab omni re maligna, quia deū sola mente uitijs non admixta uenerabatur, deū aut colere iustitiæ propriū officiū est."</note> Did Hilary say what Job had done? Was it not that he had made progress in this world, or that he had either acted or made progress through the grace of the Savior, whom he even prophesied? For even a person who has sin—but does not allow it to reign within himself—abstains from every evil thing. To such a person, a blameworthy thought may creep in, but he does not permit it to reach the completion of the deed.

But it is one thing to have sin, and another thing not to obey its desires. It is one thing to fulfill what is commanded:
<margin>Exodus 20.</margin>
*“You shall not covet,”* <note>original: "Nō cōcupisces." This refers to the tenth commandment.</note> and it is another thing, through a certain effort of abstinence, at least to do what is likewise written:
<margin>Ecclesiasticus 18.</margin>
<note>The OCR abbreviation "Eccle." here refers to the Book of Sirach (Ecclesiasticus), specifically 18:30.</note>
*“Do not follow after your lusts.”* <note>original: "Post cōcupiscentias tuas nō eas."</note> Yet one should know that none of these things can be done rightly without the grace of the Savior. To practice justice, therefore, in the true worship of God, is to struggle against the internal evils of **concupiscence** <term>concupiscence: the internal inclination or "tugging" toward sin that Augustine argues remains in the human heart after the Fall, requiring constant grace to resist.</term>...

<summary>Augustine interprets the writings of Saint Hilary, arguing that while figures like Job were indeed righteous, their purity was a gift of divine grace rather than a product of independent human will. He distinguishes between the absolute command not to covet and the practical struggle of the faithful to resist their sinful desires.</summary>
<keywords>Saint Hilary of Poitiers, Job, Grace, Free Will, Concupiscence, Exodus, Sirach, Pelagianism, Purity of Heart</keywords>

--- Page 96 ---
<meta>This page continues the text from the previous page where Augustine was discussing the testimony of Hilary of Poitiers. The opening word "tione" is the conclusion of a word from the previous page (likely "abstinentia" or "continence"), referring to the struggle against sin through the help of the Savior.</meta>

<header>BOOK OF SAINT AURELIUS AUGUSTINE</header>

...to fight by means of <note>The text likely refers to continence or abstinence.</note> [abstinence]. To be perfected, however, is to have no adversary at all. For he who fights is still in danger and is sometimes struck, even if he is not knocked down. But he who has no adversary rejoices in full peace. And he is most truly called "without sin" in whom no sin dwells—not he who, through the abstinence from evil works, says: *“Now it is no longer I who work it, but the sin that dwells in me.”* <margin>Romans 7</margin> For even Job himself is not silent about his sins; and certainly it rightfully pleases this friend of yours <note>Referring to Pelagius, whom Augustine is refuting.</note> that humility should by no means be categorized as a form of falsehood. <note>Augustine is arguing that when holy men confess sin, they are being honest, not just performing "humility" by lying about their perfection.</note> Therefore, that which Job confesses, because he is a true worshiper of God, he undoubtedly confesses truthfully.

And Hilary himself, <margin>Hilary on Psalm 118</margin> when he was explaining the passage of the Psalm where it is written: *“You have despised all those who depart from your judgments,”* said: *“For if God despised all sinners, he would certainly despise everyone, because no one is without sin. But he despises those who depart from him, whom they call apostates.”* <note>original: "Si enim deus peccatores sperneret, omnes utiq; sperneret, quia sine peccato nemo est. Sed spernit discedētes à se, quos apostatas uocant."</note> Do you see how he did not say that no one *had been* without sin—as if speaking of the past—but that no one *is* without sin? Concerning this, as I said, I do not contend; for if someone does not yield to John the Apostle—who himself said not "If we say we *had* no sin," but *“If we say we have no sin”*—how is he going to yield to Bishop Hilary? <margin>1 John 1</margin> I cry out for the grace of Christ, without which no one is justified, as if the free will of nature were sufficient. Nay, He Himself cries out for it; let us yield to Him when He says: <margin>John 15</margin> *“Without me you can do nothing.”*

## WHAT AMBROSE THINKS
### *concerning the power of not sinning, and how it is from God*

<summary>Augustine uses the writings of Saint Hilary and the Apostle John to prove that no living person is entirely without sin. ### Chapter 63

Saint Ambrose, in truth, in that very passage which this man <note>Pelagius, the opponent Augustine is addressing.</note> mentions, resisted those who say that a human being cannot be without sin in this life. For he took the opportunity to say these things regarding Zacharias and Elizabeth, <margin>Luke 1</margin> who are recorded in the Gospel to have walked in all the justifications <note>original: "iustificationibus"; often translated as "ordinances" or "commandments."</note> of the law without blame. Yet does he therefore deny that this is accomplished by the grace of God through Jesus Christ our Lord? It is beyond doubt that the righteous lived by this same faith even before His passion, for it is He who provides the Holy Spirit—given to us now—through whom charity is poured into our hearts, by which alone any who are righteous are made righteous. The aforementioned Bishop <note>Saint Ambrose, who was the Bishop of Milan.</note> also warns that this Spirit must be sought through prayers. Thus, the will, when not divinely aided, is not sufficient for him, as he says in his own hymn: *“And He grants to earnest prayers the obtaining of the Holy Spirit.”* <note>original: "Votisq; præstat sedulis sanctum mereri spiritum." This is a line from an Ambrosian hymn, emphasizing that the Spirit is a gift following prayer.</note>

<margin>Ambrose</margin> I shall also mention something from this same work of Saint Ambrose from which this man <note>Pelagius</note> quoted what he thought should be quoted. *“It seemed good to me,”* he said. <note>Ambrose is here beginning his Commentary on the Gospel of Luke.</note> It is possible that it did not seem good only to him, as he declares it seemed to him. For it did not seem so by human will alone, but as it pleased Him who, as he says, *“Christ speaks in me,”* who works so that what is good might also seem good to us. For He is the one who has mercy and who calls. And therefore, he who follows Christ, when asked why he wished to be a Christian, can answer: *“It seemed good to me.”* When he says this, he does not deny that it seemed good to God. For it is by God...

Who would not understand why this man <note>Pelagius</note> did not add these words? This is indeed the goal in this present age: that the holy Church may reach that most spotless purity which all the saints desire. In the world to come, she will lead a most pure life in divine eternity, with no wicked persons mixed in with her, and no law of sin <note>A reference to the biblical concept of the internal struggle against temptation (Romans 7:23).</note> within her resisting the law of the mind. ### WHAT JOHN, BISHOP OF CONSTANTINOPLE,
### and a certain Sixtus thought concerning
### freedom of choice and grace.

->Chapter LXIV.<-

*Likewise, John, the bishop of Constantinople <note>original: "Ioannes Constantinopolitanus"; referring to Saint John Chrysostom (c. 347–407 AD), a Church Father famous for his preaching.</note>, whose opinion he [Pelagius] has cited, says that sin is not a substance, but a malignant act. Who denies this? And because it is not natural, for that reason the law was given against it, and because it descends from the freedom of choice—who also denies this? But the issue here is not regarding human nature as it exists in this life, but regarding the grace of God by which it is healed through the Physician, Christ. This nature would not need a physician if it were healthy; yet it is defended by that man [Pelagius] as if it were healthy, or as if its freedom of choice <note>original: "arbitrii libertate"; often translated as free will.</note> were sufficient for it to be able to avoid sin. Likewise, what Christian is ignorant of what he records the most blessed Sixtus <note>original: "Sixtum"; Pelagius attributed a collection of proverbs known as the "Sentences of Sextus" to the Roman martyr-Pope Sixtus II to give his arguments more weight. Augustine remains skeptical of the interpretation but addresses the quote nonetheless.</note>, bishop of the Roman church and martyr of the Lord, to have said: that "God permitted human beings the freedom of their own choice so that, living purely and without sin, they might become like God"? But it belongs to that very choice to hear the one who calls and to believe, and to ask for the help of not sinning from the one in whom he believes. For certainly when he says, "they might become like God," they are to become like God through the charity <note>original: "charitatem"; the divine love poured into the soul.</note> of God <margin>Romans 5</margin> which is poured out in our hearts—not by the possibility of nature, nor by the free will that is in us, but through the Holy Spirit who has been given to us. And what the same martyr says: "A holy temple is..."*

Likewise, regarding what he himself <note>Augustine refers here to the author of the text Pelagius is citing, traditionally known as the Sentences of Sextus.</note> said: "A chaste man and one without sin has received power from God to be a son of God," he certainly gave warning. For when anyone has been made so chaste and without sin—though where and when that is perfected in him is a significant question, but one rightly debated among the pious, among whom it is agreed that it can happen—nonetheless, it cannot happen without the mediator between God and men, the man <margin>1 Timothy 2</margin> Christ Jesus. Yet, as I had begun to say, Sixtus wisely warned that when anyone has become such a person, and through this is rightly counted among the sons of God, it should not be thought to have been of his own power, which he instead received through grace from God; for he did not possess it in a nature already corrupted and depraved <note>original: "vitiata atq; deprauata"; Augustine’s technical terms for human nature after the Fall.</note>, <margin>John 1</margin> just as we read in the Gospel: "But as many as received Him, He gave them power to become the sons of God." They certainly were not this by nature, nor would they be at all, unless by receiving Him they had accepted such power through His grace. This is the power claimed for itself by the strength of charity <note>original: "charitatis"; the highest form of Christian love, which Augustine views as a gift of the Spirit.</note>, <margin>Romans 5</margin> which is not in us except through the Holy Spirit who has been given to us.

### Chapter LXVII

<margin>Book 3, chapter 18</margin>
Let us also come to the matter. Likewise, says Bishop Augustine in his books on **free choice of the will**: "Whatever that cause of the will may be, if it cannot be resisted, one yields to it without sin; but if it can be resisted, let it not be yielded to, and there will be no sin. Does it perhaps deceive the unwary? Then let him take care not to be deceived. Or is the deception so great that it cannot be avoided at all? If so, then there are no sins. For who sins in that which can in no way be avoided? But sin is committed; therefore, it can be avoided." <note>This is a quote from Augustine's earlier work, written against the Manichaeans, which Pelagius used to argue that humans can avoid sin by their own power.</note> I acknowledge these; they are my words. But let him <note>Referring to Pelagius.</note> also deign to acknowledge all those things said above. For the subject here is the **grace of God**, which helps us as a <term>medicine: original Latin "medicina," a favorite metaphor of Augustine for how grace heals the soul's defects</term> through the **Mediator**, not the impossibility of justice. Therefore, that cause—whatever it may be—can be resisted; it clearly can. For in this we ask for <term>help: original Latin "adiutorium," referring to divine assistance</term>, 
<margin>Matthew 6</margin>
saying: "Lead us not into temptation." We would not ask for such help if we believed that it could in no way be resisted. It is possible to avoid sin, but only with the help of Him who cannot be deceived. For even this itself pertains to avoiding sin, if we truly say: "Forgive us our debts, as we..." <note>The text cuts off mid-sentence from the Lord's Prayer.</note>

<meta>This page continues Augustine’s response to Pelagius. <header>ON NATURE AND GRACE.</header>

...as we forgive our debtors. For in two ways the evil of disease is guarded against in the body: both so that it does not happen, and so that if it has happened, it is quickly healed. So that it does not happen, let us take care by saying: *Lead us not into temptation.* So that it may be quickly healed, let us take care by saying: *Forgive us our debts.* Whether, therefore, the danger is imminent or is already present, it can be guarded against. 

But so that my opinion on this matter may be sufficiently clear from those same books—not only to that man <note>Referring to Pelagius.</note> but also to those who have not read my books on **free choice** which he read, and perhaps read this book without having read those—I ought to recall what I wrote there. If he were to agree with this and include it in his own writings, no controversy would remain between us on this matter. 

For immediately after my words which he cited, I myself added what might occur as an objection and discussed it as much as I could, saying: "And yet, even things done through ignorance are condemned and judged as needing correction, as we read in the divine authorities <note>Augustine refers to the Bible as the "divine authorities."</note>." And from here, having applied examples, I also spoke concerning human weakness, saying: "There are also things done by necessity that are to be condemned, where a person wants to do right and cannot." For from where else are those voices: 
<margin>Romans 7</margin>
"For I do not do the good I want, but I do the evil I hate, this I do." <note>original: "Non enim quod uolo facio bonum, sed quod odi malum, hoc ago." Augustine is quoting the Apostle Paul to show that even with a will to do good, human nature is often too "sick" to carry it out without help.</note> And I cited other testimonies of divine eloquence in that same opinion. 

But all these, I say, are the voices of men coming from that condemnation of death. For if this is not a punishment of man, but his natural state, then these are not sins. Then a little later I said: "It remains, therefore, that this is a just punishment from the condemnation of man..."

--- Page 104 ---
<meta>This page continues Augustine’s explanation of the "punishment of sin." He is quoting from his own earlier work, *On Free Choice of the Will*, to show that he has always maintained that while original human nature was free, the current fallen state is characterized by ignorance and difficulty, which can only be overcome through God's grace.</meta>

# SAINT AURELIUS AUGUSTINE'S BOOK

...should come. Nor is it surprising that, whether through ignorance, one does not have **free choice of the will** <term>liberum arbitrium: the capacity to choose between different possible courses of action unimpeded</term> to choose what to do rightly, or that, with carnal habit resisting—which has grown naturally (in a certain sense) through the violence of mortal succession <note>This refers to the "inheritance" of a fallen nature from one generation to the next.</note>—one sees what ought to be done and wishes to do it, but is unable to fulfill it. 

For that is the most just punishment of sin: that a person should lose that which they were unwilling to use well, even though they could have used it without any difficulty if they had wanted to. This means that whoever knowingly fails to do right loses the knowledge of what is right; and whoever was unwilling to do right when they were able loses the power to do so when they finally wish to. For truly, these two things are the penalties for every sinning soul: **ignorance** and **difficulty**. From ignorance comes the dishonor of error; from difficulty comes the affliction of torment. 

But to approve false things as true so that one errs against their will, and to be unable to refrain from lustful deeds because of the resistance and torture of the pain of the carnal bond—this is not the nature of man as he was first **instituted** <note>Augustine distinguishes between "instituted nature" (how God originally created humanity) and "condemned nature" (the state of humanity after the Fall).</note>, but the punishment of man as he is now **condemned**. 

However, when we speak of the free will to do right, we are indeed speaking of that state in which man was originally made. On this point, to those men who set forth a "just complaint" about this ignorance and difficulty being passed down and poured into the offspring of the **first man** <note>Referring to Adam and the doctrine of original sin.</note>, the following response is given: To them, I say, the brief answer is that they should be quiet and cease to murmur against God. For they might perhaps complain rightly if no human victor over error and lust existed. But since He is everywhere present—He who in many ways, through the creation that serves Him as Lord, calls the one who has turned away, teaches the one who believes, and comforts the one who hopes...

<summary>Augustine explains that humanity's current struggle with sin is a "just punishment" for the Fall, resulting in a loss of the power to do good. He argues that while ignorance and difficulty are now part of the human condition, God continues to offer a way back through grace and divine calling.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the argument from Augustine’s work "On Nature and Grace," where he quotes his own previous writings to demonstrate a consistent stance against Pelagianism. He is distinguishing between the original, uncorrupted nature of humanity and the fallen state we inherit, emphasizing that exhortation to do good is useless without the aid of divine grace.</meta>

->**ON NATURE AND GRACE.**<-

<margin>Ephesians 2</margin>
...should encourage the diligent, assist the striving, and hear the one who pleads. You are not held at fault for being ignorant against your will, but for neglecting to seek out what you do not know. Nor are you blamed because you do not bind up your own wounded limbs, but because you despise the one who wishes to heal you. Thus, I have exhorted as much as I could toward living rightly; and I have not **emptied the grace of God** <note>original: "gratiam dei non evacuavi" — a reference to Galatians 2:21; Augustine argues that his opponents make grace "empty" or "void" by claiming nature alone is sufficient.</note>, without which human nature, now darkened and corrupted, cannot be enlightened and healed. Regarding this matter, the whole question turns on those people <note>Referring to the Pelagians, who emphasized human effort over divine grace.</note>: lest we frustrate the **grace of God** <term>gratia: the free and unmerited favor of God, as manifested in the salvation of sinners</term> which is in Christ Jesus our Lord through a perverse defense of nature. Concerning this **nature** <term>natura: in this context, the inherent character and capacities of a human being</term>, I also said a little later: we also speak of "nature" in different ways. In one sense, we speak properly of human nature in which man was first created blameless in his kind; in another sense, we speak of that nature in which we are born from the punishment of that condemned man <note>Adam</note>, both ignorant and subject to the flesh. According to this latter sense, the **Apostle** <note>St. Paul</note> says: "For we also were by nature children of wrath, even as the rest."

# HOW CHRISTIAN MEN
## Are to be instructed.
### Chapter LXVIII.

If, therefore, we wish to rouse cold and sluggish souls to live rightly through Christian exhortations, let us first exhort them to the **faith** by which they might become Christians and be brought under the name of Him without whom they cannot be saved. If, however, they are already Christians and neglect to live rightly, let them be struck with terrors and raised up by the praise of rewards; so...

--- Page 106 ---
<meta>This page continues the text from the previous page, completing a sentence where Augustine argues for the necessity of both human effort and divine assistance. He is specifically addressing how teachers and preachers should guide the faithful.</meta>

<header>THE BOOKS OF SAINT AURELIUS AUGUSTINE.</header>

...so that we may remember to exhort them not only to good actions, but also to pious prayers, and to instruct them with this health of doctrine. Thus, they may give thanks when they have begun to live well, if they have performed something without difficulty; and where they feel some difficulty, they should persist in obtaining ease from the Lord through the most faithful and persevering prayers, and through prompt works of mercy. Moreover, as for those progressing in this way, I do not care overmuch where or when they are perfected in full **righteousness** <term>iustitia: here meaning a state of being right with God, often translated as "justice" or "righteousness"</term>. However, wherever and whenever they are perfected, I confirm that they can only be perfected by the **grace of God** through Jesus Christ our Lord. Indeed, when they clearly know that they have no sin, <margin>1 John 1</margin> let them not say they have no sin, lest the truth not be in them. Just as the truth is not in those who, though they have sin, say they do not have it.

## HOW LOVE RENDERS
### the commandments of God easy. Chapter 69.

But the commandments are very good, if we use them lawfully. Indeed, by that very fact where it is most firmly believed that God is just and good, and could not have commanded impossible things, we are reminded both what to do in easy matters and what to ask for in difficult ones. <margin>Matthew 11; 1 John 5</margin> For all things become easy to **love** <term>charitas: often translated as "charity," this refers to the highest form of Christian love for God and neighbor</term>, for which alone Christ's **burden is light** <note>original: "sarcina levis est" — a reference to Matthew 11:30, "For my yoke is easy and my burden is light."</note>; or perhaps love itself is the only burden that is light, according to what is said: *And his commandments are not heavy.* Thus, whoever finds them heavy should consider that it could not have been divinely said, "they are not heavy," unless there can be a disposition of the heart to which they are not heavy; and he should seek that by which...

<summary>Augustine argues that while human effort and prayer are necessary, perfection and the ability to fulfill God's commands are only possible through divine grace and the presence of love (charity) in the heart.</summary>
<keywords>Augustine, grace, righteousness, sin, love, charity, commandments, prayer, 1 John, Matthew 11</keywords>

--- Page 107 ---
<meta>This page continues Augustine’s argument from the previous page, where he was discussing how divine help allows a person to fulfill the commandments. He is specifically addressing the idea that while God's law might seem impossible or "hard," it is made "light" through the gift of love (charity).</meta>

<header>ON NATURE AND GRACE.</header>

...is not deprived of help, so that he may fulfill what is commanded. <margin>Deuteronomy 30</margin> <margin>Romans 10</margin> And what is said to Israel in Deuteronomy, if it is understood piously, holily, and spiritually, signifies this same thing. For when the Apostle had commemorated this testimony: <margin>Deuteronomy 30</margin> *The word is near you, in your mouth and in your heart,* <note>original: "prope est uerbum in ore tuo, & in corde tuo"</note> which he here expresses as being "in your hands" <note>Augustine is interpreting the "doing" of the commandment as having it in one's hands.</note>. For in the heart are the spiritual hands; this is, he says, *the word of faith which we preach.* Therefore, if anyone has turned to the Lord his God with all his heart and with all his soul, as is commanded there, he should not find the commandment of God burdensome. For how is it burdensome when it is a commandment of **love** <term>dilectio: a term for love, often used interchangeably with charity to describe the bond between God and humanity</term>? For either a person does not love, and therefore it is burdensome; or he loves, and it cannot be burdensome. But he loves if, as Israel is there admonished, he has turned to the Lord his God with all his heart and all his soul. <margin>John 13</margin> "A new commandment," He says, "I give to you, that you love one another." <margin>Romans 13</margin> And he who loves his neighbor has fulfilled the law; for **charity** <term>charitas: the highest form of Christian love, directed toward God and neighbor</term> is the fulfillment of the law. According to this, that other saying was also spoken: "If they had walked in good paths, they would surely have found the paths of **righteousness** <term>iustitia: the state of being right in God's eyes; often translated as justice</term> to be light." <margin>Psalm 16</margin> How, then, is it said: "Because of the words of your lips, I have kept hard ways," <note>original: "Propter uerba labiorum tuorũ ego custodiui uias duras." Augustine refers to Psalm 17:4 (16:4 in the Vulgate).</note> unless both statements are true?

## THAT THE WAYS OF RIGHTEOUSNESS
## are hard for fear, but light for love.
### Chapter 70.

They are hard for fear, but light for love. Therefore, **charity** that has begun is begun **righteousness**; **charity** that has progressed is progressed **righteousness**; great **charity** is great **righteousness**...

<summary>Augustine reconciles the apparent contradiction of God's law being both "hard" and "light" by explaining that love (charity) transforms the burden of the law into a joy. He argues that the degree of one's righteousness is directly proportional to the degree of love they possess.</summary>
<keywords>Augustine, Charity, Righteousness, Law, Deuteronomy, Romans, Grace, Love vs Fear</keywords>

<meta>This page follows the title page of the 1524 Nuremberg edition. Having introduced the work's title, the text now moves into the "Argumentum" or introductory summary, which Augustine himself wrote later in his life as part of his "Retractations" (a book where he reviewed and clarified his life's work).</meta>

# OF SAINT <note>original: "Divi" (Divine/Saintly)</note> AURELIUS AUGUSTINE
## Bishop of Hippo, on the book concerning the spirit and
## the letter, from the second book of his Retractations
### SUMMARY <note>original: "Argumentum." This is Augustine’s own retrospective introduction to the work, explaining why he wrote it.</note>

<image-desc>Decorative drop cap 'A' at the beginning of the text, featuring a stylized letter with floral patterns and a small figure or grotesque mask integrated into the lower curve of the 'A'.</image-desc>

To the man <note>Augustine is referring to Flavius Marcellinus, a high-ranking Roman official and friend.</note> to whom I had written three books, the title of which is *On the Merits and Remission of Sins*, where the baptism of infants is also diligently debated, he wrote back to me that he was troubled by what I had said: that it is possible for a person to be without sin if their will is not lacking and divine aid helps them, although no one has ever been, is, or ever will be of such perfect righteousness in this life. For he asked how I could say something is possible when an example of it is lacking. Because of this inquiry of his, I wrote the book entitled *On the Spirit and the Letter*, treating at length the apostolic <note>Referring to the Apostle Paul.</note> sentence where he says:
<margin>2 Corinthians 3</margin> "The letter kills, but the spirit gives life."
<margin>Romans 4</margin> In this book, as much as God helped me, I argued sharply against the enemies of God’s grace <note>The Pelagians, who argued that human beings could achieve goodness through their own will without needing special divine grace.</note>, by which the ungodly is justified. However, when I was dealing with the observances of the Jews—who abstain from certain foods according to the old law—I said: "the ceremonies of certain foods." This name <term>ceremony</term> <note>Augustine is explaining his use of the word "caerimonia."</note> is not in the common use of Holy Scripture; nevertheless, it seemed appropriate to me because the word "ceremony" is named from "lacking" <note>original: "carendo." Augustine suggests a folk etymology here, linking ceremony to the Latin word for "to be without."</note>, as if I held "cerimonies" in my memory because those observing them lack or go without those things from which they abstain. But if there is another origin for this name that is foreign to our religion, I have not spoken according to that one, but according to this one which I mentioned above.

<summary>In this preface from his later "Retractations," Augustine explains that he wrote this treatise to answer a friend's question about how human perfection is theoretically possible through grace, even if never actually achieved in practice. He also defends his specific use of the word "ceremony" to describe Jewish dietary laws.</summary>

<keywords>Saint Augustine, Grace, Free Will, Marcellinus, Justification, Law and Spirit, Etymology, Pelagianism, 2 Corinthians 3, Romans 4</keywords>

How it should be understood: "The law is not made for the righteous" <note>original: "Iusto lex non est posita," a reference to 1 Timothy 1:9.</note>, which is nevertheless good if one uses it lawfully. chapter 10.

That the soul shows <term>piety<note>In this context, "piety" (pietatem) refers to the proper devotion and gratitude a creature owes to their Creator, acknowledging God as the source of all good.</note></term> to God when it is not ungrateful, nor puffed up as if it possessed its freely-given goodness by its own power. chapter 11.

That just as for the Gentiles—who knew God through His creation yet did not glorify Him as God—this knowledge was of no benefit, so too for those who know through the Law how one ought to live, that knowledge alone does not justify them. chapter 12.

How the law of deeds and the law of faith differ, and which is more perfect; he finally concludes that we are justified not by the law of works, but by faith; not by the letter, but by the spirit; not by the merits of deeds, but by <term>gratuitous grace<note>Grace that is given freely by God's favor, rather than earned through human merit.</note></term>. chapter 13.

That not only is the Scripture regarding ritual observances called the "letter that kills," but also the tablets of the <term>Decalogue<note>The Ten Commandments.</note></term> and the precepts which—by increasing <term>concupiscence<note>original: "concupiscentia." That while the works of charity were written on tablets of stone to terrify the "wisdom of the flesh," it was the law of works and the "letter that kills"; but when that same charity is poured into the hearts of believers, it is the law of faith, giving life to the one who loves. chapter 17.

Why he calls the ancient law the "ministry of death" <note>original: "ministrationem mortis," a reference to 2 Corinthians 3:7, describing the Law's role in condemning sin without providing the power to overcome it.</note>.

<meta>The previous page concluded the Table of Contents for this work. This page begins the actual text of St. Augustine’s treatise "On the Spirit and the Letter," addressing his friend Marcellinus and responding to questions raised by Augustine’s previous writings on sin and human perfection.</meta>

ON THE SPIRIT AND THE LETTER

# BOOK BY THE HOLY AURELIUS AUGUSTINE, BISHOP OF HIPPO, ON THE SPIRIT AND THE LETTER, ADDRESSED TO MARCELLINUS.

## THAT MANY THINGS ARE POSSIBLE which are known to have never been done. Chapter 1.

<image-desc>Decorative historiated initial 'L' depicting a muscular male figure, possibly a deity or titan, entwined within the ornate flourishes of the letter.</image-desc>

Having read the short works <note>original: "opusculis," referring to Augustine's books "On the Merits and Forgiveness of Sins."</note> which I recently composed for you, my dearest son Marcellinus, concerning the baptism of infants and the perfection of human righteousness—noting that no one in this life seems to have either attained it or to be about to attain it, except for the one <term>Mediator<note>Refers to Jesus Christ, seen as the only bridge between God and humanity.</note></term>, who suffered human trials in the <term>likeness of sinful flesh<note>original: "similitudine carnis peccati," a Pauline concept meaning Christ took human form but remained without sin.</note></term> without any sin at all—you wrote back to say you were troubled. You were moved because, in the second of those two books, I stated it is possible for a man to be without sin if his will is not lacking and is assisted by divine power; yet, I also said that except for the one in whom all shall be made alive <note>Refers to Christ, citing 1 Corinthians 15:22.</note>, there has been and will be no one in whom this perfection exists while living here. It seems absurd to you to say that something is possible when no example of it exists. However, I believe you do not doubt that it has never happened that a <margin>Matthew 19</margin> camel passed through the eye of a needle, and yet He who said this also said it was possible for God. You may also <margin>Matthew 26</margin> read that twelve legions of angels could have fought for Christ so that he would not suffer, yet this did not happen. You may read that it was possible for the nations to be exterminated all at once from the land which was being given to the children of Israel; yet God [did] it little... <note>The text cuts off mid-sentence; Augustine is referencing how God drove out the Canaanites gradually rather than all at once.</note>

<summary>Augustine begins his response to Marcellinus, arguing that just because something (like human perfection) hasn't happened in history, it does not mean it is impossible for God to achieve.</summary>
<keywords>Augustine, Marcellinus, sinlessness, perfection, Mediator, divine grace, possibility vs. actuality, biblical examples</keywords>

--- Page 118 ---
<meta>This page continues the opening argument of Chapter 1, where Augustine explains that the absence of a real-world example does not disprove the possibility of an event. It then transitions into Chapter 2, addressing the relationship between human effort and divine grace.</meta>

<header>ST. AURELIUS AUGUSTINE</header>

ned to have wished to be broadened; and six hundred <note>original: "sexcenta." Augustine uses the number 600 as a common Latin idiom for a large, indefinite number, much like "a thousand" is used in English today.</note> other things could occur to us, which we might admit could have happened or could still happen, yet for which we cannot provide examples of them having actually been done. Therefore, we ought not to deny that it is possible for a man to be without sin just because there is no man—except for Him who is not only man but also God—in whom we can demonstrate that this perfection has been achieved.

## THAT ALTHOUGH IT PERTAINS TO MAN
## to act well, it is nonetheless both a divine gift, and
## work, and that Scripture testifies that no man lives
## without sin.

### Chapter II.

Here you might perhaps respond that those things I mentioned, which were not done but could have been done, are divine works; whereas, for a man to be without sin pertains to the work of the man himself, and that this is the best work by which full and perfect, and in every respect absolute <term>righteousness<note>original: "iustitia." This term encompasses both legal "justice" and the moral "rectitude" or "holiness" of a person.</note></term> is achieved. Thus, you might argue that it should not be believed that no one has been, is, or will be in this life who has fulfilled this work, if it can indeed be fulfilled by man. But you ought to consider that although it pertains to man to do this, this too is a divine gift; and therefore, you should not doubt that it is a divine work. "For it is God," says the Apostle <margin>Philippians 2:13</margin>, "who works in you, both to will and to work according to His good will." Consequently, we need not be overly troubled by those who say that a man lives or has lived without any sin at all, but we must press them to show, if they can, that it is so. For if anyone can show that the testimonies of the Scriptures—by which I believe it is established that no man living here, using his <term>free will<note>original: "libero arbitrio." The human capacity to choose between different courses of action.</note></term>, is found without sin—are to be understood differently than they sound, such as: "Do not enter into judgment with your servant, for in your sight no living person shall be justified" <margin>Psalm 143:2</margin> <note>Cited as Psalm 142 following the numbering of the Latin Vulgate used by Augustine.</note>, and other such passages, and...

<meta>catchword: demon</meta>

<summary>Augustine argues that while human sinlessness is a theoretical possibility through God's power, it is never actually realized in human experience. He asserts that even human moral progress is a divine gift ("work of God") rather than a purely independent human achievement.</summary>

<keywords>St. Augustine, sin, divine grace, free will, righteousness, justification, Philippians, Psalms, perfection</keywords>

--- Page 119 ---
<meta>This page continues Chapter 2 of Augustine's "On the Spirit and the Letter," where he discusses whether a human being can live a sinless life and the necessity of divine help to achieve righteousness.</meta>

<header>ON THE SPIRIT AND THE LETTER, BOOK 1.</header>
<sig>B</sig>

If someone were to demonstrate that someone—or many—had lived here without any sin, not only would he <note>Referring to the person considering the argument, likely Augustine himself.</note> not be at all opposed to it, but he would even congratulate him greatly, as he is not driven by any small stings of envy. For even if no one is, or was, or will be—which I am more inclined to believe—perfected in such purity, yet if it is defended and believed that it is, was, or will be so, as far as I can judge, it is not much of an error, nor is it a dangerous one, since one is misled by a certain kindness of spirit <note>original: "benevolentia." This suggests a charitable or well-meaning outlook on human potential.</note>. This is provided, however, that whoever believes this does not imagine himself to be such a person, unless he truly and clearly perceives himself to be so.

But we must most sharply and vehemently resist those who think that, without the <term>help of God<note>original: "adiutorio dei." Augustine is arguing against the Pelagian view—a 5th-century theological position—which held that human effort alone is sufficient for salvation without needing constant divine grace.</note></term>, through the power of the human will alone, they can either achieve <term>righteousness<note>original: "iustitiam." Moral rectitude or holiness.</note></term> or make progress toward attaining it. Yet, when they begin to be pressed on how they can presume to assert that this happens without divine aid, they restrain themselves and do not dare to utter such words, for they see that such a claim is impious and intolerable.

Instead, they say that these things do not happen without divine aid because God created man with <term>free will<note>original: "libero voluntatis arbitrio." The human capacity to choose between different courses of action.</note></term>, and by giving commandments, He Himself teaches how man ought to live. They argue He certainly helps in this way: by teaching, He removes ignorance, so that man knows in his actions what he should avoid and what he should seek. Thus, through the free will naturally implanted within him, walking the path that has been shown, and by living temperately, justly, and piously, he may merit reaching that same blessed and eternal life.

### THE MANNER IN WHICH THE HUMAN
### will is divinely aided to achieve
### righteousness. Chapter III.

<summary>Augustine argues that while believing in human perfection is a harmless error of "benevolence," one must fiercely oppose the idea that righteousness can be achieved through free will alone without the continuous help of God.</summary>
<keywords>divine aid, free will, righteousness, sinlessness, Pelagianism, grace, moral perfection</keywords>

--- Page 120 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument from Chapter 2 of Augustine’s "On the Spirit and the Letter," shifting from the possibility of sinlessness to the mechanism of divine grace. Augustine is responding to Pelagian ideas by emphasizing that the "letter" of the law alone is insufficient for true righteousness.</meta>

<header>ST. AURELIUS AUGUSTINE</header>

But we say that the human will is divinely assisted toward performing <term>righteousness<note>original: "iustitiam." In this context, it refers to a state of being right with God and living according to His moral law.</note></term> in such a way that—beyond the fact that man was created with <term>free choice of the will<note>original: "libero arbitrio uoluntatis." The capacity to make decisions, which Augustine argues is weakened by sin.</note></term>, and beyond the doctrine by which he is commanded how he ought to live—he also receives the Holy Spirit. Through this Spirit, there is created in his mind a delight in and a love for that highest and unchangeable good, which is God, <margin>2 Corinthians 5</margin> even now while one walks by faith and not yet by sight. Thus, with this <term>pledge<note>original: "arra." A legal term referring to a down payment or earnest money that guarantees the fulfillment of a contract; here, a foretaste of heaven.</note></term> of a free gift having been given, he may burn to cling to his Creator and be inflamed to approach the participation in that true light, so that he may find his well-being in the one from whom he has his very existence. For <term>free will<note>original: "liberū arbitriū."</note></term> is capable of nothing except sinning if the way of truth remains hidden. And even when that which ought to be done and aimed for begins to be clear, unless it also provides delight and is loved, it is not acted upon, <margin>Romans 5</margin> it is not undertaken, and a good life is not lived. Moreover, so that it may be loved, "the charity of God is poured out in our hearts," not through the free choice that arises from ourselves, but "through the Holy Spirit who has been given to us."

### HOW IT OUGHT TO BE UNDERSTOOD
->Regarding what is read: "The letter kills, but the spirit gives life." Chapter 4.<-

Indeed, that doctrine by which we have received the commandment to live rightly is the "killing letter" unless the "life-giving spirit" is present. For we must not understand what we <margin>2 Corinthians 3</margin> read—"The letter kills, but the spirit gives life"—only in that one way: that we should not take something written figuratively (where the literal meaning is absurd) as the "letter" sounds, but should instead look to the internal meaning that it signifies...

<summary>Augustine argues that human free will is insufficient for righteousness without the Holy Spirit's gift of delight and love for God. He introduces a discussion on 2 Corinthians 3:6, explaining that the Law ("the letter") only condemns unless the Spirit provides the internal desire to obey it.</summary>

<keywords>Holy Spirit, free will, righteousness, divine grace, Law and Spirit, Augustine, 2 Corinthians, Romans, charity, illumination</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Augustine’s exploration of the Apostle Paul’s statement that "the letter kills." It picks up from the previous page's discussion on how the Holy Spirit must provide the delight in righteousness that the law alone cannot provide.</meta>

# ON THE SPIRIT AND THE LETTER, BOOK 1
B 2

<margin>Romans 8</margin>
...so that we may nourish the person with spiritual understanding. For to be <term>carnally minded<note>original: "sapere secundum carnem." To set one's mind on the things of the flesh or physical nature rather than the divine.</note></term> is death, but to be spiritually minded is life and peace. This is like a person who might take many things written in the *Song of Songs* in a carnal way; instead of leading to the fruit of a luminous love, it leads to the stirrings of lustful desire. Therefore, we should not understand what the Apostle says—"the letter kills, but the spirit gives life"—only in that way. <margin>2 Corinthians 3</margin> We must also, and especially, understand it in the sense he expresses most clearly in another place: <margin>Romans 7</margin> "I would not have known <term>concupiscence<note>original: "concupiscentiam." A strong, disordered longing or "coveting." Augustine sees this as the root of all sin.</note></term> if the law had not said: *You shall not covet*." And a little later he says: "Taking the opportunity, sin deceived me through the commandment, and through it, it killed me." Behold, that is what is meant by "the letter kills." And certainly, it is not being said in some figurative way—as if it shouldn't be taken according to the literal sound of the words—when it says *You shall not covet*. Rather, it is a most perfect and wholesome precept; if anyone fulfilled it, they would have no sin at all. Indeed, the Apostle chose this general principle because it encompasses all things, as if this were the voice of the law prohibiting all sin when it says: *You shall not covet*. For no sin is committed except by coveting; therefore, the law that commands this is good and praiseworthy. But where the Holy Spirit does not assist—by inspiring a good desire in place of an evil one, that is, by <margin>Romans 5</margin> pouring out charity in our hearts—that law, however good it may be, actually increases the evil desire by prohibiting it. It is just like a rush of water: if the flow does not stop, it becomes more violent when an obstacle is placed in its way. Once it has overcome the weight of that obstacle, it crashes down with a greater volume and rolls even more violently down the slope. For I do not know how it is, but the very thing we desire becomes more pleasant precisely while it is forbidden.

<summary>Augustine argues that the biblical phrase "the letter kills" refers to how the moral law, such as the command against coveting, can actually increase human desire for sin if the Holy Spirit is not present to provide a superior love for the good. He uses the vivid metaphor of a dammed river to describe how forbidden desires grow more violent when blocked by mere rules.</summary>

<header>OF SAINT AURELIUS AUGUSTINE.</header>

And this is how sin deceives through the commandment, and kills through it, when <term>transgression<note>original: "praeuaricatio." In this context, it refers specifically to the intentional breaking of a known law.</note></term> is also added—which does not exist where there is no law. But let us consider the whole passage of the Apostle's epistle, if you please, and let us examine it as the Lord assists.

# CONCERNING AUTHORITY,
## Where "the letter kills" etc., the "letter" is understood to represent the Law. Chapter 5.

<margin>2 Corinthians 3</margin>
For I wish to demonstrate, if I can, that what the Apostle says—*the letter kills, but the spirit gives life*—was not said about figurative expressions (though it may be appropriately applied there as well), but rather more directly about the Law, which forbids what is evil. When I have shown this, it will certainly appear more clearly that living rightly is a divine gift. This is true not only because God gave man <term>free will<note>original: "liberum arbitrium." Augustine argues that while humans have the capacity to choose, grace is necessary to choose the good.</note></term>, without which one lives neither poorly nor well, nor only because <margin>Romans 5</margin> He gave a commandment to teach how one must live, but because through the Holy Spirit He pours <term>charity<note>original: "charitatem." Divine love; the inward grace that allows a person to fulfill the requirements of the law out of love rather than fear.</note></term> into the hearts of those whom He foreknew so that He might predestine them; He predestined them so that He might call them; He called them so that He might justify them; and He justified them so that He might glorify them. 

When this becomes clear, you will see, I think, that it is pointless to say that only those things which are the works of God are possible without an existing example—<margin>Matthew 19</margin> such as the passage of a camel through the eye of a needle which we mentioned, and whatever other things are impossible for us but easy for God. Therefore, human righteousness should not be excluded from these things, as if it should pertain only to the work of man and not of God. For if its perfection is possible in this life, there is no reason why it should be believed to be impossible simply because there is no prior example. This has been said in vain [enough]...

<meta>catchword: elus</meta>

<summary>Augustine argues that the "letter that kills" refers to the Law when it is obeyed out of fear rather than through the grace and love provided by the Holy Spirit. He asserts that living a righteous life is a divine gift involving predestination and justification, not merely an act of human will.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Augustine’s argument that even "human justice"—the ability to live a righteous life—is a gift of God's grace rather than a product of independent human effort. It bridges the discussion from the previous page regarding the "letter of the law" into a specific examination of Paul's Epistle to the Romans.</meta>

->ON THE SPIRIT AND THE LETTER, BOOK 1.<-

...it will be clear, since it has been shown that even human justice itself <note>original: "humanam iustitiam." In Augustine's theology, this refers to the righteousness a human possesses, which he argues is actually a result of God working within the person's will.</note> must be attributed to the operation of God—though it does not happen without the human's will. Therefore, we cannot deny that the perfection of this justice is possible even in this life, because all things are possible for God—whether those He performs by His own will alone, or those He has determined can be done by Himself with the cooperating wills of His creatures. And because of this, whatever of these things He does not do is indeed without example in terms of finished works; yet with God, and within His power, there remains a cause by which it *could* be done, and in His wisdom a reason why it *was not* done. Even if this cause is hidden from man, let man not forget that he is a man; nor should he attribute foolishness to God simply because he does not fully grasp His wisdom.

Attend, therefore, to the Apostle as he explains to the Romans, and sufficiently demonstrates what he wrote to the Corinthians:
<margin>2 Corinthians 3</margin>
*The letter kills, but the spirit gives life.* This should be understood primarily in the way we described above: because the letter of the law, which teaches that one must not sin, kills if the life-giving spirit is absent. For the law makes one know sin rather than avoid it, and therefore sin is increased rather than diminished, because <term>transgression<note>original: "praeuaricatio." To Augustine, this is the specific sin of knowingly breaking a written command, which carries more guilt than sinning in ignorance.</note></term> of the law is added to an already evil <term>concupiscence<note>original: "concupiscentiae." This refers to disordered desire or the inclination toward sin.</note></term>.
