<meta>This page continues Augustine’s scriptural analysis of the Epistle to the Romans. He is transitioning from a general discussion of "the letter" to a specific defense of Paul’s theology regarding the purpose of the Law and the necessity of Grace.</meta>

<header>OF SAINT AURELIUS AUGUSTINE</header>

<margin>Romans 5</margin> [Paul], most clearly pointing to Christ in contrast to Adam, says: *But the law entered so that transgression might abound; but where transgression abounded, grace overabounded, so that just as sin reigned unto death, so also grace might reign through righteousness unto eternal life through Jesus Christ our Lord.* <note>original: "Adam, hunc Christum apertissimé insinuans..." Augustine is explaining Paul's "Adam-Christ" typology, where Christ is seen as the "New Adam" who restores what was lost in the Fall.</note>

<margin>Romans 6</margin> Then, posing a question to himself, he says: *What then shall we say? Shall we continue in sin, so that grace may abound? Far from it!* <note>original: "Absit." This is a strong Greek and Latin negation often rendered as "God forbid" or "By no means."</note> For he saw that what he had said could be taken perversely by the immature <note>original: "à pueris peruerse." Augustine notes that those with a "childish" or undeveloped understanding of theology might misinterpret Paul’s logic.</note>—namely, that *the law entered so that transgression might abound, but where transgression abounded, grace overabounded*—as if he had said that sin is beneficial for the sake of an abundance of grace. Disproving this, he replied, *Far from it*, and added: *We who have died to sin, how shall we live in it?* That is, since grace has brought it about that we should die to sin, what else would we be doing if we live in it, except being ungrateful to grace?

For a person who praises the benefit of medicine does not say that diseases and wounds are helpful—the very things from which the medicine heals him. Rather, the more highly the medicine is praised, the more the wounds and diseases are condemned and dreaded, since it is from those very things that the medicine (which is so highly praised) liberates us. In the same way, the praise and preaching of grace is the condemnation and rejection of transgressions. For the foulness of man’s sickness had to be demonstrated to him; against his <term>iniquity<note>original: "iniquitatem." In this context, it refers to a fundamental crookedness or lack of justice in the human soul that the Law alone cannot fix.</note> even the holy and good commandment was of no use. Indeed, his iniquity was increased rather than diminished, since *the law entered so that transgression might abound*. In this way, being convicted and brought to shame, man might see that he requires not only a teacher to show him the way, but also God as a helper, by whom his steps...

<summary>Augustine uses Paul’s Epistle to the Romans to argue that the Law was intended to reveal the severity of human sin, making the need for God's "medicine" (grace) clear. He refutes the idea that grace encourages sin, comparing grace to a cure that makes one hate the disease all the more.</summary>
<keywords>St. Augustine, Grace, Law, Romans 5, Romans 6, Sin as disease, Medicine metaphor, Justification, Iniquity</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the text from the previous leaf, where Augustine used a medical analogy to explain that a patient praises the medicine, not the disease. Here, he applies that "medicine" to the mystical effects of baptism and the resurrection as described in the Apostle Paul's Letter to the Romans.</meta>

### ON THE SPIRIT AND THE LETTER, BOOK [I]
->B 4<-

[the more their steps] are directed, so that no iniquity may rule over them, and by fleeing to the help of divine mercy they are healed, so that where sin abounded, grace might superabound—not by the merit of sins, but by the help of the one coming to the rescue. <note>original: "superabundet gratia." Augustine is quoting Romans 5:20 to argue that grace is a gift for the sick, not a reward for the healthy.</note> Consequently, the Apostle shows that this same medicine was mystically demonstrated in the passion and resurrection of Christ, saying: *Do you not know that all of us who were baptized into Christ Jesus were baptized into his death?* <note>original: "eandem medicinam." Augustine frequently uses medical language to describe the healing of the soul through Christ.</note> *We were therefore buried with him through baptism into death, so that just as Christ was raised from the dead through the glory of the Father, we too might walk in newness of life. For if we have been planted together in the likeness of his death, we shall also be in the likeness of his resurrection. Knowing this, that our old man was crucified together with him,* <note>original: "uetus homo noster." A Pauline term referring to the unregenerate self or the sinful nature inherited from Adam.</note> *so that the body of sin might be done away with, that we should no longer serve sin. For he who has died has been justified from sin.* <note>original: "iustificatus est à peccato." In this context, to be "justified" means to be set free or legally cleared from the debt and power of sin.</note> *But if we have died with Christ, we believe that we shall also live with him, knowing that Christ, rising from the dead, dies no more, and death will no longer have dominion over him. For in that he died to sin, he died once; but in that he lives, he lives to God. So you also, consider yourselves dead to sin, but alive to God in Christ Jesus.* Indeed, it is clear enough that through the mystery of the Lord’s death and resurrection, the setting <note>original: "occasum," as in a sunset.</note> of our old life and the rising of the new are symbolized, showing both the abolition of iniquity and the renewal of righteousness. From where, then, does so great a benefit come to man, if not through faith in Jesus Christ?

<summary>Augustine uses Romans 6 to explain how baptism represents a mystical death to the "old self" and a resurrection to a new life of grace. He concludes that this transformation is a divine benefit accessible only through faith.</summary>

# SAINT AURELIUS AUGUSTINE

## THAT NO ONE PERFORMS

### the works of justice, except insofar as they have received from that source and from that light. Chapter VII.

<margin>Psalm 35</margin>
This holy thought preserves the children of men who hope in the protection of God's wings, so that they might be drunk from the richness of his house, and be given to drink from the torrent of his delight: since with him is the source of life, and in his light we shall see light, he who extends his mercy to those who know him, and his justice to those who are upright in heart. <note>original: "fons uitae." Augustine is quoting Psalm 35:8–10 (Psalm 36 in modern Bibles). He uses the imagery of drinking and light to show that human goodness is borrowed from God, not self-generated.</note> For he extends his mercy not because they already know him, but so that they *may* know him; nor because they are upright in heart, but so that they *may* become upright in heart, he extends his justice, by which he
<margin>Romans 4</margin>
justifies the <term>ungodly</term>. <note>original: "iustificat impiū." To "justify the ungodly" is a key Pauline concept (Romans 4:5) meaning that God declares a sinner righteous through grace before they have performed any good works.</note> This thought does not bring about pride, which is a vice that arises when someone takes charge of themselves and makes themselves their own "head" or authority for living. By this movement, one withdraws from that source of life—from whose draught alone <term>justice</term> is drunk (that is, a good life)—and from that unchangeable light. <note>original: "iustitia bibitur." Augustine defines "justice" here not as legal fairness, but as the internal quality of a "good life" given by God.</note> By participating in that light, the rational soul is in a certain way set aflame, so that it may itself become a light that is made and created,
<margin>John 5</margin>
just as John was a lamp, burning and shining. <note>original: "Iohānes lucerna ardens & lucens." A reference to John the Baptist from John 5:35.</note>
<margin>John 1</margin>
Yet he recognized from where his light came, saying: "We have all received from his fullness." <note>original: "de plenitudine eius accepimus." From John 1:16. Augustine contrasts the "Lamp" (John) with the "True Light" (Christ).</note> From whose fullness, if not from His, in comparison to whom John was not the light? For "that was the true light which enlightens every person coming into this world."
<margin>Psalm 35</margin>
Therefore, when he had said in the same Psalm, "Extend your mercy to those who know you, and your justice to those who are upright in heart," he followed by saying: "Let not the foot of pride come against me, and [the hand of the sinner not move me...]" <note>The text cuts off here at "ma," likely for "manus" (hand).</note>

<summary>Augustine explains that righteousness and spiritual knowledge are gifts of divine grace rather than rewards for human effort. Using the metaphor of light, he distinguishes between God as the source of light and humans (like John the Baptist) who merely reflect that light when they are humble.</summary>

## Chapter VIII.

<margin>Romans 2</margin>
From there are those things which I mentioned above: from there he <note>Referring to the Apostle Paul.</note> rebukes the Jew, and says that he is called a Jew by name, yet in no way fulfills what he professes. "If, however, you," he says, "are called a Jew, and rest in the law, and boast in God, and know His will, and approve of the things that differ, being instructed in the law; and you are confident that you yourself are a guide of the blind, a light to those who are in darkness, an instructor of the foolish, a teacher of infants, having the form of knowledge and truth in the law. You, therefore, who teach another, do you not teach yourself? You who preach against stealing, do you steal? You who say one should not commit adultery, do you commit adultery? You who detest idols, do you commit <term>sacrilege</term>? <note>In this context, sacrilege refers to the violation or profanation of anything held sacred, specifically how hypocritical behavior profanes the Law itself.</note> 
<margin>Isaiah 52</margin>
You who boast in the law, through transgression of the law, do you dishonor God? For the name of God is blasphemed among the Gentiles because of you, as it is written. <note>original: "Nomen enim dei per uos blasphematur in gentibus." Augustine quotes Paul, who in turn cites Isaiah 52:5.</note> Circumcision indeed is profitable if you keep the law; but if you are a transgressor of the law, your circumcision has become uncircumcision. If, therefore, the uncircumcised person keeps the requirements of the law, will not his uncircumcision be counted as circumcision?"

<note>original vocabulary: Augustine, Epistle to the Romans, Jew, law, circumcision, Isaiah</note>

<summary>In this passage, Augustine quotes the Apostle Paul to illustrate that mere ethnic identity or legalistic observance does not grant righteousness. He emphasizes that if one's actions contradict the Law they profess, they dishonor God and render their religious rituals meaningless.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Augustine’s exegesis of the Apostle Paul's letter to the Romans. Having previously discussed those who rely on the name "Jew" without following the Law's intent, Augustine now moves to the definition of a "true" Jew and the necessity of internal transformation over external ritual.</meta>

# ON THE SPIRIT AND THE LETTER, BOOK [I]

And will not that which is uncircumcision by nature, if it fulfills the law, judge you, who through the letter and circumcision are a transgressor of the law? For he is not a Jew who is one outwardly; neither is that circumcision which is outward in the flesh. But he is a Jew who is one inwardly; and **circumcision of the heart** <note>In Christian theology, this refers to a spiritual purification or the "cutting away" of sinful desires, contrasting with the physical ritual of the Old Testament.</note> is in the spirit, not in the letter; whose praise is not from men, but from God. Here he revealed in what way he had said, "you boast in God." For certainly if such a Jew truly boasted in God in the manner that grace requires—which is given freely and not by the merits of works—his praise would be from God, not from men. But instead, they boasted in God as if they alone had deserved to receive His law, <margin>Psalm 147</margin> according to that voice of the Psalm where it is said: *He has not dealt so with any nation, and his judgments he has not made known to them.* <note>original: "Non fecit sic ulli genti, & iudicia sua non manifestavit eis." Augustine uses this to highlight the Jewish people's historical sense of unique privilege in receiving God's commands.</note> Yet they thought they were fulfilling this law of God by their own righteousness, when they were rather its transgressors. Because of this, the law worked wrath for them as sin abounded—sin committed by those who had knowledge of the law. For whoever did what the law commanded without the helping spirit of grace did it out of fear of punishment, not out of love for righteousness. Because of this, that which appeared as a work before men was not present in the will before God; rather, they were held guilty for the fact that God knew they would have preferred, if possible, to commit sin with impunity. Moreover, he calls "circumcision of the heart" a pure will—namely, one free from all unlawful <term>concupiscence</term> <note>A technical term referring to strong, often disordered desires or lusts that incline a person toward sin.</note>—which is not achieved by the **letter** teaching and threatening, but by the **Spirit** helping and healing. Therefore, the praise of such people is not from men, but from God, who through His grace provides...

<summary>Augustine interprets Romans 2, arguing that true religious identity is internal (of the heart) rather than external (of the flesh). He asserts that obeying the Law out of fear of punishment is insufficient; true righteousness requires the "Spirit" to heal the will and instill a love for goodness.</summary>

<keywords>circumcision of the heart, grace, legalism, righteousness, fear vs love, original sin, Romans 2, Psalm 147, concupiscence</keywords>

--- Page 130 ---
<meta>This page continues Augustine’s argument from the previous page, where he critiqued those who claim to follow the Law but do so only out of fear. He now focuses on the distinction between external ritual and internal transformation, specifically addressing how the Law reveals the reality of sin within the human heart.</meta>

<margin>Psalm 33</margin> <note>original: "Pfal. 33." In modern bibles, this is Psalm 34:2: "My soul shall make her boast in the Lord."</note>
<margin>Psalm 21</margin> <note>original: "Pfal. 21." In modern bibles, this is Psalm 22:25: "My praise shall be of thee in the great congregation."</note>
...from which he may be praised, of whom it is said: *In the Lord my soul shall be praised,* and to whom it is said: *With you is my praise;* not like those who wish to praise God because they are humans, but themselves because they are righteous <note>Augustine is critiquing the pride of those who take credit for their own moral goodness instead of attributing it to God's grace.</note>. "But we praise God," they say, "as the author of our justification, in that He gave the law, by the looking-upon of which we might know how we ought to live." Yet they do not hear what they read:
<margin>Romans 3</margin>
*Because no flesh shall be justified by the law in the sight of God.* For it may happen in the sight of men, but not before Him who is the inspector of the heart itself and of the innermost will. Wherein observe also: even if a man who fears the law acts differently <note>i.e., performs a good deed outwardly</note>, consider what he would nevertheless prefer to do if it were permitted. And lest anyone should think that the Apostle said here that no one is justified by that law which contains many figurative precepts in the ancient sacraments—from which also comes that circumcision of the flesh, which infants were commanded to receive on the eighth day—

<margin>Romans 7</margin>
*I did not know sin except through the law. For I did not know concupiscence,* <note>original: "concupiscentia." This term refers to disordered desire or "coveting," which Augustine identifies as the root of sinful actions.</note> *unless the law said: You shall not covet.* For what else is "through the law comes the knowledge of sin"?

<meta>This page continues Augustine’s exploration of Romans 3, specifically finishing the sentence from the previous page regarding the Apostle Paul’s view on the Law and justification. It transitions into a deep dive into the concept of "the righteousness of God" (iustitia dei).</meta>

<header>BOOK ON THE SPIRIT AND THE LETTER</header>

...that the Apostle said <note>This completes the sentence from the previous page: "...he immediately added that the Apostle said..."</note> that because no one will be justified by the law, the law only shows what ought to be done and what ought to be avoided, so that the will may fulfill what the law has shown, and thus man may be justified—not through the command of the law, but through free will.

<margin>Romans 3</margin> But O man, pay attention to what follows: *But now,* he says, *the righteousness of God has been manifested without the law, being witnessed by the law and the prophets.* Does it sound too faint to those who are deaf, when he says: *The righteousness of God has been manifested?*

<margin>Romans 10</margin> Those who wish to establish their own righteousness are ignorant of this, and they do not wish to be subject to it. *The righteousness of God has been manifested,* he says; he did not say, <margin>Romans 4</margin> the "righteousness of man," or the "righteousness of one's own will," but the *righteousness of God* <note>original: "iustitia dei." Augustine is explaining that this does not refer to God's personal attribute of being just, but to the gift of righteousness He gives to humans.</note>—not that by which God is righteous, but that with which He clothes man when He justifies the ungodly. This is witnessed by the law and the prophets; indeed, the law and the prophets bear testimony to this. The Law witnesses by this very fact: that by commanding and threatening, yet justifying no one, it indicates clearly enough that man is justified by the gift of God through the help of the Spirit. The Prophets witness because the coming of Christ fulfilled what they foretold.

<margin>Romans 3</margin> For from here it follows, and he adds, saying: *But the righteousness of God through faith in Jesus Christ*—that is, through the faith by which one believes in Christ. For just as this "faith of Christ" <note>original: "fides Christi."</note> is so called not because Christ Himself believes, so that "righteousness of God" is not that by which God is righteous. Both, in fact, belong to us, but they are called "of God" and "of Christ" because they are granted to us by His generosity. Therefore, the righteousness of God has been manifested *without the law*, yet it is not *without the law*. For how could it be witnessed by the law, if it were manifested entirely without the law? But the righteousness of God is "without the law" in the sense that God confers it through the Spirit of grace upon the believer without the help of the law—that is, not being helped by...

<summary>Augustine explains that "the righteousness of God" is a gift of grace given to believers, rather than a result of following the Law's commands. He argues that the Law serves to point out sin and the need for God's help, rather than providing the power to be righteous on its own.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Augustine’s exegesis of the Apostle Paul’s Letter to the Romans. On the previous page, Augustine discussed how the Law of Moses reveals duty but does not provide the power to fulfill it. This page concludes that thought and transitions into Chapter 10, which addresses a paradox in 1 Timothy regarding who the Law is actually for.</meta>

<header>ST. AURELIUS AUGUSTINE</header>

...witnessed by the law. <note>This completes the sentence from the previous page: "The Prophets witnessed to this, as did the law."</note> Since through the law God shows man his own weakness, so that by fleeing to His mercy through faith, he might be healed. For of His wisdom it is said that she carries "law and mercy on her tongue" <note>original: "legem & misericordiam in lingua portet." This is a reference to a Latin version of Proverbs 3:16, personifying Wisdom as carrying both the strictness of the law and the kindness of mercy.</note>—the law, indeed, by which she makes the proud guilty, and mercy by which she justifies the humble.

<margin>Romans 3</margin> Therefore, the righteousness of God <note>original: "iustitia dei." As noted previously, this refers to the gift of being made righteous by God, not God's own attribute of justice.</note> comes through faith in Jesus Christ to all who believe. For there is no distinction. For "all have sinned and fall short of the glory of God"—the glory of God, not their own glory. 

<margin>1 Corinthians 4</margin> For what do they have that they did not receive? But if they received it, why do they boast as if they had not received it? Thus they fall short of the glory of God. And see what follows next: "Being justified freely by His grace." Therefore, they are not justified by the law, nor are they justified by their own will; rather, they are "justified freely by His grace." This is not because it happens without our will, but rather our will is shown to be weak through the law, so that grace may heal the will. Once the will is healed, it may fulfill the law—not because it is forced under the law, nor because it is in need of the law's threats.

## HOW IT SHOULD BE UNDERSTOOD: The 
## law is not laid down for the righteous, yet it is good, 
## if one uses it lawfully. Chapter 10.

<margin>1 Timothy 1</margin> For "the law is not laid down for the righteous," and yet it is "good if one uses it lawfully." By connecting these two seemingly contradictory points, the Apostle warns and prompts the reader to examine and resolve the question: How can the law be good if one uses it lawfully, if what follows is also true: "knowing this, that the law is not laid down for the righteous"?

<summary>Augustine argues that the Law’s purpose is to reveal human weakness so that people seek God's healing grace, which then enables the will to fulfill the Law freely. He begins to address the paradox that the Law is good for those who use it correctly, even though it is not meant for the "righteous."</summary>
<keywords>justification, grace, free will, Romans 3, 1 Corinthians 4, 1 Timothy 1, righteousness of God, healing of the will</keywords>

-># ON THE SPIRIT AND THE LETTER, BOOK [I]<-

<margin>1 Timothy</margin> posited. For who uses the law legitimately unless it is the righteous man? And yet, the law is not laid down for him, but for the unrighteous. Or is it that the unrighteous man must also use the law legitimately so that he may be justified—that is, so that he may become righteous—and be led to grace by the law as if by a <term>schoolmaster</term> <note>original: "pædagogo." In the Roman world, a pedagogue was a guardian or tutor who accompanied a child to ensure they reached their teacher safely and behaved correctly; Augustine uses this Paulist metaphor to describe the Law's role in leading humanity to Christ.</note> 
<margin>Galatians 3</margin> so that through grace alone he might be able to fulfill what the law commands? For it is by grace itself that he is justified freely—that is, with no preceding merits of his own 
<margin>Romans 11</margin> works—otherwise grace is no longer grace. For grace is given not because we have performed good works, but so that we might have the power to perform them. That is to say, it is not given because we have fulfilled the law, but so that 
<margin>Matthew 5</margin> we might be able to fulfill the law. For He [Jesus] said: "I have not come to abolish the law 
<margin>John 1</margin> but to fulfill it." Of Him it was said: "We have seen his glory, the glory as of the only-begotten of the Father, full of grace and truth." This 
<margin>Romans 3</margin> is the glory of which it was said: "For all have sinned and fall short of the glory of God." And this is the grace of which it is immediately said: "Being justified freely by His grace." Therefore, the unrighteous man uses the law legitimately so that he may become righteous. When he has become so, he should no longer use it as a vehicle once he has arrived at his destination—or rather, to use the Apostle’s own metaphor mentioned above, he should no longer use it as a schoolmaster once he has been fully educated. How then is the law not laid down for the righteous man, if it is still necessary for the righteous—not as a means by which the unrighteous is led to justifying grace, but as something the righteous man may use legitimately? Perhaps—indeed, not perhaps, but certainly—the righteous man uses the law legitimately when he applies it to terrify the unrighteous. In this way, when the disease of innate <term>concupiscence</term> <note>original: "concupiscentiæ." This refers to a deep-seated, disordered desire or "yearning" for things apart from God, which Augustine identifies as the root of sin.</note> begins to increase within them due to the stimulus of prohibition and the mounting heap of transgressions, they may flee through faith to justifying grace. Then, being delighted by the sweetness of righteousness through the gift of the Spirit, they may escape the penalty of the let... <note>The text breaks off mid-word: "lite-" likely completing as "literae" (the letter).</note>

<summary>Augustine explains that the Law serves as a "schoolmaster" to lead the unrighteous toward grace, emphasizing that grace is a free gift that enables good works rather than a reward for them. He concludes that while the law is not "for" the righteous, they may still use it to lead others toward faith by revealing the weight of their sins.</summary>

# THE WORKS OF SAINT AURELIUS AUGUSTINE

...<unclear>determining</unclear> <note>original: "reminantis" – likely part of "determinantis" (determining or limiting).</note> penalty? In this way, these two things will not be contrary, nor will they fight against each other: that the righteous man uses the law legitimately <note>original: "legitime," though OCR reads "legis time."</note> and yet the law is not laid down for the righteous man. For he was not justified by the law, but by the law of faith, through which he believed that there was no way his own weakness could fulfill what the law of works commanded, unless divine grace came to his aid. 

<margin>Romans 3</margin>
Therefore he [the Apostle Paul] says: "Where then is boasting? It is excluded. By what law? Of works? No, but by the law of faith." Whether he meant a praiseworthy boasting—the kind that is in the Lord—and called it "excluded" <note>original: "exclusam." Augustine is playing on the double meaning of the Latin verb "excludere," which can mean both "to shut out" and "to hammer out" or "emboss."</note> not in the sense of being driven away, but in the sense of being hammered out to stand in relief. This is why certain silversmiths are called <term>excluders</term> <note>original: "exclusores." In ancient metallurgy, this referred to craftsmen who created embossed or "chased" metalwork by hammering the design from the back so it stands out.</note>.

<margin>Psalm 67</margin>
Hence we also find this in the Psalms: "That they may be brought forth <note>original: "excludantur"</note> who have been tested by silver," which is to say, that those who have been tested by the eloquence of the Lord might stand out. 

<margin>Psalm 11</margin>
For it is said elsewhere: "The words of the Lord are pure words, silver tested by fire." <note>The Latin text cites the Vulgate numbering (Psalm 11); in modern English Bibles, this is Psalm 12:6.</note> On the other hand, perhaps he wished to mention a wicked boasting that comes from pride—the boasting of those who, because they seem to themselves to live righteously, boast as if they had not received their righteousness as a gift. He says this kind of boasting is "excluded" <note>here meaning "shut out" or "rejected"</note> not by the law of works, but by the law of faith. That is, it is cast out and thrown away, because through the law of faith, every person recognizes that if they live well in any way, they have it by the grace of God; and they realize they will not obtain perfection in the love of justice from any other source.

<meta>This page continues Augustine’s argument from the previous section where he distinguished between the "law of works" and the "law of faith." # BOOK ON THE SPIRIT AND THE LETTER

This thought makes a person pious, for piety is true wisdom. I speak of that piety which the Greeks call <term>theosebeia</term> <note>original: "theosebian." From the Greek θεοσέβεια, meaning "reverence for God" or "godliness."</note>; indeed, this very quality was commended to mankind when it was read in the book of Job: "Behold, piety is wisdom." 

<margin>Job 28</margin>
Furthermore, if this word were interpreted according to its Latin origin, it could be called the "worship of God" <note>original: "dei cultus."</note>, which consists primarily in this: that the soul should not be ungrateful to Him. For this reason, we are also admonished to give thanks to the Lord our God in that most true and singular sacrifice <note>Augustine refers here to the Eucharist or Holy Communion, specifically the "Sursum Corda" where the priest says, "Let us give thanks to the Lord our God."</note>. 

However, the soul will be ungrateful if it attributes to itself what it has from God—especially regarding righteousness. The soul is often puffed up by the works of righteousness as if they were its own, produced by itself for itself. This is no ordinary pride, such as one might feel regarding riches, physical beauty, eloquence, or other external or internal goods of body and mind (which even the wicked are known to possess); rather, it is a pride taken in those things which are the specific "goods of the good" <note>The "goods of the good" refers to virtues and spiritual gifts that, unlike physical beauty or wealth, are intended to lead to salvation.</note>, as if the soul were wise on its own. Driven away from the stability of the divine substance by this vice, even certain great men drifted down into the disgrace of idolatry. 

Whence the same Apostle [Paul], in the same epistle in which he is a vehement defender of grace, after saying that he was a debtor to both Greeks and Barbarians, to the wise and the unwise, and that therefore, for his part, he was ready to preach the Gospel even to those in Rome: "For I am not ashamed," he says, "of the Gospel, for it is the power of God for salvation to everyone who believes, to the Jew first and also to the Greek." 

<margin>Romans 1</margin>
"For the righteousness of God is revealed in it from faith to faith, as it is written: 'But the righteous man lives by faith.'" 

<margin>Habakkuk</margin>
This is the righteousness of God, which in the Old Testament [was]...

<margin>Psalm 3</margin>
by which He makes us saved. And this is the faith "from which and in which" <note>A reference to Romans 1:17: "from faith to faith."</note> it is revealed—namely, from the faith of those announcing it into the faith of those obeying it. By this **faith of Jesus Christ** (that is, the faith which Christ has bestowed upon us), we believe that our living righteously comes to us from God, and that it will be even more fully realized in the future; for this, we give thanks to Him with that piety by which He alone is to be worshipped.

<meta>This page continues Augustine’s examination of the Apostle Paul’s Letter to the Romans. On the previous page, Augustine argued that knowledge of God alone—whether through nature or the Law—is insufficient for salvation because it often leads to pride rather than gratitude. He now begins to distinguish between the "law of works" and the "law of faith."</meta>

# OF THE SPIRIT AND THE LETTER, BOOK [I]

Therefore, it is worthwhile for us to consider how the law of deeds—that is, the law of works <note>original: "Lex factorum, id est operum"</note>—by which that boasting is not excluded, differs from the law of faith, by which boasting is excluded. We shall do this if we are indeed able to perceive and distinguish the difference. For someone might quickly say that the law of works belongs to Judaism, while the law of faith belongs to Christianity, because circumcision and other such works of the law are requirements that Christian discipline no longer observes.

But we have been striving for a long time now to show how deceptive this distinction is. Perhaps we have already demonstrated this to those with sharp minds for discernment—and especially to you—but because this is a great matter, it is not inappropriate for us to dwell upon its clarification by providing even more testimonies.

<margin>Romans 5</margin> For Paul speaks of that very law by which no one is justified; he says it "entered in so that sin might abound." Yet, lest any unskilled person should criticize the law or any sacrilegious person should accuse it on this account, he defends it, saying: <margin>Romans 7</margin> "What then shall we say? Is the law sin? Far from it! But I did not know sin except through the law; for I would not have known <term>concupiscence</term> <note>original: "concupiscentiam"; refers to the internal impulse toward forbidden desires or "coveting."</note> <margin>Exodus 20</margin> unless the law had said: *You shall not covet.*" Thus, taking the opportunity, sin through the commandment produced in me all manner of coveting. 

He also says: "The law indeed is holy, and the commandment holy and just and good; but sin, so that it might appear as sin, produced death in me through what is good." This, therefore, is the **letter that kills** <note>original: "litera occidens"; a reference to 2 Corinthians 3:6. Augustine argues that the Law "kills" because it forbids sin but does not provide the spiritual strength to stop doing it.</note> which says: *You shall not covet.* Concerning this, he says the same thing I mentioned a little earlier: <margin>Romans 3</margin> "For through the law comes the knowledge of sin." But now, the righteousness of God <note>original: "iustitia dei"; in this context, it refers to the grace by which God makes a person righteous, rather than His role as a punishing judge.</note> has been manifested without the law, though it is testified to by the law and the prophets.

C 3

<summary>Augustine argues that the "letter that kills" is not just the old ceremonial laws of Judaism, but the moral Law itself (like the prohibition against coveting), which exposes sin without providing the power to overcome it. He distinguishes the "law of works" from the "law of faith," noting that true righteousness comes through God's grace rather than simple legal obedience.</summary>

<keywords>Law of works, Law of faith, concupiscence, righteousness of God, letter that kills, Romans 7, justification, grace vs law</keywords>

# SAINT AURELIUS AUGUSTINE

...and the prophets; but the righteousness of God is through faith in Jesus Christ for all who believe. For there is no distinction: for all have sinned and fall short of the glory of God. They are justified freely by his grace through the redemption that is in Christ Jesus, whom God set forth as a <term>propitiation</term> <note>original: "propitiatorium"; refers to a place of atonement or the "mercy seat" where God’s favor is restored.</note> through faith in his blood, to demonstrate his righteousness for the sake of the remission <note>original: "praeteritionem"; the OCR "praecetiū" is a transcription error for "passing over" or remission of previous sins.</note> of sins in the patience of God, to show his righteousness at this time, that he might be just and the justifier of him who is of the <margin>Romans 3</margin> *faith of Jesus.* Then he adds the point we are now discussing: *Where then is boasting? It is excluded. By what law? That of works? No, but by the law of faith.* Therefore, the law of works is the very one that says, <margin>Exodus 20</margin> *You shall not covet,* because through it comes the knowledge of sin. I wish to know, therefore, if anyone would dare tell me whether the law of faith does not also say, *You shall not covet.* For if it does not say so, what reason is there why we, placed under it, should not sin securely and with impunity? For this is <margin>Romans 3</margin> what those people thought the Apostle was saying, of whom he says: *And as some claim we say, "Let us do evil that good may come," whose judgment is just.* If, however, the law of faith also says, *You shall not covet*—just as so many evangelical and apostolic precepts do not cease to testify and proclaim—why then is it not also called the law of works? For it is not as if, because it lacks the works of the ancient <term>sacraments</term> <note>original: "sacramentorum"; here meaning sacred signs or rituals like circumcision.</note>—namely circumcision and the others—it therefore lacks works in its own sacraments which are appropriate for this present time. Or was the question really about the works of the sacraments, when the law was mentioned for this reason: that through the law comes the knowledge of sin, and therefore no one is justified by it?

<summary>Augustine uses Romans 3 to argue that while the "law of faith" replaces the "law of works," it still maintains moral prohibitions against coveting; however, it justifies through grace rather than the performance of rituals.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Augustine’s exposition on the distinction between the Law of Works and the Law of Faith. On the previous page, he questioned whether the "law of faith" also forbids coveting. Here, he provides his famous resolution: while both laws forbid sin, the Law of Works commands by threat, while the Law of Faith obtains the ability to obey through prayer and reliance on God's grace.</meta>

<header>### ON THE SPIRIT AND THE LETTER, BOOK I</header>

<margin>Romans 3</margin>
...boasting is not excluded through that [law of works], but through the law of faith, by which the righteous man lives. But is there not also a knowledge of sin through this [law of faith], since it also says: 
<margin>Exodus 20</margin>
*You shall not covet*? Let me briefly explain the difference. What the law of works commands by threatening, the law of faith obtains by believing. 
<margin>Wisdom 8</margin>
The former says: *You shall not covet*; the latter says: *Since I knew that no one can be continent <note>original: "continens"; in this context, it refers to the self-control or "continence" required to resist forbidden desires.</note> unless God grants it, and this very thing was a part of wisdom, to know whose gift it was, I approached the Lord and prayed.* This is that wisdom which is called <term>piety</term> <note>original: "pietas"; here Augustine uses the term to describe the true worship of God, characterized by humble dependence rather than ritual alone.</note>, by which the 
<margin>James 1</margin>
Father of lights is worshipped, from whom comes every good gift and every perfect gift. 
<margin>2 Corinthians 10</margin>
He is worshipped, moreover, with the sacrifice of praise and thanksgiving, so that His worshiper may glory not in himself, but in the Lord. And through this, by the law of works, God says, "Do what I command"; by the law of faith, 
<margin>2 Corinthians 3</margin>
it is said to God, "Give what You command." <note>original: "da quod iubes"; this is one of Augustine's most celebrated phrases, emphasizing that even the ability to obey God must be a gift from God himself.</note> For the law commands for this reason: to remind us what faith must do—that is, so that he who is commanded, if he cannot yet perform it, may know what to ask for; but if he can perform it immediately and does so obediently, he ought also to know by whose gift he is able to do so. 
<margin>1 Corinthians 2</margin>
*For we have not received the spirit of this world,* says that same most constant preacher of grace, *but the spirit who is from God, that we may know the things that have been given to us by God.* 
<margin>Romans 1</margin>
And what is the spirit of this world but the spirit of pride? By it, the foolish heart was darkened in those who, although they knew God, did not glorify Him as God or give thanks. 
<margin>Romans 10</margin>
Nor are those people deceived by any other spirit who, being ignorant of God’s righteousness and seeking to establish their own righteousness, have not submitted to the righteousness of God. Therefore, he seems to me to be more truly a son of faith who knows from whom to hope for what he does not yet possess, than he who...

<header>ST. AURELIUS AUGUSTINE</header>

...attributes to himself what he has, which of these two is to be preferred? Surely the one who both possesses [righteousness] and knows from whom he has it—provided, however, that he does not believe himself already to be what he is not yet, lest he fall into the vice of that <margin>Luke 18</margin> Pharisee. For although that man gave thanks to God for the things he possessed, he nevertheless asked for nothing more to be given to him, as if he lacked nothing for the increasing or perfecting of his righteousness. Therefore, having considered and examined these matters to the best of the strength which the Lord deigns to grant, we conclude that a man is not justified by the precepts of a good life, except through the faith of Jesus Christ. This is to say: not by the law of works, but by the law of faith; not by the letter, but by the spirit; not by the merits of deeds, but by free grace. <note>original: "gratuita gratia"; Augustine uses this redundant phrasing to emphasize that grace is a gift given entirely without any prior merit or payment.</note>

Chapter 14.

...that saying: *You shall not covet.* <note>original: "Non concupisces"; a reference to the tenth commandment and Paul's use of it in Romans 7:7 to show how the Law reveals sin.</note> And so that this which we are saying may become clearer, let us look at the <term>Decalogue</term> <note>The Ten Commandments, literally the "Ten Words" from the Greek *deka* and *logos*.</note> itself. For certainly Moses 
<margin>Exodus 31</margin> received the Law to be administered to the people on the mountain, written on stone tablets by the finger of God; this Law is constrained within ten precepts, 
<margin>Exodus 20</margin> where nothing of circumcision is commanded, and nothing of the sacrificial victims of cattle, which are no longer offered by Christians. In those ten precepts, then—with the exception of the observation of the Sabbath—let someone tell me what ought not to be observed by a Christian? Whether it be concerning the refusal to make or worship idols or any other gods besides the one true God, or not taking the name of the Lord in vain, or deferring honor to parents, or avoiding fornications, homicides, thefts, false testimonies, adulteries, or the coveting of another's property? Which of these would anyone say a Christian is not bound to keep? Is it perhaps not this law, written on those two tablets, which the Apostle calls the "killing letter," but rather the law of circumcision and the other ancient, now abolished <term>sacraments</term>? <note>Augustine uses "sacramenta" here in the broad sense of "sacred signs" or "rituals," specifically referring to the ceremonial requirements of the Old Covenant that Christians no longer follow.</note> But how can we think so, since in that Law is found the command *You shall not covet*—the very commandment through which [Paul] said, "Sin deceived me, and through it killed me," even though the commandment itself is "holy and just and good"? For what else does it mean that *the letter kills, but the spirit gives life*? Indeed, it is most evident in that very passage to the Corinthians where he says *the letter kills, but the spirit gives life*, that he intends no other "letter" to be understood than the Decalogue itself written on those two tablets. 
<margin>2 Corinthians 3</margin> For so he says: *Because you are the epistle of Christ administered by us, written not with ink but with the Spirit of the living God, not on tablets of stone, but on the tablets of the heart...*

<summary>Augustine argues that the "killing letter" described by Paul refers to the Ten Commandments themselves, not just ceremonial rituals. He explains that even moral laws can "kill" by revealing sin without providing the grace to overcome it.</summary>

For when he had said, "that we may serve in the newness of the spirit and not in the oldness of the letter," <margin>Romans 7</margin> he says, "What then shall we say? Is the law sin? God forbid. But I did not know sin, except through the law. For I did not know lust, unless the law said: 'You shall not lust.' Sin, taking opportunity through the commandment, worked in me all manner of lust. For without the law, sin is dead. I was alive once without the law; but when the commandment came, sin revived. But I died, and the commandment which was unto life, this was found to be unto death for me. For sin, taking opportunity through the commandment, deceived me, and through it, killed me. Therefore the law indeed is holy, and the commandment is holy, and just, and good. Was then that which is good (for it is good) made death to me? God forbid. But sin, that it might appear as sin, through that which is good worked death in me..." <note>Augustine is quoting the famous passage from Romans 7:6–13, where Paul describes the psychological struggle of knowing the Law but being unable to follow it without grace.</note>

<summary>Augustine argues that the "letter that kills" is not just the ceremonial law, but the moral law (like "Thou shalt not covet"). He uses the Epistle to the Romans to show that the Law, though holy, only increases human guilt and "kills" the soul until the Holy Spirit provides the grace to actually fulfill it.</summary>

## SAINT AURELIUS AUGUSTINE

...so that sin might become sinful beyond measure through the commandment. For we know that the law is spiritual, but I am carnal, sold under sin. For I do not understand what I do; for I do not perform what I want, but I do what I hate. But if I do what I do not want, I agree with the law, that it is good. But now it is no longer I who do it, but the sin that dwells in me. I find then a law: when I want to do good, evil is present with me. For I delight in the law of God according to the inner man <note>Augustine is quoting Paul (Romans 7:13–22). The "inner man" refers to the human soul or mind that recognizes God's truth, as opposed to the "outer" or physical person driven by impulses.</note>. But I see another law in my members <note>original: "membris"; referring to the parts of the body or the physical self.</note> fighting against the law of my mind, and leading me captive in the law of sin which is in my members. Wretched man that I am! Who will deliver me from the body of this death? The grace of God through Jesus Christ our Lord. Therefore, I myself serve the law of God with my mind, but with the flesh, the law of sin. It appears, therefore, that the "oldness of the letter," if the "newness of the spirit" is missing, makes men guilty through the knowledge of sin rather than delivering them from sin.

<margin>Ecclesiastes 1</margin> Hence it is also written elsewhere: "He who increases knowledge, increases sorrow." <note>Quoting Ecclesiastes 1:18.</note> Not because the law itself is evil, but because the good commandment remains in the "demonstrating letter" and not in the "helping spirit." If that commandment is kept out of fear of punishment and not out of love for justice, it is done in a servile <note>Done like a slave who fears a master.</note> way, not a liberal <note>original: "liberaliter"; done in a way befitting a free person who acts out of their own will.</note> way, and therefore it is not truly done at all. For the fruit is not good if it does not rise from the root of charity <note>original: "charitatis"; the highest form of Christian love.</note>.

<margin>Galatians 5</margin> Furthermore, if faith is present—which works through love—one begins to delight in the law of God according to the inner man. This delight is a gift not of the letter, but of the spirit, even if another law in the members still fights against the law of the mind, until in the ne...

### THAT THE GRACE OF GOD WHICH JUSTIFIES
### MAN WAS HIDDEN VEILED IN THE OLD TESTAMENT
### AND REVEALED IN THE GOSPEL OF CHRIST.

->Chapter XV.<-

This grace was hidden, veiled in the Old Testament, which has been revealed in the Gospel of Christ by a most orderly distribution of times, just as God knows how to arrange all things. 
<margin>Exodus 20</margin> And perhaps it belongs to that very hiding place of His that in the Decalogue <note>The Ten Commandments.</note>, which was given on Mount Sinai, this alone was hidden by a symbolic precept: that which pertains to the Sabbath. 
<margin>Genesis 2</margin> Now, the Sabbath is a day of sanctification <note>Set apart as holy.</note>. Nor is it without reason that among all the works God made, sanctification was first mentioned there, where He rested from all His works. Regarding this, there is no room to discuss it further here. Nevertheless, regarding the matter at hand, I believe it is enough to say that it was not for nothing that the people were commanded on that day to 
<margin>Exodus 31</margin> abstain from all servile work <note>original: "ope servili"; Augustine interprets physical labor on the Sabbath as a symbol for the "work of sin" which makes a person a slave.</note>, by which sin is signified. This is because not sinning belongs to sanctification—that is, to the gift of God through the Holy Spirit. In the law written on two stone tablets, this [the Sabbath] alone among the rest was placed in the shadow of a figure <note>A symbolic or allegorical representation.</note> in which the Jews observe the Sabbath. This was done so that by this very fact it might be signified that it was then a time for hiding grace, 
<margin>Matthew 27</margin> which was to be revealed in the New Testament through the passion of Christ, 
<margin>2 Corinthians 3</margin> as if by the tearing of the temple veil. "For when it shall have passed over," he says...

<summary>Augustine argues that the Sabbath was the only symbolic commandment in the Decalogue, serving as a hidden sign of the grace and sanctification that would later be fully revealed through Christ's sacrifice.</summary>

# THAT THE SPIRIT OF GOD, BY WHOSE
## gift we are justified, takes away the delight in
## sinning where there is liberty; who is also
## called the finger of God. Chapter XVI.

<margin>2 Corinthians 3</margin> Now the Lord is Spirit: and where the Spirit of the Lord is, there is liberty. This is the Spirit of the Lord, by whose gift we are justified: by which it is brought about in us that we find no delight in sinning, which is where true liberty exists. Just as without this Spirit, there is a delight in sinning, which is a state of slavery; from the works of this slavery we must abstain: that is, we must spiritually keep the Sabbath <note>original: "sabbatizandum"; Augustine interprets the Sabbath not just as a day of rest, but as a spiritual cessation from the "work" of sin.</note>. This Holy Spirit, through whom 
<margin>Romans 5</margin> charity <note>Divine love.</note> is poured out in our hearts, 
<margin>Romans 13</margin> which is the fulfillment of the law, is also 
<margin>Luke 11</margin> called the "finger of God" in the Gospel. For this reason, because those tablets of the law were also written by the finger of God, and because the finger of God is the Spirit of God through whom we are sanctified—so that living by faith we may do good works through love—who would not be moved by this harmony, and by the same underlying reality in both instances? For fifty days are counted from the celebration of the Passover <note>The Jewish festival commemorating the Exodus from Egypt.</note>—which was commanded through Moses to be performed by the slaughter of a symbolic lamb, signifying the future passion <note>Suffering and death.</note> of the Lord—up to the day on which Moses received the Law on tablets written by the finger of God. Similarly, from the slaughter and resurrection of Him who 
<margin>Isaiah 53</margin> was led like a lamb to the slaughter, 
<margin>Acts 2</margin> when fifty days were completed, the finger of God—that is, the Holy Spirit—filled the faithful who were gathered together as one.

<meta>This page continues from the previous discussion where Augustine was comparing the giving of the Law on Mount Sinai (Old Testament) with the descent of the Holy Spirit at Pentecost (New Testament). He is concluding the thought that the same "Finger of God" (the Holy Spirit) wrote the law on stone to terrify the proud, but now writes it on hearts to justify the faithful.</meta>

ON THE SPIRIT AND THE LETTER, BOOK I.

...were the works of charity <note>original: "opera charitatis"; in this context, Augustine refers to outward actions performed under the law without the internal motivation of grace.</note>, it was the law of works, and the letter that kills. But when charity itself is poured into the heart of believers, it is the law of faith, giving life to the one who loves.

### Chapter XVII.

In this wonderful harmony, there is certainly a very great difference:
<margin>Exodus 19</margin>
that there, the people were forbidden by a frightening terror to approach the place where the law was given; but here, the Holy Spirit came upon those who were gathered together in one place, waiting for the one who had been promised. There, the finger of God worked upon tablets of stone; here, it worked upon human hearts.
<margin>Exodus 20</margin>
There, therefore, the law was placed externally so that the unjust might be terrified; here, it was given internally so that they might be justified.
<margin>Romans 13</margin>
For "you shall not commit adultery," "you shall not kill," "you shall not covet," and if there is any other commandment—which were certainly written on those tablets—it is summarized, he says, in this word: "You shall love your neighbor as yourself." Love works no evil to a neighbor. Now the fulfillment of the law is charity. This was not written upon tablets of stone, but was poured out into our hearts
<margin>Romans 5</margin>
through the Holy Spirit who has been given to us. Therefore, the law of God is charity. The wisdom of the flesh <note>original: "prudentia carnis"; a term used by St. Paul to describe a mindset focused on worldly or selfish desires rather than spiritual things.</note> is not subject to this law, nor indeed can it be; but to terrify this wisdom of the flesh, when the works of charity are written on tablets, it is the law of works, and the "letter that kills" the transgressor. But when charity itself is poured into the heart of believers, it is the law of faith, and the Spirit giving life to the one who loves. See now how this distinction harmonizes with those apostolic words which Paul...

<summary>Augustine contrasts the Law given at Mount Sinai on stone tablets with the Grace given at Pentecost in human hearts, arguing that the former terrifies through outward command while the latter justifies through internal love.</summary>

## SAINT AURELIUS AUGUSTINE

...things mentioned previously for another reason, and which I had deferred for a more diligent treatment. "For you are manifest," he says, <margin>2 Corinthians 3</margin> "that you are the epistle of Christ, ministered by us, written not with ink, but with the Spirit of the living God; not on tablets of stone, but on the fleshy tablets of the heart." Behold how he shows that the former <note>The Old Law/Ten Commandments</note> is written outside of man, so that it might terrify him from without; while the latter <note>The Gospel/Grace</note> is written within man himself, so that it might justify <note>original: "iustificet"; to be made righteous or brought into a right relationship with God</note> him from within. However, he called them "fleshy tablets of the heart" not in the sense of carnal wisdom <note>original: "carnalis prudentiae"; referring to worldly or selfish thinking</note>, but as something living and possessing feeling, in comparison to stone, which is without feeling. <margin>In the same place.</margin> And a little later he says that the children of Israel could not look steadfastly to the end of the face of Moses, and therefore he spoke to them through a veil. This signifies that the letter of the law <term>The Letter: The written code of the law which, without the help of the Spirit, only points out sin without giving the power to overcome it.</term> justifies no one. Instead, a veil is placed over the reading of the Old Testament until one turns to Christ and the veil is taken away—that is, one passes over to grace, and understands that justification comes to us from Him, by which we may do what He commands. He commands for this reason: so that when we find ourselves failing, we might flee to Him for help. Therefore, having most watchfully said, "Such confidence we have through Christ toward God," <margin>2 Corinthians 3</margin> he immediately explained the source of this, lest it be attributed to our own strength, saying: "Not that we are sufficient to think anything of ourselves, as of ourselves; but our sufficiency is from God, who also has made us fit ministers of the New Testament, not of the letter, but of the Spirit; for the letter kills, but the Spirit gives life."

### Chapter XVIII.

<margin>Romans 4</margin>
Therefore, because the law—as is said elsewhere—was established for the sake of transgression <note>original: "praeuaricationis gratia"; the idea that the Law was given to make human sinfulness clearly visible</note>, that is, this **letter** written outside of man, he calls it for that reason both the **ministration of death** and the **ministration of condemnation**. But this other one—that is, the ministration of the New Testament—he calls the **ministration of the Spirit** and the **ministration of righteousness**, because through the gift of the Spirit we perform righteousness and are liberated from the condemnation of transgression. Therefore, the former is passing away, while the latter remains; for the terrifying <term>Pedagogue: In the ancient world, a guardian or tutor who strictly supervised a child’s behavior; Augustine uses it to describe how the Law ruled through fear until the arrival of Christ.</term> is removed when charity <note>original: "charitas"; referring to the selfless, divine love of God</note> succeeds fear. For "where the Spirit of the Lord is, there is liberty." But he says that this ministration is not from our own merits, but from mercy, speaking in this way:
<margin>2 Corinthians 3</margin>
"Therefore, having this ministration, even as we have obtained mercy, let us not grow weary; but let us renounce the hidden things of shame, not walking in craftiness, nor handling the word of God deceitfully." By this "craftiness" and "deceit," he wants us to understand hypocrisy, because the proud wish to appear righteous. Hence, in that Psalm which the same Apostle commemorates as a testimony to this very grace:
<margin>Romans 4</margin>
"Blessed," he says, "is he to whom the Lord has not imputed sin, nor is there deceit in his mouth."
<margin>Psalm 31</margin> <note>Psalm 32 in modern Bibles.</note>
This is the confession of the humble saints, not of those boasting to be what they are not.
<margin>2 Corinthians 4</margin>

Therefore, let no Christian stray from this faith, which alone is truly Christian. Nor should anyone—when he has been shamed into not saying that we become righteous through ourselves without the grace of God working this in us (because he sees that the faithful and the pious cannot bear to believe such a claim)—turn instead to this argument: saying that we cannot be righteous without the operation of God's grace simply because He gave the **Law**, because He established **teaching**, and because He commanded **good precepts**. # BOOK ON THE SPIRIT AND THE LETTER

...for the house of Israel and for the house of Judah, a new covenant <note>Original: *testamentum*. In this theological context, it refers to a formal agreement or "covenant" between God and humanity.</note>. What does "I will complete" mean, if not "I will fulfill"? Not according to the covenant which I made with their fathers on the day when I took them by the hand to lead them out of the land of Egypt.

## WHENCE THE LAW IS CALLED
## Old and New. Chapter 20.

Therefore, that former one was "old" because this current one is "new." Why then is that one called old and this one new, when the same law is fulfilled through the new covenant—the very law which said in the old: <margin>Exodus 20</margin> *Thou shalt not covet*? It is because, he says, they did not persevere in my new covenant <note>The OCR reads *testamento novo* (new covenant), though most biblical manuscripts of Jeremiah 31:32 read *testamento meo* (my covenant). Augustine is emphasizing the transition between the two.</note>, and I disregarded them, says the Lord. Therefore, because of the guilt of the "old man"—which was not at all healed by the "letter" that commands and threatens—that covenant is called "old." This one, however, is called "new" because of the "newness of the spirit" <note>A reference to Romans 7:6; the "newness of the spirit" is the internal transformation by grace, contrasted with the "oldness of the letter."</note> which heals the "new man" from the flaw of old age <note>Original: *vetustatis*. This refers to the state of decay or spiritual death inherent in the fallen human condition.</note>. Finally, pay attention to what follows, and see by how great a light it is made clear—a light which men who think they see do not wish to observe—for "this is the covenant," he says, "which I will arrange for the house of Israel: After those days, says the Lord, I will put my laws <margin>Jeremiah 31</margin> into their hearts, and in their minds I will write them." Behold, this is why the Apostle says what we mentioned above: <margin>2 Corinthians 3</margin> "Not on tablets of stone, but on the tablets of the heart," because it is not written with ink, but by the Spirit of the living God. Nor do I think the Apostle intended to mention the "new covenant" in that place for any other reason—for there he says: "Who also has made us fit ministers of the new covenant, not of the letter, but of the spirit"—except that he was looking at this very prophecy when he said: "Not on tablets of stone, but on the tablets of the heart..."

<margin>Matthew 5</margin> *...lest the law which they then received should seem blameworthy: for it is the very same law that Christ did not come to destroy but to fulfill.* <note>original: "non uenit Christus soluere sed implere." Augustine is quoting Jesus' words in the Sermon on the Mount to show that the Old Testament law remains holy and valid.</note> *Yet it is not through that same law that the ungodly are justified, but through grace; for indeed, this is the work of the life-giving spirit, without which the letter kills.* <margin>Galatians 3</margin> *For if a law had been given which could give life, righteousness would certainly be by the law; but the scripture has confined all things under sin, so that the promise by faith in Jesus Christ might be given to those who believe. By this promise—that is, by God’s favor—the law itself is fulfilled. Without this promise, the law makes people transgressors, either through the actual performance of an evil deed—if the flame of desire leaps over the barriers of fear—or at least in the will alone, if the fear of punishment overcomes the sweetness of lust.* <note>Augustine argues that even if someone follows the law because they are afraid of being punished, they are still "sinning in their heart" because they would prefer to do the wrong thing if they could get away with it.</note> *For when it says: "Scripture has confined all things under sin, so that the promise by faith in Jesus Christ might be given to those who believe," the benefit of this confinement is stated. For to what end did it confine them, except as it is said elsewhere:* <margin>in the same place</margin> <note>Referring to the same chapter in Galatians (3:23).</note> *"But before faith came, we were kept under the law, confined for that faith which was afterwards to be revealed"? Therefore, the law was given so that grace might be sought. Grace was given so that the law might be fulfilled. For it was not through any fault of its own that the law was not fulfilled, but through the fault of the "wisdom of the flesh."* <note>original: "prudentiæ carnis." A term from St. Paul referring to human intellect or impulses when they are focused purely on worldly desires rather than spiritual truth.</note> *This fault was to be demonstrated by the law and healed by grace. For what was impossible for the law, in that it was weakened through the flesh, God did by sending His own Son* <margin>Romans 8</margin> *in the likeness of sinful flesh; and for sin, He condemned sin in the flesh, so that the righteousness of the law might be fulfilled in us, who walk not according to the flesh but according to the spirit. Hence also* <margin>Jeremiah 31</margin> *in that prophetic testimony: "I will complete," he says, "what..."*

<summary>Augustine explains that the Law cannot justify a person on its own; instead, it exposes human weakness and "confines" humanity under sin to highlight the necessity of God's grace. He concludes that while the Law reveals the disease of sin, only the Holy Spirit provides the cure to truly fulfill the Law's demands.</summary>

### WHAT THE LAWS ARE THAT ARE WRITTEN
### in the hearts of the faithful. Chapter 21.

<margin>Exodus 20</margin>
<margin>Romans 13</margin>
What then are the laws of God, written by God himself in the hearts, if not the very presence of the Holy Spirit? This Spirit is the <term>finger of God</term> <note>A traditional biblical metaphor for the power or action of God; Augustine identifies the "finger" that wrote the Ten Commandments on stone with the Holy Spirit who now writes on the heart.</note> by whose presence **love** <note>original: "charitas." For although the promises of the Old Testament are earthly—excepting the <term>sacraments</term> <note>Here, Augustine uses "sacraments" in the broad sense of "sacred signs" or rituals.</note> which were "shadows of things to come," such as circumcision, the Sabbath, and other observations of days, the ceremonies of certain foods, and the manifold rites of sacrifices and sacred things which were suited to the old carnal law and the "yoke of slavery"—they nevertheless contain such precepts of justice as we are also commanded to observe now. These were expressed especially in those two tables <note>The Two Tables of the Law, better known as the Ten Commandments.</note> without the figurative language of a hidden meaning, such as: **"You shall not commit adultery," "You shall not commit murder," "You shall not covet,"** and whatever other commandment is summed up in this saying: **"You shall love your neighbor as yourself."** Nevertheless, because in that [Old] Covenant, as I said, earthly and temporal promises are recited (which are the good things of this corruptible flesh, even if they were used to symbolize the eternal and heavenly things belonging to the New Covenant), now the good of the heart itself is promised: the good of the mind, the good of the spirit—that is, an **intelligible good** <note>In Platonic and Augustinian thought, an "intelligible" good is one perceived by the intellect/spirit rather than the physical senses.</note>—when it is said: *I will put my laws in their mind, and I will write them in their hearts.* By this, he signified that they would not fear a law terrifying them from the outside, but...

Then he added the reward: *And I will be their God, and they will be my people.* <note>original: "Et ero eis in deum, et ipsi erunt mihi in populum." Augustine is quoting the promise of the New Covenant from Jeremiah.</note> This is what that man says regarding God: *But for me, it is good to cling to God.* <note>original: "Mihi autem adhaerere deo bonum est." A quote from Psalm 73:28.</note> *I will be,* He says, *their God, and they will be my people.* What is better than this good? What is happier than this felicity—to live for God? To live for God, with whom is the **fountain of life**, and in whose light we shall see light. Regarding this life, the Lord himself says: *This however is eternal life, that they may know you, the one true God, and Jesus Christ whom you have sent*—that is, you and Jesus Christ whom you have sent, the one true God. For He himself promises this to those who love Him, saying: *He who loves me keeps my commandments, and he who loves me will be loved by my Father, and I will love him and show myself to him.* 

He means, certainly, in the <term>form of God</term> <note>original: "in forma dei." This refers to Christ's divine nature, which is invisible to the wicked and only revealed to the faithful in the afterlife.</note> in which He is equal to the Father, not in the <term>form of a servant</term> <note>original: "in forma servi." This refers to Christ’s human nature, which even those who crucified him could see.</note> in which He showed himself even to the wicked. For then that which is written will come to pass: *Let the wicked be taken away so that he may not see the glory of the Lord.* This will happen when those on the left depart into eternal fire, but the righteous into eternal life. 

As I have mentioned, this eternal life is defined as this: that they may know the one true God. Hence John also says: *Beloved, we are the sons of God, and it has not yet appeared what we shall be. We know that when he appears, we shall be like him, because we shall see him as he is.* This likeness begins to be **reformed** now, as long as the <term>inner man</term> <note>original: "homo interius." Augustine’s term for the soul or the spiritual self that undergoes growth and renewal while the physical body ages.</note> is renewed from day to day, according to the image of Him who created him.

<summary>Augustine defines the ultimate reward of the New Covenant as "eternal life," which he interprets as the spiritual vision of God and the restoration of the divine image within the soul.</summary>
<keywords>eternal life, reward, knowledge of God, New Covenant, inner man, divine image, Christ's divinity, Augustine</keywords>

# ON SAINT AURELIUS AUGUSTINE

## FOLLOWING THE EXAMPLE OF THE APOSTLE, HE COMPARES THE SPIRITUAL LIFE OF THIS TIME TO THAT OF THE FUTURE. Chapter 23.

<margin>1 Corinthians 13</margin>
But compared to the perfection of that eminence which is to come, what is our current state, or how much does it amount to? Indeed, the Apostle, applying a simple example from familiar things to those unspeakable realities, compared the age of childhood to the age of manhood. *When I was a child,* he says, *I spoke like a child, I understood like a child, I thought like a child; but when I became a man, I did away with the things of a child.* <note>original: "Cum essem... paruulus." Augustine quotes Paul to explain that our current spiritual understanding is merely a "childhood" phase compared to heaven.</note> He then shows why he said this, following with: *We see now through a mirror in an enigma, but then face to face. Now I know in part, but then I shall know even as I am known.* <note>original: "per speculum in aenigmate." This famous phrase refers to seeing a blurred or indirect reflection of truth rather than the reality itself.</note>

Quoting Jeremiah 31:34, predicting a time when teaching is unnecessary because God is known directly.</note> Now, certainly, is the time of the New Testament, which through the prophet...

<sig>D 5</sig>

The promise was made through these words which we recalled from that prophecy. Is not the very act of preaching the means by which this is said everywhere? For why would the Apostle to the Gentiles <note>The "Apostle to the Gentiles" is Saint Paul.</note> call himself a teacher, unless it is because that which he himself says is coming to pass: <margin>Romans 10</margin> *How will they call upon Him in whom they have not believed? Or how do they believe in Him whom they have not heard? And how will they hear without a preacher?* 

Since, therefore, this preaching now spreads everywhere, how can this be the time of the New Testament of which the prophet said: <margin>Jeremiah 31 ¶</margin> *And no one shall teach his neighbor, and no one his brother, saying: "Know the Lord," for all shall know me from the least to the greatest of them*? What, then, does "All from the least to the greatest of them" mean, except all those who belong spiritually to the house of Israel and to the house of Judah—that is, to the sons of Isaac and to the seed of Abraham? For this is the very promise that was spoken to him: <margin>Romans 9</margin> *In Isaac shall your seed be called.* For it is not those who are children of the flesh who are the children of God, but the children of the promise are counted as the seed. <note>original: "filij promissionis." Augustine argues that being a "child of God" is a spiritual status granted by God's promise, rather than a biological inheritance.</note> 

Now this is the word of the promise: <margin>Genesis 18</margin> *At this time I will come, and Sarah shall have a son.* And not only this, but also Rebecca, having conceived by one union with our father Isaac. For while the children were not yet born, and had done nothing good or evil—so that God's purpose according to election might remain, not based on works but on Him who calls—it was said to her: "The elder shall serve the younger." <note>original: "maior seruiet minori." This refers to the twins Jacob and Esau. Augustine uses this biblical story to show that God's choice (election) happens before a person has done any "works" or good deeds, emphasizing that grace is a gift.</note> These...

<summary>Augustine reconciles the ongoing need for Gospel preaching with Jeremiah’s prophecy that all shall know God, concluding that the prophecy refers to the ultimate reward of heaven: the direct contemplation of God. He further defines the "children of God" not as biological descendants, but as those chosen through God's promise and grace.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Augustine’s exploration of who constitutes the "House of Israel" and "House of Judah" in the context of the New Covenant. Having established that the knowledge of God is a gift, he now defines the "children of the promise" as those called by grace rather than those who rely on their own works or ancestry.</meta>

<header>SAINT AURELIUS AUGUSTINE</header>

<margin>Genesis 25</margin> ...is the house of Israel or the house of Judah because of Christ, who came from the tribe of Judah. This is the house of the children of the promise—not by their own works, but by the kindness of God. For God promises that which He Himself performs; for He does not promise while another performs, which would no longer be a promise but a prediction. Therefore, it is not by works, but from the One who calls, so that the achievement belongs to God and not to them. Otherwise, the reward would be credited not according to grace, but according to what is owed, <margin>1 Corinthians 15</margin> and thus grace would no longer be grace. The most forceful defender and champion of this grace is the least of the apostles, <note>Saint Paul, who calls himself "the least of the apostles" in 1 Corinthians 15:9 because he originally persecuted the church.</note> who labored more than all of them—yet it was not he, but the grace of God with him. "All," he says, "shall know me": All the house of Israel and the house of Judah. Yet not all who are of Israel are [truly] Israel, but rather all those to whom it is said in the psalm "for the morning support"—that is, for the new light, specifically that of the New Testament. <margin>Psalm 21</margin> <note>original: "Psal. 21." In the Vulgate numbering used here, this is Psalm 22 in modern Bibles. Augustine interprets the "morning light" as the resurrection and the New Covenant.</note> "All you seed of Jacob, glorify Him; let all the seed of Israel fear Him." Truly all the seed, entirely every seed of those promised and called—but of those who are called according to His purpose. <margin>Romans 8</margin> For those whom He predestined, He also called; those whom He called, He also justified; those whom He justified, He also glorified. And therefore it is from faith, so that according to grace the promise might be firm for all the seed—not only for that which is of the Law (that is, those who come from the Old Testament to the New), but also for that which is of faith, even though the Law was not promised to it. Specifically, it is of the faith of Abraham—that is, for those who imitate the faith of Abraham, who is the father of us all, as it is written: <margin>Genesis 17</margin> <note>The marginal note in the original text likely contains a printer's error ("Gene. 7"); the quote "I have made you the father of many nations" is found in Genesis 17:5.</note> "Because I have made you the father of many nations." <margin>Romans 4</margin> Therefore, all these who are predestined, called, justified, and glorified, kno... <note>The text cuts off at "co," likely the beginning of "cognoscent" (will know) or "cohaeredes" (co-heirs).</note>

<summary>Augustine defines the "House of Israel" not as a biological lineage, but as the community of those predestined and called by God's grace. He argues that true inheritance comes through the "faith of Abraham" rather than through the works of the Law.</summary>
