# ON THE SPIRIT AND THE LETTER, BOOK [ONE]

<margin>Romans 8</margin> ...they shall know God by the grace of the New Testament, from the least to the greatest of them. Just as the <term>law of works</term> <note>Augustine defines the "law of works" (lex factorum) as the Old Testament commandments written on stone, which demand obedience but do not provide the internal power to fulfill them.</note> was written on stone tablets, and its reward was that land of promise which the fleshly house of Israel received when it was delivered from Egypt, so it pertains to the Old Testament. In the same way, the <term>law of faith</term> <note>The "law of faith" (lex fidei) is the grace of the Holy Spirit written in the heart, enabling true love and obedience.</note> is written in the hearts, and its reward—the beauty of <term>contemplation</term> <note>The Latin "species contemplationis" refers to the direct, joyful vision of God in the afterlife.</note> which the spiritual house of Israel will receive when it is delivered from this world—pertains to the New Testament. Then that which the Apostle says will come to pass: 

<margin>1 Corinthians 13</margin> "Whether prophecies shall fail, or tongues shall cease, or knowledge shall vanish away"—that is, that knowledge of "little ones" in which we live here, which is seen "in part" "through a glass, darkly." <note>original: "per speculum in aenigmate." This famous phrase from 1 Corinthians 13:12 suggests that in this life we only see a dim, indirect reflection of divine truth, like a reflection in a polished metal mirror.</note> For because of this partial knowledge, prophecy is necessary, while future things still follow past things. Because of this, "tongues" are necessary—meaning a multiplicity of signs and meanings—since a person is warned of one thing after another, by various means, if they do not yet contemplate the eternal light of clear truth with a most purified mind. 

But when that which is perfect has come, and all that which is "in part" has vanished, then He who appeared to human eyes by taking on flesh will show Himself to those who love Him. Then there will be eternal life, so that we may know the one true God. Then we shall be like Him; for then we shall know, even as we are known. 

<margin>Jeremiah 31</margin> Then no one shall teach his neighbor or his brother, saying, "Know the Lord," for all shall know Him, from the least to the greatest of them. This can be understood in many ways: either because even in that heavenly kingdom, each of the saints differs from another in glory, just as "star differs from star in glory."

<summary>Augustine contrasts the Old Testament "law of works" and its earthly rewards with the New Testament "law of faith" and its reward of eternal contemplation. <margin>Matthew 20</margin> ...it makes no difference whether it is said from the least to the greatest, as was stated, or if it were said from the greatest to the least; it likewise changes nothing. This remains true even if we understand "the least" to be those who only believe, and "the greatest" to be those who have also been able to understand—as much as is possible in this life—that **unbodily and immutable light** <note>original: "lumen incorporeum atque incommutabile." Augustine refers to the divine nature of God, which is not made of matter and never changes.</note>. Or, perhaps he wished "the least" to be understood as those who come later in time, and "the greatest" as those who came earlier. For all shall receive the promised contemplation of God at the same time, because those earlier ones provided better things for us, so that they might not be made perfect without us. And thus the earlier ones are found, as it were, to be "least" because they were delayed for a shorter time <note>This refers to the wait between the end of their lives and the final reward.</note>, just as is said through the parable in the Gospel regarding the <term>denarius</term> <note>A Roman silver coin representing a day's wage. In the parable of the workers in the vineyard (Matthew 20), everyone receives the same pay regardless of when they started.</note>, which those who came last to the vineyard receive first. Or, in whatever other way—which perhaps escapes me at present—"the least" and "the greatest" are to be understood.

### ON THE DISTANCE BETWEEN THE OLD 
### Law and faith: And how God helps us in
### doing good. Chapter 25.

However, pay as diligent attention as possible to that which I am striving with such great effort to show. When the prophet promised a New Testament, saying it would be "not according to the covenant" which was made previously with the people of Israel when they were delivered from Egypt, he said nothing there regarding the changing of sacrifices or of any <term>sacraments</term> <note>original: "sacramentorum." In this early Christian context, "sacrament" refers broadly to any sacred rite, sign, or mystery, such as circumcision or animal sacrifice, rather than just the specific seven sacraments defined later in history.</note>. This is true even though those changes were undoubtedly going to follow, as we now see they have followed—a fact which the same prophetic scripture testifies in many other places. <meta>This page continues Augustine’s comparison between the Old and New Testaments. He is specifically contrasting the "law of works" (written on stone) with the "law of faith" (written on the heart), explaining that true righteousness comes from an internal change wrought by the Holy Spirit rather than external commands.</meta>

# BOOK ON THE SPIRIT AND THE LETTER.

...to this covenant, and their writings in hearts. From where <margin>2 Corinthians 3</margin> the Apostle took the words: "not with ink, but with the Spirit of the living God; not on stone tablets, but on the fleshy tablets of the heart." And the eternal reward of this **justification** <note>The process by which a person is made righteous or "right" in the eyes of God.</note> is not the land from which the **Amorites** and **Hittites** <note>original: "Amorræi, & Aethæi." Ancient tribes mentioned in the Bible as inhabitants of the Promised Land before the Israelites arrived.</note> and other nations were driven out—which are commemorated there <margin>Psalm 72</margin>—but God Himself, to whom "it is good to cling," so that the "good of God" which they love may be God Himself whom they love. Likewise, nothing separates humans from God except sins, which are not forgiven except through <margin>Jeremiah 31</margin> that same grace. Whence, when he had said: "For all shall know me from the least to the greatest of them," he immediately added: "Because I will be merciful to their iniquity, and their sins I will remember no more." Therefore, through the **law of deeds** <margin>Exodus 20</margin> the Lord says: "You shall not covet"; but through the **law of faith** the Lord says: <margin>John 15</margin> "Without me you can do nothing." For he was speaking regarding good works, that is, of the fruit of the branches <note>A reference to the parable of the Vine and the Branches in the Gospel of John, where Jesus explains that believers must remain in him to produce "fruit" (good deeds).</note>. Since, therefore, this difference between the Old and New Testaments is apparent—that there the law is written on tablets, but here in hearts—it follows that what terrifies from without there, delights from within here; and there one becomes a transgressor through the **killing letter**, while here one becomes a lover through the **life-giving spirit**. It must not be said, then, that God helps us to perform righteousness and works in us "both the willing and the doing" for His good pleasure simply because the precepts of righteousness are sounded into our senses from the outside; rather, it is because He gives growth from within by "pouring out charity in our hearts through the Holy Spirit who has been given to us."

-> HOW FROM THE GENTILES... <-

<summary>Augustine distinguishes between the Old Testament law that commands from the outside and the New Testament grace that transforms the heart from within. He argues that true righteousness is not following a written code out of fear, but acting out of love granted by the Holy Spirit.</summary>

# OF SAINT AURELIUS AUGUSTINE

### ...how those living by the natural law differ from those in whose
### hearts the law of God is written, and what is signified by the name
### of the "Gentiles": and how it is to be understood that:
### The doers of the law shall be justified.
### Chapter 26.

<margin>Romans 2</margin> It must be considered, however, how the Apostle says: "For when the Gentiles, who do not have the law, do by nature the things of the law." These, not having the law, are a law unto themselves, who show the **work of the law** written in their hearts. This must be examined so it does not appear that there is no certain distinction in the **New Testament** <note>The "New Covenant" established through Christ, contrasted with the "Old Covenant" of the Mosaic Law.</note>, given that the Lord promised He would write His laws in the hearts of His people, yet here the Gentiles seem to have this by nature.

Therefore, this question must be treated, for it is no small matter that has arisen. For someone might say: "If God distinguishes the New Testament from the Old by this—that in the Old He wrote His law on tablets, but in the New He wrote it on hearts—then how are the faithful of the New Testament distinguished from the Gentiles? For the Gentiles already have the work of the law written in their hearts, by which they naturally do the things of the law." It would be as if they were already superior to that ancient people who received the law on stone tablets, and prior to the new people, for whom that is provided through the New Testament which nature has already provided for these Gentiles.

Or perhaps did the Apostle mention those Gentiles having the law written in their hearts because they actually belong to the New Testament? To answer this, we must look at the context of his argument. First, commending the **Gospel** <margin>Romans 1</margin> <note>original: "Evangelium." Literally the "Good News" of the Christian message.</note>, he says: "For it is the power of God for the salvation of everyone who believes; to the **Jew** first, and also to the **Greek** <note>original: "Iudæo... & Græco." In this context, "Greek" is often used as a synonym for "Gentile" or non-Jew.</note>. For the **righteousness** <note>original: "iustitia." This refers to the state of being "right" or justified before God.</note> of God is revealed in it from faith..."

<summary>Augustine addresses a potential contradiction: if the New Testament's hallmark is the law written on the heart, why does Paul say Gentiles already have this law naturally? He begins to investigate whether these "virtuous Gentiles" are actually part of the New Covenant or a separate category.</summary>

<keywords>natural law, New Testament, Gentiles, Romans 2, justification, righteousness of faith, Apostle Paul, writing on the heart</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Augustine’s analysis of Paul’s Epistle to the Romans. Having established that the New Covenant involves the law being written on the heart, Augustine now examines Romans 2:14-15 to determine if the "natural" law of the Gentiles is the same as this spiritual writing, or if Paul is referring to something else entirely.</meta>

# ON THE SPIRIT AND THE LETTER, BOOK 1.

<margin>Habakkuk 2</margin> ...in faith, just as it is written: *But the just man lives by faith.* <note>original: "Iustus autem ex fide uiuit." Augustine is quoting Paul (Romans 1:17), who in turn quotes the prophet Habakkuk.</note> Next, he speaks of those impious ones for whom, because of their pride, even the knowledge <margin>Romans 1</margin> of God was of no benefit: because they did not glorify Him as God, or give thanks. From there, he transitions to those who judge others and yet do the very things they condemn. He does this specifically because of the Jews, who were glorying in the law of God, even though he does not yet mention them by name; and thus he says: "Wrath and indignation, tribulation and anguish <margin>Romans 2</margin> upon every soul of man who works evil, the Jew first and also the Greek; but glory, and honor, and peace to everyone who works good, to the Jew first and also the Greek. For there is no **partiality** <term>acceptance of persons: the biblical concept that God does not show favoritism based on social status or ethnicity</term> with God. For whoever has sinned without the law shall perish without the law; and whoever has sinned under the law shall be judged by the law. For it is not the hearers of the law who are righteous before God, but the **doers of the law** shall be justified."

To these words he adds the point currently under discussion, saying: "For when the Gentiles, who do not have the law of God, do by nature the things which are of the law," and the rest which I have already mentioned above. Consequently, it does not seem that he meant any others here by the name of "Gentiles" <note>The Latin *gentes* refers to the non-Jewish nations of the world.</note> than those he previously indicated by the name "Greeks," when he said: "To the Jew first and also to the Greek." Furthermore, if the **Gospel** is the power of God for salvation to everyone who believes, to the Jew first and also to the Greek; and wrath, and indignation, tribulation and anguish are upon every soul of man who works evil, of the Jew first and also of the Greek; but glory, and honor, and peace are for everyone who works good, to the Jew first and also to the Greek; then this "Greek" is represented by the name of those "Gentiles" who do by nature the things which belong to the law...

<summary>Augustine interprets Paul’s letter to the Romans, arguing that the "Greeks" or "Gentiles" who fulfill the law by nature are those included in the universal promise of the Gospel, rather than a separate category of naturally virtuous people.</summary>

--- Page 168 ---
<meta>This page continues Augustine’s line-by-line analysis of Paul’s Letter to the Romans. On the previous page, Augustine introduced the idea that "doers of the law" are justified, and he now moves to reconcile this with Paul’s other famous claim: that salvation comes through grace, not through human effort or "works."</meta>

# SAINT AURELIUS AUGUSTINE

...and who have the work of the law written in their hearts, certainly these Gentiles belong to the Gospel, for the law has been written in their hearts. Indeed, for those who believe, it is the power of God for salvation. To which Gentiles, then, would He promise glory and honor and peace for doing good, if they were situated outside the grace of the Gospel? For because there is no **partiality** <term>acceptance of persons: the biblical concept that God does not show favoritism based on social status or ethnicity</term> with God, and it is not the hearers of the law but the doers who are justified: therefore, whether Jew or Greek—that is, anyone from the Gentiles who has believed—he shall likewise have salvation in the Gospel. 

For there is no <margin>Romans 3</margin> distinction, as he says later: *For all have sinned and fall short of the glory of God, being justified freely by His grace.* <note>original: "Omnes enim peccauerūt & egent gloria dei, iustificati gratis per gratiā ipsius." Augustine is quoting Romans 3:23-24 to show that everyone needs God's help regardless of their background.</note> But on what basis would he say a Greek "doer of the law" is justified without the grace of the Savior? For he would not speak against himself; when he says, "the doers of the law shall be justified," it is not as if they are justified through their own works rather than through grace. After all, he says a man is justified freely through faith without the works of the law, and he wants nothing else to be understood by the word <gloss>that</gloss> <note>The original text uses a shorthand "qd" for the Latin word "quod."</note> he says—"freely"—except that works do not precede justification.

Indeed, he says clearly elsewhere: <margin>Romans 11</margin> *If it is by grace, then it is not from works; otherwise grace is no longer grace.* <note>original: "Si gratia non ex operibus, alioquin gratia iam non est gratia." (Romans 11:6).</note> But we must understand the phrase "the doers of the law shall be justified" in such a way that we know they are not "doers" of the law unless they are first justified. Thus, justification does not come as a reward to those who are already doers; rather, justification precedes them so that they can become doers. <margin>Romans 4</margin> For what else does "justified" mean but "made just"—made just, of course, by Him who **justifies the ungodly** <note>original: "iustificat impiū" (Romans 4:5). Augustine emphasizes that God changes a person's nature from "ungodly" to "righteous."</note> so that from being ungodly, one becomes just! For if we were to speak in such a way as to say, "men shall be set free," this would certainly be understood to apply to those who were already men and...

->happens<-

<summary>Augustine argues that being a "doer of the law" is a result of God's grace rather than a prerequisite for it, asserting that justification means being "made righteous" by God.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Augustine’s line-by-line analysis of Paul’s Letter to the Romans. On the previous page, Augustine explored how "doers of the law" are justified. He now investigates the linguistic and theological meaning of "justification"—whether it means being *made* righteous by God's grace or being *declared* righteous in a legal or social sense.</meta>

### ON THE SPIRIT AND THE LETTER

...to grant liberation. For if we were to say, "men will be created," it would certainly not be understood to mean that those who were already men were being created, but rather that they become men through the act of creation itself. In the same way, if it were said, "the doers of the law shall be honored," we would correctly take this to mean that honor is granted to those who were already doers of the law. However, when it is said, "the doers of the law shall be justified," what else is being said but "the righteous shall be justified"? For the doers of the law are surely righteous. And through this, it is the same as if it were said: the doers of the law shall be created—not because they already existed as such, but so that they might exist. In this way, even the Jews, who are merely "hearers" of the law, might understand that they lack the grace of the Justifier <note>original: "iusti iustificatoris." Augustine is emphasizing that only a Righteous God can make a person righteous.</note> and require it so that they may become "doers." 

Or certainly, the phrase "they shall be justified" was said as if it were to say: "they shall be held as righteous" or "they shall be counted as righteous." Just as it was said of a certain man:
<margin>Luke 10</margin>
*But he, wanting to justify himself* <note>original: "Ille autẽ uolens se iustificare." This refers to the lawyer in Luke 10:29 who asks Jesus "Who is my neighbor?" to prove his own righteousness.</note>—that is, so that he might be held and counted as righteous. 

This is why we use the word in different ways: we say "God sanctifies His <margin>Matthew 6</margin> saints," but we also say "Hallowed <gloss>sanctified</gloss> be Thy name." The former is said because He Himself makes those people holy who were not previously holy. The latter is said so that what is already holy in itself might also be held as holy by men—that is, that it might be reverently feared. 

Therefore, when Paul mentions the Gentiles who "by nature" do the things of the law and have "the work of the law written in their hearts," he intended for us to understand those who believe in Christ. Because they do not come to the faith like the Jews, who had the law delivered to them beforehand, there is no reason for us to try to distinguish them from those to whom the prophet promised the New Testament, saying that <margin>Jeremiah 31</margin> He would write His laws in their hearts. For they too, through the grafting <margin>Romans 11</margin> by which [the apostle speaks] of the wild olive <note>original: "oleastro." This refers to Paul’s metaphor in Romans 11:17, where Gentile believers are described as branches from a wild olive tree grafted into the cultivated olive tree of Israel.</note>...

And therefore, in the house of the true Israel, in whom there is no guile, they are sharers of the New Covenant, because God puts His laws into their minds and writes them in their hearts with His own finger—that is, the Holy Spirit—through whom the love that is the fulfillment of the law is poured out within them.

### WHY THE GENTILES ARE SAID

Do not let it disturb you that he said they do "naturally" <note>original: *naturaliter*</note> those things which are of the law, and did not say "by the spirit of God," "by faith," or "by grace." For this is the very work of the spirit of grace: to restore in us the image of God in which we were naturally made.
<margin>Psalm 40</margin>
For a vice <term>vitium: a flaw, defect, or moral corruption that warps a thing's original good nature</term> is certainly something against nature, and it is this vice that grace surely heals. On account of this, it is said to God: *Have mercy on me, heal my soul, for I have sinned against you.* <note>Psalm 41:4 in modern Bibles. Augustine views sin as a disease of the soul that requires a physician.</note> Therefore, humans do naturally the things which are of the law. Those who do not do these things, do so through vice. By this vice, the law of God has been erased from their hearts. And because of this, once the vice is healed and the law is written there again, the things of the law are done naturally—not that grace is denied in favor of nature, but rather that nature is repaired through grace.
<margin>Romans 5</margin>
For through one man, sin entered into the world, and through sin, death; and so it passed into all men, in whom all have sinned. 
<margin>Romans 3</margin>
And therefore, because there is no distinction, they lack the glory of God, being justified freely <note>original: *gratis*</note> by His grace. This grace is written as righteousness <note>original: *iustitia*</note> in the renewed inner person, which fault <note>original: *culpa*</note> had erased; and this is the mercy shown toward the human race through Christ Jesus our Lord.
<margin>1 Timothy 2</margin>

...through old age. Nor could the law written on stone tablets produce this inscription—which is <term>justification: the process by which a person is made righteous before God</term> <note>original: *iuſtificatio*</note>—among the Jews; it could only produce transgression. For they too were human beings, and that power of nature was within them by which the rational soul both perceives and performs something according to the law. However, piety, which carries one over into another blessed life, possesses a spotless, eternal law that converts souls so they may be renewed by that light, and there is established in them:

<margin>Psalm 4</margin> “The light of your countenance, O Lord, is sealed upon us.” 

From this light, those who turned away deserved to wither away; but they cannot be renewed except by Christian <term>grace: the unmerited favor and help of God</term> <note>original: *gratia*</note>, that is, except by the intercession of the <term>mediator: one who acts as a bridge between God and humanity, specifically Jesus</term> <note>original: *mediator*</note>. For there is one God, and one <margin>1 Timothy 2</margin> mediator between God and men, the man Christ Jesus, who gave himself as a ransom for all. If those of whom we are speaking—who, in the manner we have already sufficiently described, do by nature the things of the law—remain strangers to his grace, what will their excusing thoughts profit them on the day when God shall judge the secrets of men, unless <margin>Romans 2</margin> perhaps they receive a more mild punishment? For just as certain <term>venial sins: minor or pardonable offenses that do not destroy the soul's relationship with God</term> <note>original: *peccata uenialia*</note>, without which this life is not lived, do not block a righteous person from eternal life, so some good works, without which life is very difficult to be—

<header>OF SAINT AURELIUS AUGUSTINE</header>

[...if you believe] in your heart that God raised Him from the dead, you will be saved. <margin>Joel 2</margin>
For "everyone who calls upon the name of the Lord will be saved."
<margin>Psalm 31</margin>
How great is the "abundance of your sweetness, O Lord," says the Psalmist, "which you have hidden for those who fear you, but have perfected for those who hope in you." From the law, we fear God; from faith, we hope in God. But for those who only fear punishment, grace remains hidden. Under this fear, the struggling soul—when it has not yet conquered <term>evil desire: the internal urge toward sin</term> <note>original: *concupiscentiam malam*</note>, and when that fear has not yet departed like a severe guardian—should flee through faith to the mercy of God. It should ask Him to give what He commands, so that, having been inspired by the sweetness of grace through the Holy Spirit, he may cause that which He commanded to bring more delight than the things that hinder us. In this way, the "abundance of His sweetness"—that is, the law of faith, His love written and poured out in our hearts—is perfected for those who hope in Him, so that a healthy soul may perform good works not out of fear of punishment, but out of love for righteousness.

<summary>Augustine explains that salvation and justification begin with an internal spiritual resurrection through faith, which eventually leads to a physical resurrection. He contrasts the fear produced by the Law with the "sweetness" and love provided by Grace, which allows a person to do good out of love rather than fear.</summary>
<keywords>justification, resurrection, baptism, grace vs law, Holy Spirit, love of righteousness, fear of punishment</keywords>

--- Page 179 ---
<meta>This page continues Augustine’s argument that the Law serves to expose sin, driving the soul to seek help. Having established that justification comes through faith, he now addresses the potential objection that grace might replace or destroy human will, arguing instead that grace is what actually makes the will truly "free."</meta>

<header>ON THE SPIRIT AND THE LETTER, BOOK [I].</header>

# THAT JUST AS THE LAW IS NOT VOIDED BY FAITH,
## so free will is not voided by grace, 
## but is established. Chapter 30.

Do we then make void <term>free will: the human capacity to choose between good and evil</term> <note>original: *liberum arbitrium*</note> through grace? Far from it! Rather, we establish free will. For just as the law is not voided by faith, so free will is not voided by grace, but established. For the law is not fulfilled except by free will; but through the law comes the knowledge of sin; through faith comes the obtaining of grace against sin; through grace comes the healing of the soul from the <term>vice: a moral flaw or spiritual sickness</term> <note>original: *vitio*</note> of sin; through the health of the soul comes the freedom of the will; through free will comes the love of righteousness; and through the love of righteousness comes the performance of the law. And because of this, just as the law is not voided but established through faith (because faith obtains the grace by which the law may be fulfilled), so free will is not voided through grace but established: because grace heals the will, by which righteousness is freely loved. All these things, which I have linked together like a chain, have their own voices in the Holy Scriptures.

<margin>Exodus 20</margin>
The Law says: "You shall not covet." <note>The Latin *non concupisces* refers to the internal desire for what is forbidden.</note>

<margin>Psalm 40</margin>
Faith says: "Heal my soul, for I have sinned against you."

<margin>John 5</margin>
Grace says: "Behold, you are made whole; sin no more, lest something worse happen to you."

<margin>Psalm 29</margin>
Health says: "O Lord my God, I cried to you, and you healed me."

<margin>Psalm 53</margin>
Free will says: "I will freely sacrifice to you."

<margin>Psalm 118</margin>
Love of righteousness says: "The <term>righteous: those who follow God's law</term> <note>original: *iusti*</note> have told me of delights, but they are not like your law, O Lord."

Why then do wretched men dare to boast of free will before they are set free, or of their own strength if they have already been liberated? They do not notice that in the very name "free will," liberty is sounded. 

<margin>2 Corinthians 3</margin>
"But where the Spirit of the Lord is, there is liberty."

<summary>Augustine argues that grace does not destroy the human will but heals it, creating a logical chain from the Law's demands to the eventual free love of righteousness. He concludes that true freedom of the will is only possible through the presence of the Holy Spirit.</summary>

<keywords>free will, grace, law, faith, healing of the soul, righteousness, St. Augustine, justification</keywords>

Augustine is quoting Jesus to argue that true freedom is a gift of grace, not an inherent human independence from God.</note>

# WHETHER FAITH IS ESTABLISHED IN OUR 
## own power: and what it means to be in someone’s power: and how faith is twofold. Chapter 31.

Someone will ask: whether faith itself—in which the beginning of salvation, or of this connection toward salvation which I have mentioned, seems to exist—is established in our own <term>power: the capacity or right to act</term> <note>original: *potestate*</note>? We shall see this more easily if we first examine somewhat more carefully what "power" actually is. For since there are these two things, "to will" and "to be able": it follows that neither does he who wills immediately have the power; nor does he who has the power immediately will. For just as we sometimes want what we cannot do, so also we are sometimes able to do what we do not want. 

Even in the words themselves, the meaning resonates: for the word "will" <note>original: *uoluntas*</note> is derived from the verb "to will" <note>original: *uelle*</note>, while the word "power" <note>original: *potestas*</note> took its name from the verb "to be able" <note>original: *posse*</note>. Therefore, just as the one who wills has "will," so the one who is able has "power." But for something to be done by power, the will must be present. For no one is usually said to have "exercised power" if he did something unwillingly; although, if we look more closely, even what someone is forced to do unwillingly—if he does it, he does it by <insert>his</insert> will... <note>The text breaks off mid-sentence at the bottom of the page; Augustine is moving toward the argument that even coerced actions involve a choice of the will.</note>

<vocab>Augustine, faith, power, will, free will, grace, sin</vocab>

<summary>Augustine begins Chapter 31 by defining the relationship between the human will and the power to act, arguing that "power" requires the presence of "will." He uses this linguistic and philosophical framework to prepare for an analysis of whether the act of faith is within human control or is a gift of grace.</summary>
<keywords>Augustine, faith, potestas, voluntas, free will, grace, slavery to sin, salvation, terminology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Augustine’s analytical breakdown of human agency. # ON THE SPIRIT AND THE LETTER, BOOK I

...does it. But because he would prefer something else, he is said to do it unwillingly—that is, not wanting to do it. Indeed, he is compelled by some evil to do what he does while wanting to avoid it or remove it from himself. For if his <term>will: the faculty of choice</term> <note>original: *uoluntas*</note> were so great that he would prefer not doing this over not suffering that [the threatened evil], he would without a doubt resist the one compelling him and not do it. 

And because of this, if he does it, he does so not indeed with a full and free will, but nevertheless he does not do it except by will. Since the result follows this will, we cannot say that <term>power: the capacity to act</term> <note>original: *potestatem*</note> was lacking in the one doing it. For if, yielding to the one compelling him, he wished to do it but could not, we would say that the will was present (though extorted), but the power was absent. When, however, he did not do it precisely because he did not want to, the power was certainly there, but the will was lacking as long as he did not do it while resisting the one compelling him. 

This is why even those who compel or persuade usually say: "Why do you not do what you have the power to do, so that you may be free from this evil?" And those who are entirely unable to do what they are being compelled to do (because they are believed to be able), usually respond with an excuse and say: "I would do it if it were in my power." 

What more then are we looking for? Since we define this as "power": where the <term>capability: the means or opportunity to act</term> <note>original: *facultas*</note> to act is joined to the will. Hence, someone is said to have a thing in their power if they do it if they want to, and do not do it if they do not want to. 

Now consider that point which we set out to examine: whether <term>faith: the act of believing</term> <note>original: *fides*</note> is in our power? For we are speaking now of that faith which we exercise when we believe something, not that which we give when we promise something; for that too is called "faith." But we use the word differently: in one sense we say, "he did not have faith in me" <note>meaning: he did not believe what I said</note>, and in another sense, "he did not keep faith with me" <note>meaning: he broke a promise or was unfaithful</note>. For that former...

<summary>Augustine defines "power" as the intersection of wanting to do something and having the physical or situational ability to do it. He uses this definition to begin investigating whether the act of religious faith is something humans can generate through their own power.</summary>

Since this is absurd—for what is <term>believing</term> <note>original: *credere*</note> except to consent that what is said is true? <margin>Romans 13; 1 Corinthians 4</margin> 
But as the Apostle says: "There is no power except from God." What then is the reason? Why should it not also be said to us concerning this: "For what do you have that you have not received?" For God gave even the means that we might believe. However, we never read in the holy Scriptures: "there is no will except from God." And it is rightly not written, because it is not true; otherwise, God—which is unthinkable—would be the author of sins, if there were no will except from Him. For a bad <term>will</term> <note>original: *uoluntas*</note> is already sin in itself, even if the result is lacking—that is, if it lacks the power to act. Furthermore, when a bad will receives the power 
<margin>Romans 9</margin> 
to fulfill what is intended, it comes from the judgment of God, with whom there is no injustice. He punishes even in this way, and not unjustly for that reason, because He does so secretly. Otherwise, the unjust man does not know he is being punished, except when he feels it through manifest punishment against his will, which...

<summary>Augustine argues that faith is an act of the will and thus within human power, yet acknowledges that all power ultimately originates from God. He carefully distinguishes this from the origin of the "will" itself to ensure God is not blamed for the existence of sinful intentions.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Chapter 32 of Augustine’s "On the Spirit and the Letter." In the preceding section, Augustine began distinguishing between "believing a person" (which can be gullibility) and "believing God." He is now refining the definition of the faith that leads to justification, moving from a faith based on fear of the law to a faith based on the love of righteousness.</meta>

<header>OF ST. AURELIUS AUGUSTINE</header>
<meta>catchword: His name</meta>

...[he] does not indeed believe everyone, but believes God: because it is not said that he believes all things <note>original: *non omnia quidem credit*. This completes the thought that a wise person is not gullible to every human word, but places their trust specifically in God’s word.</note>. Therefore, no one doubts that this is the faith commended by the Apostle—the faith by which one believes God. But there is still a distinction to be made here: for even those who are under the law, and who attempt to achieve their own righteousness through the fear of punishment, believe God. Yet they do not achieve the righteousness of God for this very reason: that it is love <note>original: *charitas* — often translated as "charity," but here meaning the selfless, divinely-inspired love for what is good.</note> that performs it. Love takes pleasure only in what is permitted. Fear, by contrast, is compelled to carry out in its actions what is permitted, while its will holds onto something else; it would prefer, if it were possible, that what is forbidden were actually permitted. Thus, even these people believe God. For if they did not believe Him at all, they certainly would not dread the punishment of the law. But the Apostle does not commend this kind of faith, for he says:

<margin>Romans 8</margin>
> *For you did not receive the spirit of slavery again in fear, but you received the spirit of adoption as sons, in which we cry out: Abba, Father.*

That fear, then, is servile <note>Like that of a slave (servus) rather than a child.</note>. And so, although the Lord is believed in that state, righteousness is not loved; rather, damnation is feared. But the sons cry out, "Abba, Father." Of these two words, one comes from the circumcision and the other from the uncircumcision <note>original: *una est ex circumcisione, altera ex præputio*. Augustine is pointing out that "Abba" is the Hebrew/Aramaic word for father, and "Pater" is the Latin/Greek word. This signifies the union of Jews and Gentiles in the early Church.</note>: the Jews first and then the Greeks: since there is one God who justifies the circumcision by faith, and the uncircumcision through faith. Moreover, when they cry out, they are asking for something.

And what do they ask for, except that for which they hunger and thirst? And this is surely nothing other than what was said of them:

<margin>Matthew 5</margin>
> *Blessed are those who hunger and thirst for righteousness, for they shall be satisfied.*

Therefore, let those who are under the law cross over to this state, so that they may become sons instead of slaves. Yet they should not do this in such a way as to cease being servants, but rather so that as sons, they may serve their Lord and Father freely <note>original: *liberaliter* — meaning both "generously" and "as a free person" (liber).</note>, for they have received this power as well. For that Only-begotten one gave the power to become children of God to those believing in [His] name—

<margin>John 1</margin>

This is the righteousness of God which He not only teaches through the commandment of the law, but also grants through the gift of the Spirit.

### WHETHER THE WILL BY WHICH

But it follows that we should inquire a little further, whether that **will** by which we believe is itself also a gift of God, or whether, being naturally implanted within us, it is applied by **free choice** <note>original: *libero arbitrio*. This refers to the human capacity to make decisions and choose between options.</note>. For if...

<summary>Augustine argues that true faith operates through love (charity) which is a gift of the Holy Spirit, rather than through a fear of punishment. He defines God's righteousness and charity as qualities God imparts to humans to make them righteous and loving.</summary>

--- Page 187 ---
<meta>This page continues the transition from Chapter 32 into Chapter 33 of Augustine’s "On the Spirit and the Letter." Following the discussion on how charity is poured into the heart, Augustine now addresses a potential theological trap: if the "will to believe" is a gift from God, does that make God responsible for those who do not believe? If it is not a gift, does man have something to boast about independent of God?</meta>

### ON THE SPIRIT AND THE LETTER, BOOK I
->F 2<-

...[if we should have] said that this [will] is not a gift of God, we must fear lest we think we have found something of our own to answer the Apostle [Paul] when he rebukes us, saying:
<margin>1 Corinthians 4</margin>
*For what do you have that you have not received? But if you have received it, why do you boast as if you had not received it?* <note>original: "Quid enim habes quod non accepisti..."</note> We might then be able to answer: "Behold, we have a **will to believe** which we did not receive; behold the point where we may boast, for we did not receive it." But if we say that even a will of this kind is nothing but a gift of God, we must again fear lest the unfaithful and the ungodly seem to excuse themselves—and perhaps with some justice—by claiming they did not believe simply because God was unwilling to give them that will.

<margin>Philippians 2</margin>
For that which was said: *For it is God who works in us, both to will and to work according to his good pleasure,* <note>original: "Deus est enim qui operatur in nobis..."</note> is already a matter of **grace** <note>original: *gratiae*. In this context, grace is the unmerited assistance given by God to humans for their regeneration or sanctification.</note>, which faith obtains so that there may be good works of men; those works which
<margin>Galatians 5, Romans 5</margin>
faith performs through **love** <note>original: *dilectionem*. A specific term for "cherishing" or "highly esteeming" love.</note>, which is poured out in our hearts through the Holy Spirit who has been given to us. If we believe in order to obtain this grace, we certainly believe by an act of the will.

The question arises: from where does this will come to us? If it is from nature, why is it not in everyone, since the same God is the creator of all?
<margin>1 Timothy 2</margin>
If it is by the gift of God, why again is this not given to everyone, since God *wills all men to be saved and to come to the knowledge of the truth?*

Therefore, let us first say this, and see whether it satisfies the question: that **free will** <term>liberum arbitrium: the capacity of the soul to choose between different paths</term>, naturally assigned by the Creator to the rational soul, is that neutral power which can either be directed toward faith or inclined toward unbelief. And therefore, even regarding this will by which a person believes in God, it cannot be said that the man has something he did not receive. For, when God calls, the will arises from the free choice which he received naturally when he was created. But God "wills all men to be saved and to come to the knowledge of the truth"...

<summary>Augustine explores whether the "will to believe" is a natural human capacity or a specific gift of grace, navigating the tension between human responsibility and divine providence. He proposes that free will is a neutral power given by God at creation that can respond when God calls.</summary>
<keywords>free will, grace, faith, 1 Corinthians 4, Philippians 2, 1 Timothy 2, salvation, divine calling</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion from the previous section where Augustine argues that while God works in the human heart to produce the "will to believe," this divine action does not bypass human agency. He is responding to the objection that if God provides the will, humans are not responsible for their unbelief. Here, he reconciles divine sovereignty with human responsibility by explaining that God's will is never defeated, even by those who reject Him.</meta>

# OF ST. AURELIUS AUGUSTINE

...yet not in such a way as to take away from them their **free will** <note>original: *liberum arbitrium*. This refers to the human capacity to choose, which Augustine argues remains intact even under the influence of divine grace.</note>, by which, using it either well or poorly, they may be judged most justly. When this happens, the unfaithful indeed act against the will of God when they do not believe his Gospel; yet they do not thereby "overcome" His will. Rather, they defraud themselves of the great and highest good and entangle themselves in the evils of punishment. They are destined to experience, through their suffering, the power of Him whose mercy they despised when it was offered in His gifts. 

Thus, the will of God is always invincible. It would only be "overcome" if He could not find a use for those who despise Him, or if they could in any way escape what He has decreed for such people. 

For example, suppose a master says: "I will that all these servants of mine work in the vineyard, and after their labor, they shall rest and feast; however, if any of them is unwilling to do this, he shall forever grind at the **mill**." <note>original: *pistrino*. In the Roman world, being sent to the "pistrinum" or flour mill was a standard, grueling punishment for enslaved people. Augustine uses this as a metaphor for the inevitability of divine justice.</note> It seems, indeed, that whoever despises the command acts against the will of his lord; but he would only "overcome" that will if he could despise the mill and escape it as well. This is something that can in no way happen under the power of God. 

Whence it is written: 
<margin>Psalm 61</margin> 
*God has spoken once* <note>original: "Semel locutus est deus." This is Psalm 62:11 in modern Bibles (61 in the Vulgate). Augustine interprets "once" as meaning "unchangeably."</note>—that is, unchangeably—though this may also be understood as referring to His **only Word** <note>referring to Christ as the Logos or "Word" of God</note>. Then, adding what it is that God has spoken unchangeably, the Psalmist says: *These two things have I heard: that power belongs to God, and to you, O Lord, belongs mercy, for you render to everyone according to their works.* 

Therefore, that person will be held guilty unto damnation under God's **power** <note>original: *potestate*</note> who has despised the opportunity to believe in His **mercy** <note>original: *misericordiam*</note>. 

But whoever has believed in Him—whoever has not despised the One who offers to absolve them from all sins, heal them of all vices, and kindle and enlighten them with His heat and light—that person shall possess good works through His **grace** <note>original: *gratia*. Grace is the central theme of this book: the unearned help God gives to humans to enable them to do good.</note>. Through these works, they may also be redeemed, even in their physical body, from the corruption of death; they shall be crowned and satisfied with good things.

<summary>Augustine explains that human free will remains even under God’s sovereignty; while people can reject God’s mercy, they cannot escape His power, as justice ensures that even disobedience serves His ultimate purpose.</summary>
<keywords>free will, justice, mercy, grace, sovereignty of God, Psalm 61, punishment, redemption</keywords>

### HE ANSWERS THE REBUTTAL
->against the points made above, and also concerning another small question:<-
->why one person yields to divine persuasion,<-
->and another does not. Chapter 34.<-

If this discussion is sufficient to solve that question, let it be enough. But if it is answered that we must take care lest anyone think that sin—which is committed through **free will** <note>original: "liberũ arbitriũ"</note>—should be attributed to God, then consider the following. In that which is said: 
<margin>1 Corinthians 4</margin> *What do you have that you did not receive?*, the very will by which we believe is also attributed to the gift of God, because it arises from the free will which we received when we were created. One should attend and see that this will is not to be attributed to the divine gift only because it comes from free will (which was naturally created within us), but also because God acts through the persuasion of things seen <note>Augustine refers to "visions" or "perceptions" (*visorum*)—the external or internal stimuli that spark a thought.</note> so that we may will and that we may believe. This happens either externally through Gospel exhortations—where even the commands of the law do some good if they remind a person of their own weakness, so that they may take refuge in justifying grace by believing—or internally, where no one has it in their own power to control what enters their mind, although to consent or to dissent is a matter of one's own will. Therefore, in these ways, when God acts upon a rational soul so that it may believe—for indeed, the will cannot believe anything if there is no persuasion or calling to which it may believe—surely it is God who works in a person even the very *willing to believe*. In all things, His mercy precedes us. However, to consent to God’s calling, or to dissent from it, as I have said...

Let us therefore at last conclude the book. I do not know if we have accomplished anything by its great length—not with you, whose faith I already know, but with the minds of those for whose sake you wished me to write. These are people who prefer to defend their own opinion, I will not say against our own, but (to speak more mildly) I will not even say against His who spoke through His apostles; rather, they certainly defend it against such a great apostle as Paul. They do this not against a single sentence of his, but against such a vehement, intense, and vigilant struggle on his part. ...surely to you, as it has been said, it can happen that a man is without sin if his <term>will</term> <note>original: "uolūtas"; referring to the human faculty of choice which Augustine argues must be empowered by God.</note> is not lacking, with the helping of divine aid. And so God's grace not only shows what must be done, but also helps so that what has been shown can be done. However, to you it seemed absurd for a thing that is possible to be without an example <note>Augustine is addressing Marcellinus's objection that if something is possible, there should be a real-world example of it.</note>. Having established that sinlessness is theoretically possible through God's grace, Augustine now addresses the objection that such a state has no historical example. ### SAINT AURELIUS AUGUSTINE

...to seek, whether for one reading or thinking, for we cannot deny those things are possible for God, even if an example of them is lacking. But because it can be said that those [miraculous] works are divine, whereas living righteously belongs to our own works, I have undertaken to show that this too is a divine work. I have done this in this book, perhaps more wordily than is necessary, but against the enemies of the <term>grace of God</term> <note>original: "gratiæ dei"; Augustine is likely targeting the Pelagians, a group who argued that humans could achieve goodness primarily through their own effort rather than divine help.</note> I still seem to myself to have said too little. Nothing delights me so much to discuss at length as when His Scripture supports me most fully, <margin>2 Corinthians 10</margin> and the aim is that *he who glories may glory in the Lord*, and that in all things we may give thanks to the Lord our God, having our <term>hearts lifted up</term> <note>original: "sursum corda"; a famous phrase from the ancient Christian liturgy, still used today, inviting the faithful to focus on God.</note> to where, <margin>James 1</margin> from the "Father of lights," every best gift and every perfect gift comes. 

For if a thing is not a work of God simply because it is performed through us—or because we act only while He gives the gift—then neither would that be a work of God when a mountain is moved into the sea. Yet the Lord said this could be done through the faith of men, and he attributed this to their own action, saying: 
<margin>Mark 11</margin>
> If you have faith in you like a grain of mustard seed, you will say to this mountain, "Lift yourself up and cast yourself into the sea," and it will be done, and nothing will be impossible for you.

Certainly, He said this "to you," not to Himself or to the Father; and yet, a human being does this in no way except by God giving the power and working through him. See, then, how <term>perfect righteousness</term> <note>original: "perfecta iustitia"; the state of being entirely without sin, which Augustine argues is possible in theory but rare in practice.</note> is without an example among men, and yet it is not impossible. It would, in fact, happen if a <term>will</term> <note>original: "uoluntas"; the human faculty of choice, which Augustine believes must be "healed" by grace to function correctly.</note> were applied that was great enough for so great a task. And the will would be that great if nothing pertaining to righteousness were hidden from us, and if those things so delighted the soul that whatever other pleasure or pain might hinder us, that delight would overcome it. That this is not the case pertains not to impossibility, but to the <term>judgment of God</term> <note>original: "iudicium dei"; Augustine suggests that our current lack of spiritual "delight" is a result of the fallen human condition.</note>. For who does not know that it is not in a man’s power to know what he should know, nor does it follow that once something has been recognized as desirable...

<summary>Augustine argues that human righteousness is a gift of God's grace rather than a solo human achievement, comparing it to the miraculous power of faith that moves mountains. He explains that while perfect sinlessness is possible for God to grant, its absence in reality is due to the limitations of human will and divine judgment.</summary>

### XXXVI. THAT THE COMMANDMENT TO LOVE
### GOD WILL BE PERFECTLY FULFILLED NOT HERE, BUT IN OUR HEAVENLY HOMELAND.

But perhaps someone might think that we lack nothing for the knowledge of <term>righteousness</term> <note>original: "iustitiæ"; in Augustine's Latin, this refers to a state of being "right" or "just" before God, which involves both following laws and having a transformed heart.</note>, since the Lord, "summing up and shortening His word over the earth," said that the whole law and the prophets hang on two commandments. Nor did He keep these secret, but brought them forth in the clearest words: <margin>Matthew 22</margin> "You shall love," He said, "the Lord your God with all your heart, and with all your soul, and with all your mind; and you shall love your neighbor as yourself." What is truer than that when these are fulfilled, righteousness is completely fulfilled? Yet let him who notices this also notice how in many things we all stumble, while we think we are pleasing (or at least not displeasing) the God whom we love by what we do. And later, when warned by His Scripture or by certain and clear reasoning, when we have learned what does not please Him, we pray for forgiveness by repenting. 

Human life is full of such examples. But why do we know less of what pleases Him, except because He Himself is less known to us? <margin>1 Corinthians 13</margin> For now we see through a mirror dimly, but then we shall see face to face. <note>original: "per speculum in ænigmate"; famously translated in the King James Bible as "through a glass, darkly." Augustine uses this to explain that our current spiritual understanding is indirect and fuzzy compared to the direct vision of God in the afterlife.</note> For who would dare to think that when we have reached the point He described—"that I may know even as I am known"—the love of God in those who behold Him will be only as great as the love the faithful have now? Or that our current love is even comparable to that future love, as if they were nearly the same? Furthermore, if the greater our knowledge is, the greater our love will be, then surely we must believe that however much is lacking in our love now, just as much is lacking for the perfection of righteousness. For a thing can be known or believed and yet not be loved; but that which is neither known nor believed cannot be loved...

<summary>Augustine argues that while the commandment to love God is simple to state, it cannot be perfectly performed in this life because our knowledge of God is currently imperfect and "dim."</summary>

ST. AURELIUS AUGUSTINE

nor can it be believed, it is not possible. # ON THE SPIRIT AND THE LETTER, BOOK [I]

[what] is owed to full and perfect <term>righteousness</term> <note>original: "iustitiae"; Augustine refers to a state of being right with God, which he argues is a gift of grace rather than human effort.</note>, is now to be reckoned as a fault. For it is one thing not yet to have attained total <term>charity</term> <note>original: "charitatem"; the highest form of Christian love.</note>; it is another thing to follow no <term>lust</term> <note>original: "cupiditatem"; often translated as "cupidity" or worldly desire.</note>. Wherefore a person, although he loves God far less than when he can love Him in the <term>vision</term> <note>original: "conspectu"; referring to the "Beatific Vision," the direct sight of God in the afterlife.</note>, ought nevertheless to desire nothing unlawful; just as even in those things which are adjacent to the bodily senses, the eye can take no delight in darkness, even though it cannot be fixed upon the brightest light. But see, let us now imagine such a human soul in this corruptible body: even if it has not yet absorbed and consumed all movements of earthly lust by that most super-eminent perfection of the love of God, nevertheless, in this "lesser righteousness," it consents by no inclination to that same lust for the purpose of doing something unlawful, so that it might already belong to that immortal life.

<margin>Deuteronomy 6</margin>
> You shall love the Lord your God with all your heart, and with all your soul, and with all your strength.

But to this life:
<margin>Romans 6</margin>
> Let not sin reign in your mortal body, that you should obey its desires,

and to the former:
> You shall not covet;

And to this one:
<margin>Ecclesiasticus 18</margin>
> Do not follow your lusts. <note>Ecclesiasticus is also known as the Wisdom of Sirach.</note>

To that state, one should seek nothing more than to persist in that perfection. To this state, one should hold to what is done in work, and hope for the perfection of the other as a reward; so that through that state, the righteous person may live without end in the vision which they desired in this one; while through this state, the righteous person lives by faith, in which they desire that vision for a certain end. Once these things are established, it will be a sin for one living by faith to sometimes consent to some unlawful delight—not only in perpetrating those horrific crimes and outrages, but also in these lighter matters: such as lending an ear to some voice that should not be heard, or a tongue to something that should not be said, or so thinking something in the heart itself, that he would prefer it were lawful <insert>to do something that is not.</insert>

<summary>Augustine distinguishes between the "perfect righteousness" of the afterlife, where one sees God directly, and the "lesser righteousness" of this life, which is lived by faith and requires constant resistance against worldly desires.</summary>

<keywords>St. Augustine, righteousness, charity, lust, faith vs vision, Deuteronomy, Romans, Ecclesiasticus, sin, mortal body</keywords>

--- Page 198 ---
<meta>This page continues Augustine’s argument from the previous section, where he distinguished between the "perfect righteousness" of the afterlife and the "lesser righteousness" achievable by faith in our current mortal state.</meta>

<header>OF BISHOP AURELIUS AUGUSTINE</header>

nor can it be believed [otherwise]. But if we must believe that the saints were able to arrive at such great <term>love</term> <note>original: "dilectionem"; Augustine often uses "dilectio" and "caritas" interchangeably to describe a divinely inspired love.</note>—than which certainly the Lord himself testified there can be no greater in this life—namely, that one should lay down his life for the faith and for his brothers; when we come from this <term>pilgrimage</term> <note>original: "peregrinatione"; a common metaphor for the earthly life of a Christian traveling toward their true home in heaven.</note>, in which we now walk by faith and not by sight, to that sight which we hope for but do not yet see, and wait for with patience, undoubtedly that same love will be not only above what we have here, but far above what we ask or understand. Yet it cannot for that reason be more than "from the whole heart, from the whole soul, from the whole mind." For nothing remains in us that could be added to the "whole," because if anything remains outside it, then it will not be the whole. 

Therefore, this will be the first commandment of <term>righteousness</term> <note>original: "iustitiæ"; refers to the state of being in a right relationship with God.</note>, by which we are commanded to love God with the whole heart, the whole soul, and the whole mind. To this is added the second concerning loving one's neighbor, which we shall fulfill in that life when we see face to face. 

<margin>1 Corinthians 13</margin>
But this is commanded of us even now, so that we might be reminded what to ask for in faith, where to send our hope ahead, and that—forgetting those things which are behind—we ought to stretch ourselves toward those things which are before. 

<margin>Philippians 3</margin>
And through this, as it seems to me, in that righteousness which is to be perfected, he has made much progress in this life who, by progressing, has learned how far he is from the perfection of righteousness. 

<margin>Habakkuk 2</margin>
<margin>Romans 1</margin>
<margin>2 Corinthians 5</margin>
But it can be said that there is a certain "lesser righteousness" suitable for this life, by which the righteous man lives by faith, although he is a pilgrim away from the Lord; and therefore, walking by faith and not yet by <term>hope</term> <note>original: "spem"; while usually contrasted with "sight" (specie), Augustine here emphasizes the current state of waiting.</note>, it is not absurdly said that it also belongs to this righteousness not to sin. For even if there cannot yet be such a great love of God as [is due to] that knowledge...

<summary>Augustine explains that while perfect love (loving God with the "whole" heart) is only fully realized in the afterlife when we see God face to face, the commandment serves as a goal for our current earthly progress. He introduces the concept of a "lesser righteousness" for this life, where one lives by faith while still a pilgrim.</summary>
<keywords>righteousness, charity, love of God, pilgrimage, faith vs sight, commandments, spiritual progress</keywords>

--- Page 199 ---
<meta>This page continues the sentence from the previous page, completing the word "perfectioni" (perfection) and proceeding to discuss the distinction between the perfect righteousness of heaven and the "lesser" righteousness of faith on earth.</meta>

<header># BOOK ON THE SPIRIT AND THE LETTER</header>

[perfecti]on which is full and perfect, it must now be accounted as a fault. For it is one thing not yet to have attained full **love** <note>original: "charitatem"; refers to the highest form of Christian love (caritas), distinct from mere affection.</note>, and another thing to follow no **lust** <note>original: "cupiditate"; often translated as "cupidity" or "desire," referring here to selfish or forbidden longing.</note>. For this reason, although a person loves God far less than they can when they love Him in His direct presence, they must nevertheless desire nothing forbidden; just as even in those things adjacent to the bodily senses, the eye can take no delight in darkness, even though it cannot fix its gaze on the brightest light. But look, let us now imagine such a human soul in this corruptible body, which—although it has not yet swallowed up and consumed all movements of earthly **desire** <note>original: "libidinis"; here meaning the impulse toward sin or worldly pleasure.</note> with that most outstanding perfection of the love of God—nevertheless, in this "lesser righteousness," it consents with no inclination to that same desire to perform anything forbidden, so that it may already belong to that immortal life.

<margin>Deuteronomy 6</margin>
*You shall love the Lord your God with all your heart, and with all your soul, and with all your strength.* But to this other state: <margin>Romans 6</margin> *Let not sin reign in your mortal body, that you should obey its desires;* to the former: *You shall not covet*; to the latter: <margin>Ecclesiasticus 18</margin> *Do not go after your lusts*; to the former, to seek nothing more than to persist in 그 perfection; to the latter, to have in hand that which one performs in work, and to hope for the perfection of the former as a reward—so that through the former, the **righteous** <note>original: "iustus"; the person justified or made "right" before God.</note> person may live without end in that sight which they desired in this life; while through the latter, the righteous person lives by **faith** <note>original: "fides"; the trust in God's promises before they are seen.</note>, in which they desire that sight with a certain goal. These things being established, it will be a **sin** <note>original: "peccatum"</note> for one living by faith sometimes to consent to some forbidden delight—not only in perpetrating those horrific crimes and outrages, but even in these lighter matters: such as lending an ear to some voice that should not be heard, or a tongue to something that should not be said, or thinking something in the heart in such a way that they would prefer it were lawful [to do it]...

<summary>Augustine distinguishes between the perfect love of God experienced in heaven and the struggle against "lust" or earthly desire required of believers on earth. He argues that while we cannot reach full perfection in this life, "lesser righteousness" consists in refusing to consent to sinful inclinations.</summary>
<keywords>divine love, lust, righteousness, faith, sin, Deuteronomy, Romans, Ecclesiasticus, Augustine, morality</keywords>

--- Page 200 ---
<meta>This page continues from the previous page's discussion on the limits of human perfection in this life, completing the thought that even a "lesser righteousness" involves a struggle against forbidden desires.</meta>

<header># OF SAINT AURELIUS AUGUSTINE</header>

[perfection] <note>original: "perfectioni"; completing the word from the previous page.</note> of that which evilly delights, and which is known through the commandment to be unlawful. Indeed, this very consent is a step toward sin—one that would certainly be taken if the fear of punishment did not prevent it. Such righteous people, though they live by faith, would have no need to say:
<margin>Matthew 6</margin>
**"Forgive us our debts, as we also forgive our debtors"**; and they would prove that which is written to be false:
<margin>Psalm 142</margin>
**"No one living shall be justified in your sight."** <note>original: "Non iustificabitur in conspectu tuo omnis vivens." Augustine uses this to show that even the holiest people are not perfectly "just" or "righteous" compared to God's standard.</note> And that saying:
<margin>1 John 1</margin>
**"If we say that we have no sin, we deceive ourselves and the truth is not in us."** And that:
<margin>3 Kings 8</margin>
<note>In modern Bibles, this reference is found in 1 Kings 8:46.</note>
**"Because there is no man who will not sin."** And that:
<margin>Ecclesiastes 7</margin>
**"There is no righteous person on earth who will do good and not sin."** For both of these testimonies speak not of the past (that is, "has sinned") but of the future (that is, "will sin"), as do whatever other passages holy scripture records in this regard.

But since these words cannot be false, I see it follows that whatever kind or however great the **righteousness** <term>iustitiam: the state of being right or "just" before God, often through a combination of faith and action</term> we might be able to define in this life, there is no human being in it who has absolutely no sin.
<margin>Luke 6</margin>
It is necessary for every person to give so that it may be given to them: to forgive so that they may be forgiven. And if a person possesses any righteousness, they should not presume it comes from their own strength, but from the **grace** <note>original: "gratia"; the unearned favor and help of God.</note> of God who **justifies** <term>iustificantis: the process by which God makes or declares a sinner to be righteous</term>. Even then, they must still hunger and thirst for righteousness from Him who is the living bread and the fountain of life. He works this justification in His saints—who labor amid the temptations of this life—in such a way that He still has something to generously provide to those who ask, and something to mercifully forgive those who confess.

But let these people <note>Augustine is likely referring to the Pelagians, who argued that humans could achieve a state of sinless perfection through their own will.</note> find, if they can, anyone living under the burden of this corruptible nature whom God no longer has cause to forgive. Yet even such a person must confess that it was not by the teaching of the Law, but by the infusion of the Spirit of grace, that they were helped to reach a state where they might avoid not just some criminal sin, but the failings inherent to this mortal age itself. Truly, although if those divine testimonies...

<summary>Augustine argues that total sinlessness is impossible in this life, citing various scriptures to show that even the righteous must constantly seek God's forgiveness and grace. He refutes the idea that human effort alone, or mere knowledge of the Law, can make a person perfect.</summary>
<keywords>righteousness, grace, justification, original sin, forgiveness, Lord's Prayer, Pelagianism, Saint Augustine</keywords>

--- Page 201 ---
<meta>This page concludes Augustine’s treatise "On the Spirit and the Letter." It follows the previous page's argument that while perfect sinlessness is theoretically possible for God to grant, it is not the standard human experience in this life, serving instead to foster humility.</meta>

<header># ON THE SPIRIT AND THE LETTER, BOOK I</header>

[sin] <note>original: "peccatum"; completing the word "sin" from the previous page's "s..."</note> who might receive the prize appropriately; they simply cannot find such a person. Nevertheless, in no way must it be said that God lacks the **possibility** <term>possibilitas: the divine power or capability to act</term> by which the human will is so aided that not only is this **righteousness** <term>iustitia: the state of being right before God</term> which comes from faith perfected in a person in every part, but also that righteousness according to which one must afterwards live for eternity in the very contemplation of God.

For since if He now wished even this corruptible body to put on
<margin>1 Corinthians 15</margin>
**incorruption**, and if He commanded a man to live here among those destined to die in such a way that he would not die at all—so that, with all old nature entirely consumed, no **law in his members** would resist the **law of his mind**,
<margin>Romans 7</margin>
and he would know God as present everywhere just as the saints are later to know Him—who would be so mad as to dare to assert that God cannot do this? But why He does not do so, men ask; yet those who ask do not consider that they are themselves only men. I know that just as there is no impossibility, so also there is no **injustice** with God.
<margin>Romans 9</margin>
And I know that **He resists the proud, but gives grace to the humble.**
<margin>James 4</margin>
And I know that to him <note>Referring to the Apostle Paul.</note>, so that he might not be exalted, there was given a **thorn in the flesh**, a messenger of Satan to buffet him; and to him,
<margin>2 Corinthians 12</margin>
praying once, twice, and a third time, it was said: **"My grace is sufficient for you; for power is made perfect in weakness."** There is, therefore, something in the hidden and profound depths of the judgments of God, so that even the mouths of the righteous may be silenced in their own praise, and not opened except in the praise of God. But who can search out this "something," who can track it, who can know it? So **unsearchable are His judgments, and His ways past finding out!**
<margin>Romans 11</margin>
**For who has known the mind of the Lord, or who has been His counselor? Or who has first given to Him, and it shall be repaid to him? Because from Him, and through Him, and in Him are all things:**

> **To Him be glory forever and ever. Amen.**

->T H E  E N D.<-

<summary>Augustine concludes by arguing that God possesses the power to make humans perfect and immortal now, but in His unsearchable wisdom, He allows human weakness to persist to ensure that all glory goes to Him rather than human pride.</summary>
<keywords>divine possibility, righteousness of faith, humility, thorn in the flesh, unsearchable judgments, grace, Saint Augustine</keywords>
